<meta>This page begins the formal dedication of the work to Queen Christina of Sweden. It follows the title page of the 1651 first edition of Leonardo da Vinci's "Treatise on Painting," edited by Raphaël du Fresne.</meta>

<sig>ã iij</sig>

# TO THE MOST SERENE AND MOST POWERFUL
# PRINCESS CHRISTINA,
## BY THE GRACE OF GOD
## QUEEN
### OF THE SWEDES, GOTHS, AND VANDALS,
### GREAT DUCHESS OF FINLAND,
### DUCHESS OF ESTONIA AND KARELIA,
### LADY OF INGRIA, etc.

---

->MOST SERENE QUEEN,<-

It is evident through the long record of the most illustrious histories that the art of painting has always been held in high esteem; and everyone knows that Alexander <note>Alexander the Great (356–323 BCE).</note>, who for greatness of spirit and of deeds was the Gustavus <note>A reference to Gustavus Adolphus (1594–1632), the late King of Sweden and father of Queen Christina. The author is flatteringly comparing the ancient conqueror to the Queen’s father.</note> of his century, held the great Apelles <note>The most renowned painter of ancient Greece, who enjoyed the exclusive patronage of Alexander the Great.</note> in honor. I shall say nothing of Fabius <note>Quintus Fabius Pictor (c. 300 BCE), a member of a high-ranking Roman family who broke social taboos by practicing painting, earning the surname "Pictor" (The Painter).</note>, who in that city <note>Rome.</note> where kings felt honored by the title of citizen, was called "The Painter" for the practice of such a noble art, and left that name to his family. Nor, to encourage Your Majesty to value this virtue <note>Here, "virtue" refers to artistic skill or excellence.</note>, do I believe it necessary to remind you that the Emperor Antoninus <note>Likely Marcus Aurelius Antoninus (reigned 161–180 CE), known as the philosopher-emperor, though the author may be referencing the general Roman imperial tradition of patronage.</note> with those same hands that gave laws to the

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of Leonardo da Vinci's treatise to Queen Christina of Sweden, comparing her lineage to the great patrons of antiquity like Alexander the Great.</summary>
<keywords>Queen Christina of Sweden, dedication, Alexander the Great, Gustavus Adolphus, Apelles, Fabius Pictor, Roman history, art patronage</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication to Queen Christina of Sweden from the 1651 first edition of Leonardo da Vinci’s "Treatise on Painting." It transitions from praising ancient Roman precedents to celebrating the Queen's own intellect and her role as a modern patron of the arts.</meta>

world, and with those same hands he <note>Referring to the Emperor Antoninus mentioned on the previous page.</note> sometimes delighted in handling the brushes. Everyone knows the love she <note>Queen Christina.</note> bears for literature and for all the fine arts, and the world admires her as a protector and possessor of even the most hidden sciences; it is amazed to see her rule so happily united with so many virtuous qualities. Hoping, therefore, that this work—which I have consecrated to her merits and which carries on its front the August name of Your Majesty—will be welcomed by you, I have entreated Monsieur Bourdelot <note>Pierre Michon Bourdelot (1610–1685), a French physician and scholar who was a prominent figure at the Queen's court in Stockholm.</note>, the delight of the learned men of our nation and one who has a particular taste for matters of painting, to present it to Your Majesty, so that through the kindness of the presenter, the gift may acquire more grace in your eyes. 

The author <note>Leonardo da Vinci (1452–1519).</note>, who wrote at the beginning of the last century, was favored by great princes; and King Francis I <note>Francis I of France (reigned 1515–1547), a major patron of the Renaissance who invited Leonardo to spend his final years in France.</note>, who like Your Majesty was the guardian deity of the talented, wished to have him in his court despite his advanced age, and it is known that he died in the King’s arms <note>A famous, though likely apocryphal, legend popularized by Vasari to illustrate the high status of artists.</note>. Fortunate old man! Since today it is his good fortune to live again in the hands of a lady who, by her rule over so many fierce and warlike nations, can be called the most powerful, and by her rule of virtue, the most complete and glorious princess of the universe. Those who speak the language of the gods <note>A poetic reference to poets and philosophers.</note> must, with one voice, call her the Queen of Parnassus <note>In Greek mythology, Mount Parnassus was the home of the Muses; calling her its Queen designates her as the ultimate patron of the arts and sciences.</note>.

But rather than penetrate further into the vast field of her praises—this being a subject not suited to the slenderness of my style—I come to humbly entreat Your Majesty to accept my labors. For the sake of the reputation of Leonardo da Vinci and for the public utility, I have restored a very important work which, accompanied by your illustrious name, shall overcome the shadows of oblivion and pass into the most distant posterity. I shall remain happy if you deign to receive it with a kindly brow, just as I give and dedicate it with the deep affection of my heart, being no less reverent of your greatness than an admirer of your glories.

->OF YOUR MAESTY,<-

Humblest and most devoted
servant,
RAPHAËL TRICHET 
DU FRESNE.

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication of the 1651 first edition of Leonardo da Vinci’s "Treatise on Painting." The previous page concluded by noting Leonardo’s death in the arms of King Francis I and his metaphorical rebirth in the hands of Queen Christina of Sweden. This page addresses Pierre Bourdelot directly, explaining the scholarly labor behind the book's publication.</meta>

## TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS AND MOST EXCELLENT LORD
# LORD PIERRE BOURDELOT,
### First Physician to the Most Serene Queen of Sweden.

->*Most illustrious and most excellent Lord, and my most revered master.*<-

**I** believed that the nobility of the art whose precepts are explained in this work, and the merit of Leonardo da Vinci, who is its author, as well as the beauty and curiosity with which this book has been printed, could not be adorned with a more glorious name than that of his great Queen. I believed also that if Your Excellency would lend me your hands on this occasion <note>A metaphorical request for Bourdelot to act as the intermediary to present the book to the Queen.</note>, my offering would be more welcome. And I easily persuaded myself that by entreating you, as I do, you would not deny me this service, as much for our long friendship as for the love you bear for painting. This taste was born in you and in me at the same time—that is, when we went about examining with such accuracy the beauties of both the one and the other Rome <note>This refers to the study of both Ancient (classical) and Modern (17th-century) Rome.</note>. Growing with continuous application, this passion has become, especially in you, ever more refined and exquisite.

In printing this treatise, I have made use of various manuscripts. One, which is most noble due to a great number of figures sketched by the learned hand of Monsieur Poussin <note>Nicolas Poussin (1594–1665), the preeminent French Baroque painter, provided the original drawings for the illustrations in this edition.</note>, belonged to Monsieur de Chantelou <note>Paul Fréart de Chantelou (1609–1694), a major French collector and patron.</note>, who received it from the most virtuous Knight del Pozzo <note>Cassiano dal Pozzo (1588–1657), a celebrated Roman scholar and secretary to Cardinal Francesco Barberini.</note>. At the time when Monsieur de Chantelou went to Italy to conquer beautiful things, had our great Cardinal <note>Cardinal Richelieu (1585–1642).</note> not died for the glory of the kingdom, he would have brought Rome to Paris. The other manuscript, which is much more correct, was communicated to me by the courtesy of Monsieur Thévenot <note>Melchisédech Thévenot (1620–1692), a diplomat and scientist known for his extensive library.</note>, a gentleman adorned with every kind of fine literature and knowledge.

But whether through the ignorance or negligence of those who copy books, or for whatever other reason, few chapters were found in which there was not some hitch—principally in those involving geometry, which, due to the absurdity of the figures, remained almost unintelligible. I hope to have restored everything to its original purity. However, many things remain that seem to desire the file <note>A metaphor for further editing or polishing.</note>: there are many useless repetitions, many truncated arguments, and the wording is irregular in several places. Although there is some order in the chapters, it is not yet such as is required in a perfect work; from this, one easily concludes that Leonardo da Vinci never gave it the final touch. Nevertheless, the work is significant and of marvelous utility; for Your Excellency knows well that a sketch by Michelangelo <note>Michelangelo Buonarroti (1475–1564), Leonardo's contemporary and rival.</note> is worth more than four finished statues by any other mediocre sculptor.

Having therefore purged the text as much as possible, the figures were engraved with the diligence you see to further illustrate it. Monsieur Errard <note>Charles Errard (c. 1606–1689), a founding member of the French Academy who supervised the engravings for this book.</note>, a most talented painter who, for his profound knowledge of drawing, can only be compared to the most excellent men of the last centuries, is the one to whom the completion and ornaments of this work are owed. A philosopher might say of him that, because of the true taste he has for ancient things, the soul of one of those first masters had passed into him. He has added several fi- <note>original: "fi-" refers to "figure" (figures) on the following page.</note>

<summary>In this dedication, the publisher explains how he compiled Leonardo da Vinci’s scattered notes using manuscripts from famous collectors like Cassiano dal Pozzo and Melchisédech Thévenot. He credits Nicolas Poussin for the drawings and Charles Errard for the engravings, admitting that while the text is unpolished, Leonardo's genius makes even his rough notes invaluable.</summary>
<keywords>Pierre Bourdelot, Leonardo da Vinci, Nicolas Poussin, Paul Fréart de Chantelou, Cassiano dal Pozzo, Melchisédech Thévenot, Michelangelo, Charles Errard, book production, provenance, manuscripts, geometry in art</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedication from the editor, Raphaël Trichet du Fresne, to Pierre Bourdelot. It transitions from discussing the illustrations provided by Nicolas Poussin to announcing the inclusion of works by Leon Battista Alberti and new biographies of the artists written by the editor himself to supplement those of Vasari. The catchword "VITA" (Life) suggests that the subsequent section of the book begins the biographies.</meta>

figures, and among others those seen toward the end of the book, where there is discussion on the method of drapery <term>panneggiare: the artistic technique of rendering the folds and flow of fabric in painting or sculpture</term> and of dressing the figures: for the rest, use was made of those ideas and sketches by Monsieur Poussin <note>Nicolas Poussin (1594–1665), the leading painter of the French Baroque, who lived and worked primarily in Rome.</note> which were found in the manuscript of Monsieur de Chantelou <note>Paul Fréart de Chantelou, a French diplomat and major patron of the arts who was a close friend of Poussin.</note>. Such is the history of this treatise. I believed that Your most Excellent Lordship would appreciate being informed of it. 

Due to the similarity of the subject matter, there have been added the three books on painting by Leon Battista Alberti <note>A 15th-century humanist, architect, and principal theorist of Renaissance art.</note>, and the treatise on the statue <note>Original: "trattato della statua," referring to Alberti's *De Statua*.</note> by the same author, which was no longer to be found, having never been printed except for a single time. Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari, whose 1550/1568 book "The Lives of the Artists" is the foundational text of art history.</note> previously wrote the lives of both authors, but because he omitted many things worthy of observation, I have set out to write them anew, adding what my reading of books and the knowledge I have acquired of Italian matters have suggested to me. I have written in the Italian language because, knowing enough of it to be understood, it seemed to me that the nature of the work <note>Meaning the accompanying text by Leonardo da Vinci.</note> required it so. 

If at another time I have the occasion to speak Latin or French, perhaps I will succeed better, and will be able to explain my concepts more happily. Meanwhile, I beseech Your most Excellent Lordship to be my protector, and to offer my writings that help which they perhaps could not hope for from their author, manifesting the superabundance of your love in the multitude of my defects. And I kiss your hands a thousand times.

->*Of Your most Excellent Lordship,*<-

->The most devoted and most cordial servant,<-
->**RAPHAËL TRICHET DU FRESNE.**<-

<summary>In this concluding dedication, Raphaël Trichet du Fresne explains the scholarly sources for the 1651 edition, including illustrations by Poussin and the addition of works by Leon Battista Alberti, while justifying his decision to write the new biographies in Italian.</summary>

This follows the promise made in the preceding dedication to Pierre Bourdelot to provide a more detailed and updated account of the artist's life than that found in Vasari's earlier works.</meta>

### LIFE
## OF LEONARDO
# DA VINCI
->DESCRIBED BY<-
### RAFAELLE DU FRESNE.

***

<margin>
S
</margin>

*ince nobility of blood, which is an imaginary thing, creates such a distinction among men that it raises some above others, who is there who would not value that of the soul—which consists in effective virtue and resides in that part which draws its origin from heaven—as being capable of carrying men from the lowest state even to the boundaries of divinity? Adorned with this true and most resplendent nobility, Leonardo da Vinci was able to equal the greatest men of his century in glory and honors, and by rising above the lowliness of his birth, to live, associate, and die with kings and great princes; and, what is granted to few, to leave immortality to his name. He was born in the castle of Vinci, located in the lower Val d'Arno, not too far from Florence <note>Original: "Fiorenza," the archaic Italian name for the city of Florence.</note>, and his father was Piero da Vinci. The latter, noticing the genius of his son—who among his other studies always applied himself to drawing—resolved to assist that natural inclination of his, and having brought him to Florence, decided to place him with Andrea Verrocchio, a painter of some reputation at that time. This man, admiring the talent of the youth, formed that judgment of him which time later proved very true; and having accepted him as his disciple, he promised all the more to Ser Piero to instruct him, as there was a close friendship between them, and because Leonardo, through his fine manners and character, seemed worthy of his care. In the school of Andrea, who not only applied himself to painting but was also a sculptor, architect, engraver <term>intagliatore: a carver or engraver, often of wood or gemstones</term>, and goldsmith, he learned not only the art of painting, but furthermore all those others where design <term>disegno: a central concept in Italian art referring to both the physical act of drawing and the underlying intellectual structure or "design" of a work</term> was involved. And such was the progress that he made there, that in a short time he left be-*

<summary>This page introduces a biography of Leonardo da Vinci, beginning with a philosophical defense of merit over birthright before detailing his early education under Andrea del Verrocchio in Florence.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, biography, nobility of soul, Andrea Verrocchio, Florence, apprenticeship, Renaissance education, draftsmanship</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Leonardo da Vinci, specifically focusing on his apprenticeship with Andrea del Verrocchio and his early independent works in Florence. It follows the previous page's introduction to Leonardo's parentage and his entry into Verrocchio's workshop.</meta>

V I T A

# V I T A

*...after his own master. Of whom one reads that while painting on a panel for the friars of Vallombrosa <note>A religious order; the painting mentioned is "The Baptism of Christ," now in the Uffizi Gallery.</note>, who are at San Salvi outside of Florence <note>Original: "Fiorenza," the archaic name for Florence.</note>, depicting the story of Saint John baptizing Christ, he wished for Leonardo to help him. He gave him an angel to color, which held some garments in its hands. Leonardo executed it with such mastery, just as Andrea had commissioned, that it far surpassed the rest of the work; and everyone clearly judged that the other parts of the picture were much inferior in beauty to the angel. Verrocchio blushed, and seeing himself surpassed by a youth who was his pupil—incensed against his own brushes—he never again wished to use colors, and said goodbye to painting forever.*

*Having left the school, Leonardo, being now at an age to govern himself, produced those works in Florence that are mentioned by Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari (1511–1574), the famous biographer of Italian Renaissance artists.</note>. Namely, for the King of Portugal, the cartoon <note>A full-scale preparatory drawing for a painting or tapestry.</note> of Adam and Eve when they sinned in the terrestrial paradise, in which, besides the two figures, he painted in chiaroscuro <note>The technique of using strong contrasts between light and dark to create volume.</note> with incredible patience and diligence the trees and the grasses of the meadows. He also made, at the request of his father Piero for a peasant of his from Vinci, upon a round shield <note>Original: "rotella," a circular wooden buckler.</note> of fig wood, such a composition of various and strange little animals—such as snakes, lizards, green lizards, crickets, and locusts—that from all of them together one creature was formed, so frightening and horrible that, in the manner of Medusa’s head, it rendered anyone who looked at it immobile with shock. But the father, judging that this was not a work to be put into the hands of a rustic, sold it to certain merchants; it was later bought for 300 ducats by the Duke of Milan. In a painting, he made a most rare Madonna, and among other things, he depicted a carafe full of water with some flowers inside, upon which with admirable skill he had imitated the dew on the water; which painting Pope Clement VII later owned. Vasari also makes mention of a drawing made on a sheet for Antonio Segni, his very close friend, in which with rare invention and his usual accuracy he depicted a Neptune in the middle of a stormy sea, with his chariot pulled by sea horses, accompanied by whales, tritons, and other fantastic things that seemed appropriate to him for such a subject.*

*In this place, we shall observe that although Vinci knew to such a degree what that divine proportion consists of—which is the mother of beauty—that his figures full of grace inspired love in the beholders, he nonetheless took such pleasure in painting bizarre and distorted things. If he encountered some peasant who, with a strange and somewhat extraordinary face, appeared slightly ridiculous, he would be so captivated by the oddity of the object that he would follow him for an entire day, until, having a perfect idea of him, he returned home and drew him as if he had him present. And Paolo Lomazzo <note>A 16th-century painter and art theorist.</note> observes in the sixth book of his work on painting, chapter 32, that in his time Aurelio Luini <note>Original: "Lovino," referring to the son of Bernardino Luini, a follower of Leonardo.</note> had fifty of these drawn by his hand in a book. In this genre is painted that picture which is seen here in Paris among many others preserved in a room of the royal palace of the Tuileries under the care of Monsieur Le Maire, a painter—as everyone knows—of no ordinary worth. In this, two knights are painted in the act of forcibly taking a banner from two others; this group was part of a larger work, namely the cartoon he made for the hall of the palace in Florence, as will be mentioned below, but because of its beauty, it was painted by him in a small size with incredible taste and love. Here, beyond the...*

# OF LEONARDO DA VINCI.
## ẽ ij

...[the fu]ry of the horses, and the oddity of the garments, one sees the heads of the combatants wrinkled, inflamed, and infuriated, with an air so extraordinary and extravagant, and—so to speak—caricatured <note>Original: "caricata," meaning exaggerated or burdened, from which we get the modern word caricature.</note>, and like a grotesque mask <note>Original: "mascarone," an architectural ornament representing a distorted face.</note>, that at the same time they awaken both fear and laughter in the minds of the beholders.

Returning to the first works of Leonardo da Vinci, Giorgio Vasari says that he began in an oil painting a head of Medusa of extravagant invention, which remained imperfect. He also gave a beginning to a panel of the Adoration of the Magi, in which there were some most beautiful heads; but it was never finished, as usually happened to most of his things. This was because, having an infinity of beautiful insights, and being of a lively nature and most fertile genius, no sooner had he begun a work than the thought of putting another into execution came to him. And besides the profession of painting, which by that most diligent manner embraced by him could have occupied him entirely, he attended to sculpture and modeled divinely well. He was most intelligent in geometry, and in mechanics he never ceased thinking of new devices, and was the inventor of diverse machines. He was a most excellent architect, and knew like no other the science of mirrors <note>The study of optics and catoptrics.</note> and perspective <term>perspective: the mathematical art of representing three-dimensional depth on a flat surface</term>. He also studied the properties of herbs <note>Botany</note>, and penetrating with his genius even into the heavens, he applied himself to the studies of astronomy, and made many observations regarding the motion of the stars. In music he turned out admirable, and was so graceful in singing and playing that he surpassed all the musicians of his time: and so that no virtue would be lacking in him, that same fury inspired in him by Apollo <note>The Greek and Roman god of the arts and music; "furore" refers to divine creative inspiration.</note> that made him a painter and musician, also made him a poet. But all his compositions having been lost, only this moral sonnet has reached us.

->He who cannot do what he wants, let him want what he can,<-
->For to want what cannot be done is folly.<-
->Therefore, that man is to be held wise,<-
->Who from that which he cannot do, withdraws his will.<-
->Because all our delight and sorrow<-
->Lies in knowing how to will and be able to say yes and no,<-
->Therefore, only he can do what is right who with duty<-
->Draws reason out of its sheath. <note>Original: "foglia," literally leaf, but used here as a metaphor for a covering or husk.</note><-
->Nor is it always right to want what a man is able to do,<-
->Often what seems sweet turns bitter.<-
->I have already wept for what I wanted once I had it.<-
->Therefore you, reader of these notes,<-
->If you wish to be good to yourself, and dear to others,<-
->Always wish to be able to do what you ought.<-

He was also distracted by many pleasures, for he liked horses beyond measure and handled them with dexterity; and being no less agile and robust of limb than of beautiful presence, and graceful in every action, he was a distinguished fencer and swordsman. But above all, he delighted in conversing often with friends, and was so well-mannered in his dealings, and explained his thoughts with such grace and urbanity, that he drew to himself the souls of whoever listened to him.

<meta>This page continues the biography of Leonardo da Vinci, moving from a description of his multi-faceted genius and his tendency to leave works unfinished to his relocation to Milan and his major accomplishments there in both engineering and painting.</meta>

# LIFE

So many rare qualities, and such a great acquisition of the sciences, spread Leonardo’s name throughout all Italy, and induced Ludovico Sforza, called "the Moor" <note>Original: "il Moro," a nickname likely referring to his dark complexion or the mulberry tree on his coat of arms.</note>, who favored the virtuous and was liberal toward almost everyone, to call him to Milan, assigning him an annual salary of five hundred scudi. The first thing that prince did was to form an academy for architecture, into which he introduced Leonardo, who, casting aside the Gothic styles of the first school—already established in that same city a hundred years earlier under Michelino—opened the way to restore that art to its first and ancient purity. He was then employed by the same prince to conduct the waters of the Adda as far as Milan, and to form that navigable canal, commonly called the Martesana Canal <note>The Naviglio della Martesana, one of the great engineering feats of the Renaissance.</note>, with the addition of more than two hundred miles of navigable river reaching the valleys of Chiavenna and Valtellina. The undertaking was difficult and important, and worthy of Leonardo's fine intellect because of the noble competition with the "Great Canal" <note>The Naviglio Grande.</note> which had been made two hundred years earlier in the times of the Milanese Republic on the other side of the city, by which the waters of the Ticino river are diverted for navigation and for the irrigation of the countryside as far as Milan. But he overcame all the difficulties that were encountered, and with multiplied <term>locks: or "sostegni," water-control structures used to change the level of a canal</term>, or as we might say, sluices, he made the ships travel through mountains and valleys with great ease and safety.

The prince, not content that Leonardo should illustrate his state only as an architect and engineer, also wished for him to adorn it with some signal work of painting. He therefore ordered him to paint the Last Supper of Christ with the Apostles in the refectory <note>The communal dining hall of a monastery.</note> of the Dominican fathers of Santa Maria delle Grazie: which was executed by Leonardo with such mastery that the work was afterwards esteemed by everyone as a miracle of painting. And truly, all the refinements of art were displayed there with such pomp that everyone writes—and it is the common voice—that in design, in expression, in diligence, and in coloring, nothing superior to this was ever seen. The grace and majesty he gave to the heads of the apostles were extraordinary, and especially to those of the two Jameses; so much so that when he came to finish the head of Christ, being unable to reach a more eminent degree of beauty, he left it imperfect out of despair.

And because, while working on the painting, it seemed to the prior of the convent that the work was lasting too long, he often complained importunately to Leonardo, and even carried his grievances to the ears of the Duke. When the Duke discussed this once with Leonardo, he learned from him that nothing remained to be done except the two heads of Christ and Judas. He explained that as he could not imagine the infinite beauty of the Son of God, he knew even less how he could express it with brushes. But as for the ugliness of Judas, son of hell, which was occupying his thoughts, he would not lack the snout of that ungrateful friar, who with an intolerable and insolent tediousness had made himself beyond measure troublesome to them both.

He succeeded marvelously, as Vasari writes, in expressing that suspicion which had entered the apostles, wanting to know who was betraying their master. And Lomazzo recounts (who, having made a large copy of it in San Barnaba in Milan, had that work strongly impressed upon his soul) that in each one could be seen admiration, terror, grief, suspicion, love, and similar passions and affections in which everyone was then found, and finally

<meta>This page continues the biography of Leonardo da Vinci, focusing on his artistic and scientific achievements during his long residency in Milan under the patronage of Ludovico Sforza, and begins the complex history of his surviving manuscripts.</meta>

-># OF LEONARDO DA VINCI<-

...the betrayal conceived in the mind of Judas, with a countenance exactly resembling a villain. Thus, Leonardo clearly demonstrated how perfectly he understood the motions that the soul causes in the body <note>Leonardo referred to these as "moti mentali," the physical expression of internal emotions.</note>, which is the most delicate part of art and, due to its difficulty, the least practiced. Such a work was worthy of immortality, but because it was painted in oil upon a damp wall, it was of short duration, and today it is entirely ruined. When Francis I <note>King of France from 1515 to 1547 and a great patron of Leonardo.</note> was in Milan, he desired that every possible means be attempted to carry it to France to enrich his kingdom; however, since it was painted upon a thick wall, thirty feet high and wide, the thought proved vain. It is, however, likely that he had some copies made of it, and one of these is perhaps the one seen today in the royal parish of St. Germain <note>Saint-Germain-l'Auxerrois in Paris.</note>, fixed to the wall on the left hand as one enters the church through the south-facing door. In the same refectory <note>The dining hall of the convent of Santa Maria delle Grazie.</note> where Leonardo painted that **Last Supper**, he also painted from life Duke Ludovico and Duchess Beatrice, his wife, both kneeling with their children before them, and a Christ on the cross on the other side. For the same Duke, he also painted a Nativity of Christ on an altarpiece, which was sent to the Emperor.

Among Leonardo’s other occupations during his stay in Milan, his study of human anatomy was most important. In this, he was assisted by Marc’Antonio della Torre, who at that time lectured and wrote on this subject in Pavia. Leonardo became most perfect in this study and produced a book drawn in red chalk and hatched with a pen, which later remained in the hands of Francesco Melzi, his disciple. He also drew for Gentile Borri, who professed the art of arms (in which Leonardo himself took great delight), an entire book of men fighting on foot and on horseback, in which the rules of that science were expressed. For the glory and growth of his Milanese Academy, and for the instruction of the academicians, he wrote many things and composed several works on various subjects. These remained for a long time neglected and almost unknown with the Melzi gentlemen at their villa in Vaprio <note>Original: "Vauero," referring to Vaprio d'Adda.</note>, and were later dissipated and dispersed here and there, as is the common fate of books.

There was a certain Lelio Gavardi d’Asola, Provost of San Zeno in Pavia and a close relative of Aldo Manuzio <note>The famous Venetian printer.</note>, who, having been the tutor of the Melzi gentlemen and frequently visiting the said villa, took thirteen volumes from there and carried them to Florence, hoping for a great price from the Grand Duke. However, that prince died in the meantime, and Gavardi went to Pisa. There he encountered Giovanni Ambrogio Mazzenta, a Milanese gentleman who was then a student. Mazzenta made Gavardi feel a <term>scruple: a feeling of guilt or moral doubt</term> regarding the ill-gotten gains; Gavardi felt remorse and begged him that, upon returning to Milan, he should restore the books to the Melzi family. This he did; but in returning them, Signor Orazio Melzi, head of that family, was amazed by the punctuality of both men and made a gift of the said books to Signor Giovanni Ambrogio, which then remained in the house of the Mazzenta family. Because they made too pompous a display of them, Pompeo Leoni, sculptor to the King of Spain, made Melzi realize how valuable those books were, and promised him honors and offices if, by recovering them, he made a gift of them to King Philip. Moved by such hope, Melzi flew to Signor Guido Mazzenta, brother of Giovanni Ambrogio, and kneeling, begged him to return those...

-># LIFE<-

...works of da Vinci. Moved by the prayers of his colleague, he returned seven of them, and six remained in the Mazzenta house. One of these was donated to Cardinal Borromeo for his Ambrosiana library, and another to Ambrogio Figgini, who upon dying left it to his heir Ercole Bianchi. A third was acquired by Charles Emmanuel, Duke of Savoy, and upon the death of Signor Guido, the remaining ones came into the hands of the aforementioned Pompeo Leoni <note>A famous sculptor and collector who compiled many of Leonardo's notes into the "Codex Atlanticus."</note>, who left them to Cleodoro Calchi, his heir, who sold them for 300 scudi to Signor Galeazzo Lonato. Leonardo was accustomed, when he wished to philosophize and apply himself with intense focus to study, to retreat to the said villa of Vaprio <note>The Villa Melzi d’Eril in Vaprio d’Adda.</note>, and it is known that he lived there for many years with Francesco Melzi, his disciple. Below, the index of his writings will be placed.

After the fall of "Il Moro" <note>Ludovico Sforza, nicknamed "The Moor," Leonardo's primary patron in Milan.</note>, who was led as a prisoner to France in the year 1500 and died in the Tower of Loches, the study of the fine arts in Milan grew quite cold due to the wars that followed. The academy already begun there gradually dissipated; in this academy, Francesco Melzi, Cesare da Sesto, Bernardino Luini, Andrea Salai, Marco d'Oggiono, Antonio Boltraffio, Paolo Lomazzo, and other Milanese had become excellent in painting. They were all imitators of da Vinci to such an extent that their works were—and are today—often believed, esteemed, and sold as being by Leonardo's hand, especially those of Sesto and Luini, who most closely approached the master's style. But Lomazzo would have risen above them all had he not lost his sight in the greenest years of his youth, as had been predicted by Girolamo Cardano <note>A famous Italian polymath, physician, and astrologer.</note>. Being unable to work with his hand, he dedicated himself to treating painting with his intellect, and though blind, he composed those books <note>Such as the *Trattato dell'arte della pittura* (1584).</note> which are esteemed as excellent even by the most sharp-sighted, in which he continually proposes da Vinci as the ideal of the true and perfect painter.

At the time when Louis XII, King of France, came to Milan (which was one year before the capture of Il Moro), Leonardo was asked by the city's leading citizens to invent some whimsical and magnificent machine with which to gift and delight that great prince. He made a lion of such artifice that, after walking a good distance in a hall, it stopped before the King, and then, opening its chest, it was seen to be full of lilies <note>The fleur-de-lis, the heraldic symbol of the French monarchy.</note>. Through an error by the one who wrote under Lomazzo (Book 2, Chapter 1), it is read that such a thing was done for Francis I, which cannot be true because he entered Milan in the year 1515, at which time Leonardo was in Rome, as will be seen below.

The turmoil in Lombardy and the misfortunes of the Sforza family, Leonardo's patrons, forced him to abandon Milan and return to Florence, his homeland. The first thing he did there was that famous cartoon <term>cartoon: a full-scale preparatory drawing for a fresco or painting</term> of the Virgin with Christ and Saint Anne, with Saint John, which was intended for the high altar of the Annunziata. It was visited in a great flock by all the people of Florence. This cartoon was later carried by Leonardo himself to France, where the King desired him to paint it in color.

He then made for Francesco del Giocondo the much-celebrated portrait of Lisa, his wife, commonly called *La Gioconda* <note>Known in the English-speaking world as the *Mona Lisa*.</note>, which is seen at Fontainebleau in the company of many other precious paintings of the "Most Christian King" <note>A traditional title for the Kings of France.</note>, and was bought for four thousand scudi by Francis I. It is said that he spent four years working on that portrait and that he nevertheless left it unfinished, having such delicate taste and a mind so sharp and subtle that to reach the truth of na—

# OF LEONARDO DA VINCI.

*...nature, he always sought excellence upon excellence and perfection upon perfection; not contenting himself with what was already done, however beautiful, he pursued with anxiety that "something more" that could be achieved. While he painted, he was accustomed to have around Lady Lisa <note>Lisa Gherardini, the subject of the "Mona Lisa."</note> people who sang, played music, and laughed, to keep her cheerful and to avoid that common drawback of portraits, which for the most part result in a melancholy air. And truly, in this work one sees a smile <note>original: "gigno"</note> so pleasing that, as Vasari says, it is a thing more divine than human to behold. Also beautiful is another portrait by the same Leonardo which is at Fontainebleau, and is said to be of a Marchioness of Mantua <note>Likely Isabella d'Este.</note>. Most beautiful was that of Ginevra d'Amerigo Benci, a girl of famous beauty in those times. Nor should one omit the "Flora," painted with wonderful charm and a truly divine air; this is preserved in Paris and is in the hands of a private individual.*

*Having, around the year 1503, to decorate the Council Hall in the palace of Florence, Leonardo was elected by public decree to paint it. To this effect, he created a cartoon <note>A full-scale preparatory drawing for a fresco or mural.</note> full of art and beautiful considerations, in which was expressed a history of Piccinino <note>The Battle of Anghiari, where the Florentine Republic defeated the Milanese forces led by Niccolò Piccinino.</note>. He had already colored the greater part of it in oil when, noticing that due to the priming layer <term>imprimitura: the initial layer of paint or ground applied to a surface.</term> being too thick everything was peeling from the wall, and that his labors were in vain, he abandoned the work.*

*At that time—which was during the papacy of Pius III, not the second, as one reads in Vasari—Raphael of Urbino, who had barely reached the age of twenty and had recently left the school of Pietro Perugino, came to Florence for the first time, eager to see that famous cartoon and enamored by the fame of Leonardo da Vinci, who was then passing the sixtieth year of his age. He stood amazed at the sight of Leonardo's works, and never had a more powerful stimulus to make him run and swiftly reach 그 high perfection of art, which caused him to be revered by everyone as the god of painting; departing from that time forward from the dry and harsh manner of Perugino to pass into the softness and tenderness of Vinci. The young Raphael was also a spectator, not without profit, of the disputes that later caused such enmity between Leonardo and Michelangelo Buonarroti. Michelangelo was not yet twenty-nine years old, and by public order had created for another wall of the same Council Hall that much-celebrated cartoon of the War of Pisa <note>The Battle of Cascina.</note>, filled with various nudes made in competition with Vinci. Until the year 1513, Leonardo remained in Florence and painted many things there. Francesco Bocchi, in the book he wrote on the beauties of Florence <note>"Le bellezze della città di Firenze," published in 1591.</note>, mentions a small painting that in his time was seen in the house of Matteo and Giovan Battista Botti, in which a Madonna was painted with supreme art and diligence, with a wonderfully beautiful Christ child who lifted his face with singular grace. By Borghini <note>Raffaello Borghini, author of "Il Riposo."</note>, a head of St. John the Baptist is mentioned as a rare thing, which was in the hands of Camillo degli Albizi.*

*But upon the elevation to the papacy of Leo X, in whom the love of painting and all the fine arts was an inherited trait, Leonardo hurried to Rome to pay his respects to that prince and patron <term>Mecenate: a generous patron of the arts, named after the Roman advisor Maecenas.</term> of talented men. When the Pope ordered a panel painting from him, Vasari recounts that Leonardo immediately began with great preparation to distill oils and prepare the varnish; and that Leo, being informed of this, said that nothing could be hoped for from one who thought of the finish before having even examined the beginning.*

<meta>This page continues the biography of Leonardo da Vinci, transitioning from his rivalry with Michelangelo in Italy to his final years in France under the patronage of King Francis I, concluding with a survey of his known paintings in French and Italian collections.</meta>

<header>LIFE</header>

of the work. He <note>Likely referring to Giorgio Vasari, the famous biographer of Renaissance artists.</note> also recounts certain minor things unworthy of the greatness of Vinci’s genius, which must be considered suspicious, having been written by a person very partial to Michelangelo. As we have said, Michelangelo professed an open enmity toward Leonardo and delighted in diminishing his reputation with feigned and mythical pranks. That implacable hatred deeply displeased Leonardo; seeing himself called by King Francis, who had fallen in love with his works during his stay in Milan, he resolved—though he was over seventy years old—to embrace such an honored and glorious offer and to make the journey to France.

The pleasure the King felt was extraordinary, seeing himself the possessor of a man of such talent, whom he so greatly esteemed and desired. And although Leonardo could hardly work anymore due to his old age, he was nonetheless always well-regarded and pampered by the King. Everyone knows that having been ill for many months at Fontainebleau, the King came to visit him. Wishing to sit up in bed out of reverence to recount the details of his illness, Leonardo suffered a seizure. The King took his head to help and support him; having recognized this favor, Leonardo breathed his last in the King's arms at the age of seventy-five. He died more glorious than any other painter—if it is true that "a beautiful death honors a whole life." <note>original: "un bel morir tutta la vita honora." This is a famous quote from the poet Petrarch.</note>

He was very beautiful of body, as was mentioned above. Once his youth had passed, with a philosophical negligence he let his hair and beard grow long, so that he looked like an ancient Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary Hellenistic figure associated with wisdom and alchemy.</note> or a Druid. He never wished to take a wife, or if he had one (as another painter used to say), it was none other than Art, and his works were his children. One should not believe that all of them have been mentioned here, because the Great Duke of Florence has many others, and I remember having seen several in England. <margin>In the *Idea of the Temple of Painting* by Paolo Lomazzo, chapter 33.</margin> mention is made of a *Conception of the Virgin* <note>Known today as the "Virgin of the Rocks."</note> painted for the church of San Francesco in Milan. In the Ambrosian Library of the same city, many drawings and paintings by this author are preserved.

Here in Paris, in the Palais-Cardinal <note>The residence of Cardinal Richelieu, now the Palais-Royal.</note>, one can see a Madonna by his hand, who sits in the lap of Saint Anne and holds in her hands the Christ Child, who plays with a lamb. There is a very beautiful landscape, but the head of the Virgin remains unfinished. Cardinal Richelieu owned a *Herodias* <note>A painting of Salome with the head of John the Baptist.</note> of exquisite beauty. The *Saint John in the Desert*, a full figure which is at Fontainebleau, and another painting of a Madonna with Christ, Saint John, and an angel of miraculous beauty set in a landscape, are things to be observed. In the study of the Marquis de Sourdis in Paris, there is another Madonna of great reputation.

Monsieur de Ciarmois, secretary to Marshal de Schomberg—a gentleman of rare qualities who, joining curiosity with intelligence, has made a considerable collection of fine paintings—possesses one by Vinci. In it, with two half-figures, is represented the young and handsome Joseph who, while fleeing, turns his back to the beautiful but dishonest wife of Potiphar. The whole is painted with great love and diligence; the expression is wonderful, and the modesty of the one and the lust of the other seem in the two faces to be real things rather than fictions. Belonging to the same gentleman is a Madonna with Saint Anne and a Christ Child to whom Saint Michael offers a pair of scales, and a Saint John playing with a lamb; it is a painting of extreme beauty. But it would be too much to try to record all of Vinci's paintings; it remains that after the works of the brush, we should speak of those of the pen.

Vinci used to write with his left hand, according to the custom of the Hebrews <note>A reference to Leonardo's famous "mirror writing," which moves from right to left.</note>, in which...

<meta>This page continues the bibliographic overview of Leonardo’s lost and extant manuscripts, focusing on his scientific and artistic treatises, leading into the history of how this specific edition was compiled and translated into French.</meta>

# OF LEONARDO DA VINCI.

*...manner those thirteen volumes were written, of which we have already made mention; and the handwriting being good, it was read quite easily by means of a large mirror. <note>Leonardo da Vinci famously used "mirror writing," writing from right to left, possibly to prevent smudging ink as a left-handed writer or to keep his notes private.</note> It is probable that he did this so that not everyone could so easily read his writings.*

*The project of the Martesana canal <note>The Naviglio della Martesana is one of the famous canals in Milan which Leonardo helped modernize.</note> gave him the occasion to write a book on the nature, weight, and motion of waters, full of a great number of drawings of various wheels and machines for mills, and for regulating the course of waters and raising them on high.*

*He wrote on the anatomy of the human body, as has already been said, which work was adorned with various drawings made with great study and diligence, and he himself makes mention of it in chapter 22 of this treatise on painting.*

*The book on the anatomy of horses is mentioned by Vasari, Borghini, and Lomazzo. <note>These were prominent 16th-century art historians and theorists.</note> Since he was excellent at sculpting them and painting them—as the painting of the four fighting knights mentioned above bears witness—there is no doubt that the work was of extraordinary beauty and utility.*

*In chapters 81 and 110 of this treatise, he cites a work of his on perspective, divided into several books. Perhaps in that work, the method of drawing figures larger than life was taught, which was praised by Lomazzo in his "Idea," <term>Idea del tempio della pittura</term> <note>A foundational book on art theory by Giovanni Paolo Lomazzo, published in 1590.</note> chapter 4.*

*In chapters 112 and 123, he promises to make a book on the movements of the body and its parts: an anatomical subject that has never been touched by anyone.*

*He also promises in chapter 268 a treatise on "ponderation," or rather, the balancing of the body. <term>ponderatione</term> <note>From the Latin 'ponderare,' this refers to the distribution of weight in a figure to achieve a natural, balanced posture (contrapposto).</note>*

*The book on shadows and lights is found today in the Ambrosian Library in Milan, in folio, <note>A "folio" is a large-format book made by folding sheets of paper only once.</note> covered in red velvet; it is the one that, as was said above, was given by Signor Guido Mazzenta to Cardinal Borromeo. He treats that subject as a philosopher, a mathematician, and a painter, and mentions it in this treatise, chapter 278. He was miraculous in this part of painting, imitating with such sagacity the effects that light produces with color, that his works had more of the natural than of the artificial.*

*There remains the "Treatise on Painting," which contains various precepts of that art, along with the methods of drawing and coloring. Vasari recounts that a certain Milanese painter, passing through Florence, showed him that work and told him that when he reached Rome, he would immediately have it printed; but that was not carried out by him. What was not done in Rome is now, after a full century, put into execution in Paris, where, by the comparison of various manuscripts—all corrupt and spoiled—a work has been restored by me which, for the excellence of its precepts and the merit of its author, is worthy of immortality. And to make it even more familiar to our nation, <note>The "nation" here refers to France; this text served as the introduction to the first printed edition of Leonardo's work, published in Paris in 1651.</note> Signor di Ciambre <note>original: "Signor di Ciambre." This refers to Roland Fréart de Chambray, the French diplomat and art theorist who translated Leonardo's work into French.</note>, a gentleman most intelligent in all parts of design and who (as we said of the great Pope Leo X) by an instinct shared by his family delights in every sort of virtue and study, has made a version of it in the French language, which is worth an entire commentary, having expressed the sense of the author with exquisite and successful diligence.*

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, manuscripts, mirror writing, Martesana canal, anatomy, perspective, Ambrosian Library, Cardinal Borromeo, Trattato della Pittura, Roland Fréart de Chambray, Paris 1651 edition</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the bibliographic catalog of art-theoretical texts mentioned on the previous page to the actual text of one of the most foundational manuals in art history: Leonardo da Vinci’s Treatise on Painting. This version represents the standard organized structure of Leonardo’s notes, which were compiled posthumously to provide a systematic curriculum for aspiring artists.</meta>

## TREATISE
# ON PAINTING
### BY LEONARDO
## DA VINCI.

---

#### *What the young student should learn first.*
#### C H A P T E R  O N E.

The young student <note>Leonardo uses the term *giovane* (youth/young man) to refer to the apprentice or student entering a workshop.</note> must first learn perspective, for the measurements of all things: then, step by step, learn from a good master to grow accustomed to well-proportioned limbs; then from nature, to confirm the underlying principles of the things learned; then for a time view the works by the hands of various masters, to develop the habit of putting into practice and applying the things learned.

---

#### *What the study of the young should be.* C H A P. II.

The study of the young, who desire to make progress in the sciences that imitate all the forms of the works of nature, should be focused on drawing <note>Original: *disegno*. This term refers to both the physical act of drawing and the intellectual concept of design.</note>, accompanied by the shadows and lights appropriate to the location where such figures are placed.

---

#### *What rule should be given to young painters.* C H A P. III.

WE clearly know that sight is among the fastest operations that exist, and in one instant it sees infinite forms; nonetheless, it understands only one thing at a time. Let us take a case: You, the reader, look at this entire written page in a single glance; you will immediately judge it to be full of various letters: but you will not know in that time what letters they are, nor what they mean: therefore you must go word by word, line by line, if you wish to gain knowledge of these letters. Again, if you want to climb to the height of a building, you must ascend step by step, otherwise it would be impossible to reach its height.

<summary>This page serves as the opening to Leonardo da Vinci’s Treatise on Painting, outlining a structured pedagogical approach for art students that emphasizes perspective, drawing from nature, and gradual, step-by-step learning.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Treatise on Painting, perspective, drawing, art education, light and shadow, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s foundational advice for the apprentice, moving from the initial steps of perspective and proportion into the psychological and philosophical requirements of being a "universal" artist. It contains the famous anecdote regarding Botticelli and the role of chance in inspiration.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

And so I say to you, whom nature turns toward this art: if you wish to have a true knowledge of the forms of things, you shall begin with their individual parts, and do not move on to the second part until you have the first well-fixed in your memory and in your practice. If you do otherwise, you will waste your time, or truly, you will greatly prolong your studies. and I remind you to learn diligence before speed.

### *Recognition of the youth inclined toward painting. Chap. IV.*

**M**ANY men have a desire and love for drawing <note>Original: *disegno*. In the Renaissance, this meant both the physical act of drawing and the intellectual capacity for design.</note>, but no natural disposition for it. This can be recognized in children who lack diligence and never finish their works with proper shading.

### *Precept for the painter. Chap. V.*

**T**HAT painter is not praiseworthy who does only one thing well, such as a nude, a head, draperies, animals, landscapes, or similar specialties; for there is no mind so dull that, by turning itself to one thing and constantly practicing it, it cannot eventually do it well.

### *How the youth should proceed in his study. Chap. VI.*

**T**HE mind of the painter must continually transform itself into as many discourses as there are figures of notable objects appearing before him. He should stop at these, observe them, and derive rules from them, considering the location, the circumstances, the lights, and the shadows.

### *On the manner of study. Chap. VII.*

**S**TUDY the science <note>Leonardo uses "science" (*scienza*) to mean the underlying principles and laws of nature, such as optics and anatomy.</note> first, and then follow the practice born from that science. The painter must study with a system, leaving nothing that is not committed to memory, and seeing what difference exists between the limbs of animals and their joints.

### *Advice to the painter. Chap. VIII.*

**T**HE painter must be universal and solitary; he must consider what he sees and speak with himself, choosing the most excellent parts of the species of whatever thing he sees. He should act like a mirror, which transforms itself into as many colors as are placed before it; by doing this, he will seem to be a second nature.

### *Precept of the universal painter. Chap. IX.*

**H**E is not universal who does not love equally all the things contained in painting. For instance, if one likes only landscapes, he considers his work to be a simple investigation; just as our Botticello <note>A diminutive of Botticelli (Sandro Botticelli). Leonardo is referencing a contemporary debate on the value of landscape painting.</note> said that such study was vain, because by merely throwing a sponge full of various colors against a wall, it would leave a stain where one could see a landscape. It is indeed true that various inventions can be seen in such a stain—whatever a man chooses to seek within it, such as human heads, diverse animals, battles, rocks, seas, clouds, woods, and similar things. It acts like the sound of bells, which...

<summary>Leonardo argues that a true painter must be "universal," mastering all subjects rather than specializing, and insists that artistic practice must be built upon a foundation of scientific principles.</summary>
<keywords>artistic education, diligence, universal painter, Botticelli, landscape painting, science and practice, observation</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s advice on artistic inspiration and the psychological discipline of the painter. It follows the discussion of using accidental marks—like stains on walls—to stimulate the imagination, while cautioning against relying on such tricks for finished work.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

...one can understand it to say whatever you like. Thus, even if those stains provide you with invention <note>In Renaissance art theory, *invention* (invenzione) refers to the initial creative idea or composition of a work.</note>, they do not teach you how to finish any particular detail; and such a painter <note>Leonardo is likely referring to Sandro Botticelli, whom he criticized on the previous page for claiming one could create a landscape simply by throwing a sponge soaked in colors at a wall.</note> produced very dismal landscapes.

## *How the painter must be universal.* Cap. X.

You, painter, who wish to be universal and please diverse judgments, shall ensure that in a single composition there are things of great darkness and of great sweetness of shadow, while nonetheless making known the causes of such shadows and sweetness. <term>Universal: Leonardo’s ideal artist was a polymath who understood all subjects of nature—anatomy, botany, geology, and optics—rather than specializing in just one.</term>

## *Precept for the painter.* Cap. XI.

THAT painter who does not doubt achieves little. When the work surpasses the judgment of the artist, that artist gains little; but when the judgment surpasses the work, that work never ceases to improve, unless greed prevents it.

## *Precept as above.* Cap. XII.

THE painter must first accustom his hand by copying drawings by good masters, and once this habit is formed, under the judgment of his teacher, he must then accustom himself to copying good objects in relief, following those rules that will be stated regarding drawing from relief. <term>Relief: Three-dimensional models, such as sculptures or casts, used to teach how light falls on solid forms.</term>

## *Precept for sketching narratives and figures.* Cap. XIII.

THE sketching of narrative scenes <note>Original: *historie*. In this era, the "history painting"—depicting a story from the Bible, mythology, or history—was considered the highest form of art.</note> should be quick, and the arrangement of limbs not too finished. Pay attention only to the positions of those limbs, which you can then finish at your leisure as you please.

## *On correcting the errors you discover.* Cap. XIV.

I REMIND you, painter, that when by your own judgment or by another’s advice you discover any error in your works, you should correct it, so that in publishing such works, you do not publish your foolishness <note>Original: *matteria*. Here used to mean "folly" or "lack of sense."</note> along with them. And do not excuse yourself by persuading yourself that you will restore your reputation in your next work; for painting does not die upon its creation, as music does, but lasts a long time, and time will bear witness to your ignorance. And if you excuse yourself by saying you must struggle with necessity and lack the time to study and make yourself a true painter, blame no one but yourself; for only the study of virtue is food for both the soul and the body. How many philosophers were born rich and, so that riches would not hinder them, gave them away?

## *On judgment.* Cap. XV.

NOTHING deceives us more than our own judgment when passing sentence on our own works; the criticisms of enemies will be more useful to you than the opinions of friends, for friends are as one with you, and thus can deceive you with your own judgment.

<sig>A ij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo advises painters to be "universal" in their mastery and stresses that an artist's critical judgment must always stay ahead of their manual skill to ensure constant improvement. He also warns that because painting is a permanent medium, errors will leave a lasting testimony to a painter's ignorance, unlike the fleeting nature of music.</summary>
<keywords>universality, artistic judgment, sketching, narrative scenes, correcting errors, permanence of painting, relief drawing, discipline</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s practical advice for the artist’s mental preparation. Having previously cautioned against relying solely on accidental marks for finished work, he now explains how those same marks can be used as a psychological tool to trigger creativity, while emphasizing the rigorous need for observation from nature and the mastery of light and shadow.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

## *A way to awaken the intellect to various inventions.* Chap. XVI.

I shall not omit to include among these precepts a new method of speculation, which, although it may seem small and almost worthy of laughter, is nonetheless of great utility in awakening the intellect to various inventions. And it is this: If you look at certain walls stained with dampness, or stones of various mixtures, you may be able to see there the invention and likeness of diverse landscapes, various battles, swift actions of figures, strange expressions of faces, and costumes, and infinite other things; because in confused things, the intellect is awakened to new inventions. <note>Leonardo is describing a form of pareidolia—the tendency to see meaningful patterns in random data—as a creative spark for "invention," the Renaissance term for original composition.</note>

## *On studying even when you wake up, or before you fall asleep in the dark.* Chap. XVII.

Furthermore, I have found it to be of no small utility, when you find yourself in the dark in bed, to go over in the imagination the superficial outlines of the forms previously studied, or other notable things of subtle speculation; and in this way, the things held in the memory are confirmed.

## *That one must first learn diligence before quick practice.* Chap. XVIII.

<margin>*</margin> When you wish to undertake good and useful study, use a slow pace in your drawing, and judge among the lights which ones and how many hold the first degree of brightness; and likewise among the shadows, which are those that are darker than others, and in what way they blend together, and their quality. Compare one with another: consider where the outlines are directed, and in the lines, how much of the part should be in one direction or the other, and where it is more or less evident, and likewise wide or thin. And finally, ensure that your shadows and lights are united without strokes or marks, in the manner of smoke; <term>Sfumato: From the Italian "fumo" (smoke). This is Leonardo’s signature technique of softening the transition between colors and tones to mimic the way light actually behaves in nature, avoiding harsh outlines.</term> and when you have accustomed your hand and habit to that diligence, the quick practice will come to you so naturally that you will not even notice it.

## *How the painter should be eager to hear the judgment of everyone.* Chap. XIX.

Certainly, a painter should not refuse, while he draws or paints, the judgment of anyone; for we know that a man, even if he is not a painter, will have knowledge of the forms of a man—whether he is a hunchback, if he has a thick leg or a large hand, if he is lame, or has other failings. And if we know that men can judge the works of nature, how much more will they be able to judge our errors.

## *That a man should not trust himself so much that he does not look at nature.* Chap. XX.

He who leads himself to believe that he can retain within himself all the effects of nature is deceived, because our memory is not of such great capacity; therefore, you shall see everything from nature. <note>original: "dal naturale." This refers to drawing from life rather than from imagination or from other drawings.</note>

## *On the variety of figures.* Chap. XXI.

The painter must seek to be universal, because he lacks much dignity if he does one thing well and another poorly; like many who only study the...

<summary>Leonardo explains how random patterns can spark artistic "invention" and emphasizes the importance of mental rehearsal, the "sfumato" technique, and the necessity of constant observation from nature.</summary>
<keywords>pareidolia, invention, memory, sfumato, light and shadow, anatomy, universal painter, nature, artistic discipline</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page concludes Leonardo’s advice on the diversity of the human form, warning against repetitive, "stamped out" figures. It then transitions into foundational principles for the artist: the necessity of being "universal" in one's skills, the danger of practice without scientific theory (specifically perspective), and the imperative to study nature directly rather than imitating the styles of other masters.</meta>

->OF LEONARDO DA VINCI.<-

...nude, measured, and proportioned, and they do not seek its variety; for a man may be well-proportioned and yet be fat and short, or tall and thin, or of medium build. He who does not take this variety into account always makes his figures <term>in stampa</term> <note>original: "in stampa"; literally "in print," meaning repetitive or standardized as if produced from a single mold</note>, which deserves great reproach.

### *On being universal.* CHAP. XXII.

It is an easy thing for the man who understands to make himself universal, because all terrestrial animals have a similarity of members—namely muscles, nerves, and bones—and they do not vary except in length or thickness, as will be demonstrated in the [treatise on] anatomy. As for the animals of the water, which are of great variety, I will not persuade the painter to establish a rule for them.

### *On those who use practice without diligence, or science.* CHAP. XXIII.

Those who fall in love with practice without diligence, or science—to put it better—are like pilots who take to the sea in a ship without a rudder or compass, who never have certainty of where they are going. Practice must always be built upon good <term>theory</term>, of which <term>perspective</term> is the guide and the gateway; and without that, nothing is done well, neither in painting nor in any other profession.

### *On not imitating another painter.* CHAP. XXIV.

A painter should never imitate the manner of another, because he would be called the grandson and not the son of nature; for since natural things are in such vast abundance, one should rather recur to nature herself than to the masters who have learned from her.

### *On drawing from nature.* CHAP. XXV.

When you have to draw from nature, stand at a distance three times the size of the object you are drawing; and ensure that when you draw, or when you begin any line, you look through the whole body you are drawing to see what features align with the direction of your principal line.

### *Warning to the painter.* CHAP. XXVI.

Note well in your drawing how, among the shadows, there are imperceptible shadows of darkness and of shape. This is proven by the third [proposition], which says that globular surfaces have as many varieties of darkness and light as there are varieties of darkness and light in the objects facing them. <note>Leonardo likely refers here to his own scientific propositions regarding optics and the reflection of light between objects.</note>

### *How high the light should be for drawing from nature.* CHAP. XXVII.

The light for drawing from nature should be from the north, so that it does not change; and if you use a southern light, keep the windows <term>impannate</term> <note>windows covered with cloth or parchment to diffuse the direct rays of the sun</note>, so that the sun, shining all day, does not cause the light to shift. The height of the light must be situated in such a way that every object casts a shadow on the ground as long as the object is high.

<sig>A iij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo emphasizes that true mastery requires "universal" knowledge of anatomy and a firm foundation in the "science" of perspective, rather than mere repetitive practice or the imitation of other artists' styles.</summary>
<keywords>universality, anatomy, perspective, theory vs practice, imitation of nature, lighting conditions, northern light, shadows</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s technical advice for painters, shifting from the general philosophy of universal skill to specific observations on light, atmosphere, and the mechanics of the human figure. It follows the previous page's warning against repetitive, "stamped" figures by providing the scientific basis for achieving variety and realism through the study of light and anatomy.</meta>

# TREATISE ON PAINTING

## *What light should be chosen for portraying the figures of bodies.*
### CHAP. XXVIII.

**T**he figures of any body are constrained to take that light in which you imagine those figures to be: that is, if you imagine such figures in the open countryside, they are surrounded by a great abundance of light when the sun is not visible; and if the sun shines upon said figures, their shadows will be very dark compared to the illuminated parts, and will create shadows with sharp boundaries—both the <term>primary shadows</term> <note>Shadows located on the object itself</note> and the <term>derived shadows</term> <note>Shadows cast by the object onto another surface</note>. Such shadows will harmonize little with the lights, because from that side the blue of the air provides illumination and tints the part it reaches with its own color; this is quite evident in white objects. That part which is illuminated by the sun will show itself to participate in the color of the sun; you will see this very clearly when the sun sets in the west amidst the redness of the clouds, so that those clouds are tinted with the color that illuminates them. This redness of the clouds, together with the redness of the sun, makes everything that receives light from them turn reddish; meanwhile, the part of the bodies that does not see this redness remains the color of the air. Anyone seeing such bodies would judge them to be of two colors. You cannot escape this: once the cause of such shadows and lights is shown, you must make them participate in these causes, otherwise your work is vain and false. And if your figure is in a dark house and you see it from outside, such a figure will have <term>sfumato</term> <note>original: "sfumate"; a technique of blending tones so subtly that they transition like smoke, without perceptible edges</note> shadows when you stand in the line of the light. Such a figure will have grace and bring honor to its artist by being of great relief, with soft and smoky shadows, especially in those parts where you see the darkness of the dwelling the least; for there the shadows are almost imperceptible, and the reason for this will be explained in its proper place.

## *On the quality of light for portraying reliefs, natural or artificial.*
### CHAP. XXIX.

**L**ight that is cut off from shadows with too much starkness is highly criticized. To avoid such an inconvenience, if you depict bodies in the open countryside, do not make the figures illuminated by the direct sun, but imagine some quantity of mist or transparent clouds being interposed between the object and the sun. Since the figure is not hit by direct sunlight, the boundaries of the shadows will not be sharply defined against the lights.

## *On drawing nudes.*
### CHAP. XXX.

**W**hen you draw nudes, ensure that you always draw them whole, and then finish that limb which seems best to you, putting it into practice with the other members; otherwise, you would develop a habit of never joining the limbs together well. Never make the head turned in the same direction as the chest, nor the arm moving like the leg. If the head turns toward the right shoulder, make its parts lower on the left side than on the other; and if you make the chest project outward, make it so that if the head turns to the left side, the parts on the right side are higher than those on the left.

<summary>Leonardo explains how environmental light—such as golden hour or overcast skies—dictates the color and softness of shadows, and provides rules for "contrapposto" in figure drawing to ensure natural movement.</summary>
<keywords>lighting, shadows, sunset, sfumato, outdoor light, anatomy, nude drawing, contrapposto, artistic theory</keywords>

## BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.

### *On drawing from artificial relief or from nature.* CHAP. XXXI.

Whoever draws from a relief <note>Refers to drawing from a three-dimensional model or sculpture rather than a flat image</note> should position themselves so that the eye of the figure being depicted is at the same level as the eye of the artist.

### *A method for accurately drawing a site.* CHAP. XXXII.

Take a pane of glass as large as half a sheet of royal paper <note>A standard large paper size of the era</note> and fix it firmly before your eyes—that is, between your eyes and the object you wish to depict. Then, position your eye two-thirds of a <term>braccio</term> <note>An "arm's length," roughly 58 centimeters or 23 inches</note> away from said glass, and steady your head with a device so that you cannot move it at all. Then, close or cover one eye, and with a brush or a stylus, mark onto the glass what appears on the other side. Then trace that glass with paper, and after <term>pouncing</term> <note>original: "spoluerizzandola" — a technique where the lines of a drawing are pricked with holes and charcoal dust is rubbed through onto a new surface</note> it onto a high-quality sheet of paper, paint it if you wish, making good use of <term>aerial perspective</term> <note>The technique of representing distance by making distant objects paler, bluer, and less distinct</note> afterward.

### *How landscapes should be portrayed.* CHAP. XXXIII.

Landscapes should be portrayed in such a way that the trees are half-illuminated and half-shaded. However, it is better to depict them when the sun is partially obscured by clouds; for then the trees are illuminated by the universal light of the sky and the universal shadow of the earth, and these are darker in their parts the closer those parts are to the ground.

### *On drawing by candlelight.* CHAP. XXXIV.

When using this light at night, a frame or tracing paper should be placed in between—or even without tracing it, just a sheet of thin <term>chancery paper</term> <note>original: "carta sottile cancellaresca" — a high-quality, thin paper used for official documents</note>—and you will see that your shadows lose their harsh edges.

### *In what way one should draw a face and give it grace, shadow, and light.*
### CHAP. XXXV.

A profound grace of shadows and lights is added to the faces of those who sit in the darker parts of dwellings. The eyes of the observer see the shaded side of such a face darkened by the shadows of the aforementioned dwelling, while seeing the illuminated side of that same face enhanced by the brightness given to it by the splendor of the air. Because of this intensification of shadows and lights, the face gains great <term>relief</term> <note>A sculptural, three-dimensional quality</note>, and on the illuminated side, the shadows are almost imperceptible. Through this representation and intensification of shadows and lights, the face acquires a great deal of beauty.

### *On the light for portraying the flesh tones of faces and nudes.*
### CHAP. XXXVI.

This workspace should be open to the air, with the walls colored a flesh-tone. Portraits should be made in summer when clouds cover the sun; or indeed, you should make the southern walls high enough so that the sun's rays do not strike the northern walls, so that the reflected rays do not spoil the shadows.

<summary>Leonardo provides technical advice on using glass panes for accurate perspective, the best atmospheric conditions for landscapes, and how to use indoor/outdoor lighting to give human faces a soft, "graceful" three-dimensional quality.</summary>
<keywords>perspective glass, landscape painting, candlelight, chiaroscuro, facial grace, workspace setup, relief, skin tones</keywords>
