### *On drawing figures for narrative paintings.* <note>original: "historie" — complex compositions depicting a specific event or story</note> Cap. XXXVII.

**T**he painter must always consider, on the wall which he is to paint with a <term>narrative</term>, the height of the site where he wishes to place his figures. What he draws from nature for this purpose must be viewed from an eye level as much lower than the object being drawn as the object itself will be placed higher than the eye of the spectator; otherwise, his work will be worthy of reproach.

### *For drawing a nude from life, or other things.* Cap. XXXVIII.

**A**ccustom yourself to holding in your hand a <term>plumb line</term> <note>A string with a lead weight used to determine a perfectly vertical line</note> to see how different parts of the body align with one another.

### *Measurements and divisions of the statue.* Cap. XXXIX.

**D**ivide the head into twelve degrees, and each degree divide into 12 points, and each point into 12 minutes, and the minutes into <term>minimi</term>, and the <term>minimi</term> into <term>semiminimi</term>. <note>Leonardo is proposing a highly precise duodecimal system (base-12) for measuring human proportions, allowing for microscopic accuracy in his anatomical studies.</note>

### *How the painter should position himself relative to the light and his model.* Cap. XL.

**A. B.** shall be the window. **M.** shall be the point of the light. I say that in whatever position the painter stands, he will be well-placed, provided that his eye stays between the shaded part and the illuminated part of the body he is drawing; you will find this place by positioning yourself between point **M.** and the line that divides the shadow from the light upon the body being portrayed.

<margin>chap. 40</margin>

![Perspective and lighting diagram showing light from a window A. B. passing through point M. to illuminate a sphere.](diagram)

### *On the quality of light.* Cap. XLI.

**A** large and high light, and one that is not too powerful, is what will make the details of bodies appear very pleasing. <note>Leonardo frequently advocated for "soft" lighting, such as that found in courtyards with walls painted black or during overcast days, to create his famous smoky transition between light and shadow (sfumato).</note>

### *On the error made in the judgment of limbs.* Cap. XLII.

**T**hat painter who has clumsy hands will make them similar in his works, and so <note>This refers to Leonardo's theory that artists have a natural tendency to paint figures that resemble themselves—both in their beauties and their physical defects—unless they are consciously aware of the bias.</note>

...will happen to him in any limb, unless long study forbids it. Therefore, every painter should observe which part of his own person is most unattractive, and with every effort, take great care to guard against it <note>Leonardo frequently warned that painters unconsciously paint figures that look like themselves, including their own physical defects.</note> [in his work].

---

### *That one must know the internal form of man.* Cap. XLIII.

**T**hat painter who has knowledge of the nature of the nerves, muscles, and <term>tendons</term> <note>original: "lacerti" — refers to the fleshy part of the muscles or the bundles of muscle fibers</note> will know well, when moving a limb, how many and which nerves are the cause of it, and which muscle, by swelling, is the cause of the shortening of that nerve; and which cords, turned into very fine cartilage, surround and encase said muscle. He will not do as many others who, in different poses, always show those same features in the arms, backs, chests, and other muscles.

---

### *On the painter's defect.* Cap. XLIV.

**I**t is a very great defect in a painter to repeat or replicate the same movements and the same folds of clothing in a single <term>narrative</term> <note>original: "historia" — a complex composition depicting a specific event or story</note>, and to make all the heads resemble one another.

---

### *Precept, so that the painter is not deceived in the selection of the figure he portrays.* Cap. XLV.

**T**he painter must create his figure according to the rule of a natural body which is commonly of laudable proportion. In addition to this, he should have himself measured and see in which part his own person varies much or little from that aforementioned laudable standard. Once he has gained that knowledge, he must strive with all his study to avoid falling into the same failings in the figures he creates that he finds in his own person. You must fight against this vice with the utmost strength, since it is a failing born together with <term>judgment</term> <note>original: "giuditio" — here meaning the artist's innate aesthetic sense or critical faculty</note>. For the soul is the master of your body <note>Original text has "crpo", a typo for "corpo" (body).</note> and that of your own judgment, and she naturally delights in works similar to those she performed in composing your own body. From this arises the fact that there is no woman so unattractive that she does not find some lover—unless she is truly monstrous—and in all this, you should pay the greatest attention.

---

### *The defect of painters who portray a thing in relief indoors by one light, and then place it in a landscape by another light.* Cap. XLVI.

**I**t is a great error for those painters who portray a thing in <term>relief</term> <note>A three-dimensional object or model</note> by a particular light in their houses, and then place such a portrait into a work with the universal light of the open air in the countryside—where such air embraces and illuminates all parts of the view in the same way. In doing so, the painter creates dark shadows where there can be no shadow; or if there is one, it is of such clarity that it is imperceptible. In the same way, they create reflections where it is impossible for them to be seen.

---

### *On painting, and its division.* Cap. XLVII.

**P**ainting is divided into two principal parts, the first of which is...

...figure, that is, the line that distinguishes the shape of bodies and their individual parts; the second is the color contained within those boundaries.

### Figure, and its division. CAP. XLVIII.

**T**he figure of bodies is divided into two other parts: the proportionality of the parts to one another, which must correspond to the whole, and the movement appropriate to the <term>mental state</term> <note>original: "accidente mentale" — Leonardo's term for the internal thoughts or emotions that cause a physical reaction.</note> of the living thing that moves.

### Proportion of limbs. CAP. XLIX.

**T**he proportion of the limbs is divided into two other parts: equality and motion. By equality, it is meant—in addition to measurements corresponding to the whole—that you should not mix the limbs of young people with those of the old, nor those of the fat with those of the thin, nor graceful limbs with those that are clumsy and sluggish. Furthermore, do not give masculine bodies feminine limbs, ensuring that the attitudes or movements of the elderly are not rendered with the same vitality as those of the young, nor those of a woman like those of a man. Ensure that the movements and limbs of a strong person are such that they demonstrate their physical health in those very limbs.

### On movements, and various actions. CAP. L.

**T**he figures of men should have actions suited to their activity, so that in seeing them, you understand what they are thinking or saying. These will be well-learned by anyone who imitates the movements of the <term>mute</term> <note>original: "mutoli" — Leonardo often recommended observing the deaf and those unable to speak, as they communicate purely through physical gesture.</note>, who speak with movements of their hands, eyes, eyebrows, and their whole person when they wish to express the concept of their mind. Do not laugh at me because I propose a teacher without a tongue to teach you an art he himself cannot do; for he will teach you better through deeds than all others will with words. Therefore, you, painter of one kind or another, pay attention—according to what is required—to the quality of those who speak and to the nature of the thing being discussed.

### That sharp boundaries must be avoided. CAP. LI.

**D**o not make the boundaries of your figures a different color than the background itself against which those figures end; that is, do not make dark profiles between the background and your figure <note>This is a foundational principle of Leonardo’s *sfumato* technique, where edges are blurred into the atmosphere rather than sharply outlined.</note>.

### That in small things errors are not seen as they are in large ones. CAP. LII.

**I**n things of a small form, one cannot comprehend the quality of your error as easily as in large ones. The reason is that if this small thing is made in the likeness of a man or another animal, its parts—due to the immense reduction—cannot be scrutinized with that <term>proper finish</term> <note>original: "debito fine" — the level of detail and refinement required in a completed work.</note> from the artist that would be appropriate. Thus, the work not being finished, you cannot comprehend its errors. For example, you might look at a man from a distance of 300 <term>braccia</term> <note>original: "braccia" — a Florentine unit of measure, approximately 23 inches or 58 centimeters.</note>, and you will judge with difficulty whether he is handsome or ugly, or if he is monstrous or of common quality; you will see that even with your greatest effort, you will not be able to...

He also warns against using harsh outlines and explains how distance or small scale can hide technical flaws.</summary>

...persuade one to make such a judgment; and the reason is that, because of the aforementioned distance, this man diminishes so much that the quality of his parts cannot be understood. And if you wish to see clearly this diminution of the man mentioned above, place a finger near your eye at a distance of a <term>palm</term> <note>A "palmo" was a traditional unit of measure based on the width or length of a hand, roughly 8-10 inches.</note>, and raise and lower that finger until its upper tip ends below the figure you are looking at, and you will see an incredible diminution appear. For this reason, one often doubts the identity of a friend seen from afar.

### *Why painting can never appear as standing out in relief, as natural objects do.*
### CHAPTER LIII.

<margin>See chapter 351.</margin>
Painters often fall into despair regarding their imitation of nature, seeing that their paintings do not have that <term>relief</term> <note>original: "rilievo" — the illusion of three-dimensional depth on a flat surface.</note> and that vitality which objects seen in a mirror have. They claim that they have colors which far exceed, in brightness and darkness, the quality of the lights and shadows of the object seen in the mirror, blaming their own ignorance in this case, and not the underlying reason, because they do not know it. It is impossible for a painted thing to appear with such relief that it resembles objects in a mirror—although both are on a flat surface—unless it is seen with only one eye. And the reason is this: Two eyes see one object behind another, like A and B seeing M and N; M cannot entirely hide N, because the base of the <term>visual lines</term> <note>original: "linee visuali" — the lines of sight extending from the eyes to the object.</note> is so wide that it sees the second body behind the first. But if you close one eye, like S, the body F will hide R, because the visual line originates from a single point and creates its base on the first body, whereby the second body of equal size will never be seen.

### *Why the arrangement of figures one above the other is a thing to be avoided.*
### CHAPTER LIV.

**T**HIS universal practice which is used by painters on the walls of chapels is very much to be reasonably blamed, because they make...

<meta>This page follows Leonardo’s discussion on why paintings cannot perfectly mimic the three-dimensional depth (relief) seen in mirrors or natural life. Here, he moves into the practical application of perspective in narrative cycles and the specific technical qualities of light, anatomy, and gesture.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

...[placing] a narrative scene on a plane with its landscape and buildings; then they rise another level and make another narrative, changing the <term>point</term> <note>original: "punto" — the vanishing point in perspective, where parallel lines appear to converge.</note> from the first, and then a third and fourth, so that a single wall is seen executed with four vanishing points. This is the height of folly for such masters. We know that the point is placed at the eye of the observer of the narrative. And if you should ask: "How am I to depict the life of a saint divided into many narrative scenes on the same wall?" To this I answer that you should place the first plane with the vanishing point at the height of the eye of the observers of said narrative; on this plane, depict the first narrative scene at a large scale. Then, by gradually diminishing the figures and buildings upon various hills and plains, you will create the entire setting for that story. In the rest of the wall, in its upper portion, you shall make trees that are large in comparison to the [background] figures, or angels if they suit the narrative, or birds, clouds, or similar things. Otherwise, do not bother yourself with it, for all your work will be false.

### *What kind of painting should be used to make things appear more prominent.* Cap. LV.

Figures illuminated by a <term>particular light</term> <note>original: "lume particolare" — light coming from a single, specific source like a window or a candle, as opposed to diffuse "universal" light.</note> show more <term>relief</term> <note>original: "rilievo" — the illusion of three-dimensional depth.</note> than those illuminated by universal light. This is because particular light creates reflected lights, which make the figures stand out from their backgrounds. These reflections are born from the light of one figure bouncing into the shadow of the figure standing in front of it, illuminating it in part. But a figure placed before a particular light in a large, dark space receives no reflection, and only the illuminated part is visible. This [technique] is only to be used in imitations of the night, using a small, particular light.

### *Which requires more reasoning and utility: light and shadow, or the outlines of bodies.* Cap. LVI.

The boundaries of bodies require more reasoning and ingenuity than shadows and lights. This is because the <term>lineaments</term> <note>The outlines or skeletal/muscular contours.</note> of the limbs, which are not flexible, are immutable and always remain the same, whereas the locations, quality, and quantity of shadows are infinite.

### *Notes to be made by the author.* Cap. LVII.

Describe which are the muscles and which are the <term>cords</term> <note>original: "corde" — here referring to tendons or sinews.</note> that are revealed or hidden by the various movements of each limb, or those that do neither. And remember that such an action is of the utmost importance among painters and sculptors who make a profession of studying muscles. Do the same for a child, from his birth until the time of his old age, through all the stages of his life; in all of them, you shall describe the changes of the limbs and joints, and which ones grow fat or grow thin.

### *Precepts of painting.* Cap. LVIII.

A painter should always seek out the readiness of natural gestures made by men spontaneously, born from the powerful influence of their emotions. He should make brief notes of these in his little books and then use them for his purposes.

<summary>Leonardo criticizes the use of multiple vanishing points on a single wall and offers rules for organizing narrative cycles, while also emphasizing the importance of particular light for relief and the study of anatomical changes over a lifetime.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, vanishing point, narrative scenes (historia), relief (rilievo), particular light, reflected light, anatomy, muscles, life stages, spontaneous gesture, sketching</keywords>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

...by having a man remain in that same pose, to observe the quality and appearance of the limbs that are used in such an action.

### *How a painting must be viewed from a single window. Cap. LIX.*

<term>Painting</term> <note>original: "La pittura"</note> must be viewed from a single window <note>Leonardo uses "window" here as a metaphor for a single, fixed viewpoint or vanishing point.</note>, as is evident from the nature of objects made in this way. And if you wish to depict a round ball at a great height, you must make it elongated, similar to an egg, and stand far enough back that, through <term>foreshortening</term>, it appears round.

### *On shadows. Cap. LX.*

The <term>shadows</term> <note>original: "L'ombre"</note> which you discern with difficulty, and whose boundaries you cannot recognize—but rather perceive and transfer into your work with a blurred judgment—you must not make finished or truly defined, so that your work may possess a <term>clever resolution</term> <note>original: "ingegnosa risolutione" — likely referring to "sfumato," Leonardo’s technique of soft, smoky transitions between colors and tones.</note>.

### *How children should be depicted. Cap. LXI.*

Little <term>children</term> <note>original: "putti" — often used in art to refer to the winged infants or cherubs common in Renaissance painting.</note> should be depicted with quick and twisting gestures when they sit; and when standing, with shy and fearful gestures.

### *How old men should be depicted. Cap. LXII.*

<term>Old men</term> should be rendered with sluggish and slow movements, their legs bent at the knees when they stand still; their feet should be parallel and spaced apart, their bodies stooped downward, the head leaning forward, and the arms not too widely extended.

### *How old women should be depicted. Cap. LXIII.*

<term>Old women</term> should be depicted as bold and spirited, with angry movements like infernal furies; their movements should appear quicker in the arms and head than in the legs.

### *How women should be depicted. Cap. LXIV.*

<term>Women</term> should be depicted with modest gestures, their legs pulled close together, their arms gathered together, and their heads low and tilted to the side.

### *How a night scene should be depicted. Cap. LXV.*

That which is entirely deprived of light is total darkness. Since <term>night</term> is in such a condition, if you wish to depict a narrative scene within it, you must arrange it so that there is a great fire; then, that thing which is closest to said fire will be most deeply tinged with its color, because the closer an object is to a source, the more it participates in its nature. And since fire tends toward a red color, you shall make everything illuminated by it appear reddish; and those things further from the fire should be more tinted by the black color of the night. The figures placed in front of the fire will appear dark against its brightness, because the part of the object you see is colored by the darkness of the night and not the brightness of the fire; those figures at the sides should be half-dark.

<sig>B iij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo outlines the "decorum" of painting, detailing how to use a single viewpoint for perspective and how to characterize different figures—such as the shy child, the sluggish old man, or the modest woman—through their physical posture. He also provides technical instructions for painting night scenes using the dramatic contrast of firelight, a precursor to the chiaroscuro style.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, foreshortening, sfumato, shadows, putti, decorum of gestures, night scenes, firelight, chiaroscuro, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

and half-reddish; and those things that can be seen beyond the edges of the flame will all be illuminated by a reddish light against a black background. As for the gestures, you should make those who are nearby shield themselves with their hands and with their cloaks to shelter from the excessive heat, and turned with their faces in the opposite direction, appearing to flee; those further away, you should make a great part of them shield their eyes with their hands, as they are pained by the excessive brightness.

### How one must depict a <term>tempest</term>. Cap. LXVI.
<note>original: "fortuna" — while this word usually means luck, in the context of Renaissance art and seafaring, it refers to a violent storm or gale.</note>

If you wish to well represent a storm, consider and weigh its effects carefully: when the wind, blowing over the surface of the sea or the land, moves and carries with it those things that are not firmly fixed to the general mass. To represent such a storm, you shall first show the clouds split and broken, driven in the direction of the wind, accompanied by sandy dust lifted from the seashores; and branches and leaves, lifted by the power of the wind, scattered through the air along with many other light objects. Show the trees and grasses bent to the ground, almost as if showing a desire to follow the course of the winds, with branches twisted out of their natural path and with their leaves ruffled and overturned. And the men who find themselves there, some fallen and wrapped in their clothes and the dust so that they are almost unrecognizable; and those who remain standing should be behind some tree, embracing it so that the wind does not drag them away. Others with their hands over their eyes because of the dust, crouched on the ground, with their clothes and hair streaming in the direction of the wind. The sea, disturbed and stormy, should be full of swirling foam among the high waves, and the wind should lift the finest foam into the embattled air, like a thick and tangled mist. Of the ships that are within, show some with torn sails, with fragments of them fluttering in the air along with some broken ropes; others with broken masts fallen, and the ship itself turned sideways and broken among the tempestuous waves, with men crying out as they cling to what remains of the vessel. You shall show the clouds driven by impetuous winds, striking the high peaks of the mountains, and show them tangled and twisted like waves striking the rocks. The air should be terrifying because of the dark gloom created by the dust, mist, and thick clouds.

### How one must depict a battle. Cap. LXVII.

YOU SHALL first show the smoke of the <term>artillery</term> <note>original: "artiglieria" — Leonardo was an accomplished military engineer and often sketched the effects of gunpowder weapons.</note> mixed into the air along with the dust raised by the movement of the combatants' horses. This mixture you shall use as follows: since dust is a terrestrial and heavy thing, and although because of its fineness it is easily raised and mixed into the air, it nonetheless tends to return downward, and its highest ascent is made by its finest part. Therefore, it should be seen least at its peak and will appear almost the color of the air. The smoke that mixes into the dust-filled air, when it then rises to a certain height, will look like dark clouds, and at the summit, the smoke will be seen more clearly than the dust. The smoke will lean toward a somewhat bluish color, while the dust will keep its own color. On the side from which the light comes, this mixture of air, smoke, and dust will appear much brighter than on the opposite side. The more the combatants are within this turbulence, the less they will be seen, and there will be less difference...

<summary>This page provides technical instructions for painters on how to realistically depict natural disasters—specifically storms—and the chaotic atmosphere of a battlefield. Leonardo emphasizes the physical behavior of elements like wind, dust, and smoke to create a convincing sense of motion and drama.</summary>

[receiving] light from their fires into their shadows. You shall make the faces, the figures, the air, and the <term>arquebusiers</term> <note>original: "archibugieri" — soldiers armed with an early type of portable long gun</note> glow red, along with those who are near them. And this redness, the further it moves from its source, the more it fades. The figures that are between you and the light, being far away, will appear dark against a light background; and the closer their legs are to the ground, the less they should be seen, because the dust there is thicker and denser. And if you depict horses running outside the throng, make small clouds of dust separated from one another by the distance of the horse’s strides; that cloud which is furthest from the horse should be the least visible—indeed, let it be high, scattered, and thin—while the one nearest should be the most evident, smaller, and more dense.

The air should be full of arrows flying in various directions: some rising, some descending, and some on a level line. The bullets of the <term>musketeers</term> <note>original: "scoppetieri" — soldiers using the 'scoppietto', a smaller early firearm</note> should be accompanied by a bit of smoke following their flight. The first figures [in the foreground] you shall make dusty in their hair, eyebrows, and other places where dust can settle. 

You shall make the victors running with their hair and other light things fluttering in the wind, with their brows lowered; they should thrust forward opposite limbs—that is, if they move the right foot forward, the left arm should also come forward. And if you depict someone who has fallen, you shall show the mark of his sliding through the dust, turned into bloody mud; and in the dampness of the earth, you shall show the imprinted footsteps of the men and horses that have passed by. You shall show some horses dragging their dead masters, leaving behind the mark of the dragged body in the dust and mud. 

You shall make the vanquished and beaten pale, with their brows raised and joined, and the skin above them should be full of sorrowful wrinkles. The sides of the nose should have several wrinkles forming an arc from the nostrils and ending at the beginning of the eye. The nostrils should be high, which causes these folds, and the arched lips should reveal the upper teeth. The teeth should be parted as if in a scream of lamentation. One of the hands should form a shield for the fearful eyes, turning the palm toward the enemy; the other should be on the ground, supporting the wounded torso. 

Others you shall make screaming with their mouths wide open, and fleeing. You shall show many kinds of weapons under the feet of the combatants, such as broken shields, spears, swords, and other similar things. You shall make dead men, some half-covered by dust, and others entirely. The dust that mixes with the flowing blood turns into red mud, and you shall see the blood of its natural color running in a winding course from the body into the dust. Others, as they die, should be clenching their teeth, rolling their eyes, pressing their fists to their bodies, and with their legs contorted. 

One might see someone disarmed and beaten down by the enemy, turning toward said enemy with bites and scratches, taking a cruel and bitter revenge. One could see a riderless, light horse running with its mane scattered to the wind among the enemies, doing great damage with its hooves. You might see some crippled man falling to the ground and making a cover of his shield, while the enemy, leaning low, struggles to deal him death. Many men could be seen fallen in a heap under a dead horse. You will see some victors leaving the fight and exiting the crowd, wiping their eyes and cheeks with their hands because they are covered in mud made from the watering of their eyes caused by the dust. You will see the reserve squadrons standing full of hope and suspicion, with their brows knit, shading their eyes with their hands and peering—

*On the method of depicting distant things in painting.* CHAPTER LXVIII.

**I**t is clearly seen that one layer of air is thicker <note>Leonardo uses the term "aria grossa" (thick or dense air) to describe the atmosphere near the ground which is filled with moisture and dust.</note> than another, specifically that which borders the level earth; and the higher it rises, the thinner and more transparent it is. For tall and large things that are far from you, their lower parts will be little seen, because you see them through a line that passes through the continuous thicker air. The summit of such heights is proven to be seen through a line which—although it originates from the side of your eye in the thick air—nevertheless, ending at the highest peak of the seen object, ends in air much thinner than that of its base. For this reason, the further this line moves from you from point to point, it always changes quality from thin to thinner air. Therefore, you, painter, when you make mountains, make sure that from hill to hill the heights are always lighter than the bases; and when you make them further from each other, make the heights lighter; and the higher a peak rises, the more it will show the variety of its form and color.

->*How the air should be made lighter the lower you depict it ending.*<-
->CHAPTER LXIX.<-

**B**ecause this air is dense near the earth, and the higher it rises, the thinner it becomes, when the sun is in the east and you look toward the west (looking toward the south or north), you will see that thick air receive more light from the sun than the thin air, because the rays find more resistance. And if the sky in your view ends at the low plain, that furthest part of the sky will be seen through that thicker and whiter air, which will distort the true color seen through its medium; the sky will appear whiter there than it does above you, because the visual line passes through a smaller quantity of air corrupted by thick vapors <note>original: "humori" — literally "humors," referring here to atmospheric moisture or mists</note>. And if you look toward the east, the air will appear darker to you the lower it goes, because in said low air the luminous rays pass through less.

*To make figures stand out from their background.* CHAPTER LXX.

**T**he figures of any body will appear to have more <term>relief</term> <note>original: "rilevare" — the illusion of three-dimensional depth on a flat surface</note> and stand out more from their backgrounds <note>original: "campi" — literally "fields," referring to the backdrop or negative space</note> when those backgrounds consist of light or dark colors with as much variety as possible at the boundaries of the aforementioned figures (as will be shown in its proper place), and when in said colors the decrease of lightness in the whites and of darkness in the dark colors is observed.

### *On depicting the sizes of painted things.* CHAPTER LXXI.

In the representation of the sizes that things placed before the eye naturally possess, the first figures <note>Leonardo refers to the "primary" figures in the foreground.</note> should be depicted just as finished—even when they are small, like the works of miniaturists <note>original: "miniatori"; these were artists who painted highly detailed illustrations in manuscripts.</note>—as the large works of painters. However, the small works of miniaturists must be viewed from up close, and those of the painter from a distance. In doing so, these figures should correspond to the eye with equal bulk; and this occurs because they arrive at the eye with an equal visual angle <note>The angle formed at the eye by the rays of light coming from the extremities of an object.</note>. This is proven as follows: let the object be **B.C.** and the eye be **A.**; let **D.E.** be a sheet of glass through which the species <note>original: "specie"; a technical term in Renaissance optics referring to the visual images or "simulacra" transmitted from an object to the eye.</note> of **B.C.** penetrate. I say that, with the eye **A.** remaining stationary, the size of the painting made in imitation of said **B.C.** must be a smaller figure in proportion to how much closer the glass **D.E.** is to the eye **A.**, and it must be equally finished. And if you finish that figure **B.C.** on the glass **D.E.**, your figure must be less finished than the [actual] figure **B.C.**, and more finished than the figure **M.N.** made on the glass **F.G.**; because if the figure **P.O.** were as finished as the natural **B.C.**, the perspective of said **O.P.** would be false. For while the diminution <note>The reduction in size due to distance.</note> of the figure would be correct—**B.C.** being diminished into **P.O.**—the finish would not agree with the distance. In seeking the perfection of the finish of the natural **B.C.**, then **B.C.** would appear to be at the close distance of **O.P.**; but if you seek the diminution of **O.P.**, then **O.P.** appears to be at the distance of **B.C.**, and in the diminishing of the finish at the glass **F.G.**

<note>The following diagram illustrates the eye at point A, projecting lines through various vertical planes (the "glass" mentioned in the text) to show how the perceived size of an object (B-C) changes depending on where the picture plane (D-E or F-G) intersects the visual pyramid.</note>

![A technical diagram of linear perspective illustrating visual rays emanating from an eye (point A) and passing through various vertical planes (F-G, D-E, B-C) to show how size and detail diminish with distance.](diagram_placeholder)

---

### *On finished things and blurred things.* CHAPTER LXXII.

Things that are finished and distinct should be placed nearby, and those that are blurred—that is, with confused boundaries—are depicted in remote parts.

---

### *On figures that are separate, so that they do not appear joined.* CHAPTER LXXIII.

The colors with which you clothe your figures should be such that they lend grace to one another; and when one color serves as the field <note>The background or area immediately surrounding a figure.</note> for another, let it be such that they do not appear joined.

<summary>Leonardo explains the geometric relationship between an object's distance, its size on a painted surface (the "glass"), and the level of detail (finish) required to maintain a realistic illusion. He emphasizes that detail must decrease as distance increases to prevent "false perspective."</summary>

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

...joined together, even if they were of the same nature of color, but let them be varied in brightness as required by the interposition of distance and the thickness of the air that lies between them; and by the same rule, let the definition of their edges follow—that is, more or less distinct or blurred, according to what their proximity or remoteness requires.

->*Whether the light should be taken from the front, or from the side, and which gives more grace.*<-
->CHAPTER LXXIV.<-

Light taken from the front for faces placed against side walls that are dark will cause such faces to have great relief <note>original: "rilieuo"; the illusion of three-dimensionality and depth in a painting.</note>, and especially if the light comes from above. This relief occurs because the front parts of such a face are illuminated by the general light of the air placed before it, whereby such illuminated parts have almost imperceptible shadows. Behind these front parts of the face follow the side parts, darkened by the aforementioned side walls of the room, which darken the face more as those parts of the face go deeper between them. Furthermore, it follows that the light descending from above deprives itself from all those parts to which the protrusions of the face act as a shield, such as the eyebrows which take light away from the eye sockets, the nose which takes it from a large part of the mouth, and the chin from the throat, and other similar reliefs.

->*On reverberation.* CHAPTER LXXV.<-

Reverberations <note>Leonardo uses "riuerberatione" to describe light bouncing from one surface to another, much like an echo of sound.</note> are caused by bodies of a bright quality, with flat and semi-dense surfaces, which, when struck by light, bounce it back toward the first object, much like the bounce of a ball.

->*Where there cannot be luminous reverberation.*<-
->CHAPTER LXXVI.<-

The surfaces of all dense bodies are clothed in various qualities of light and shadow. Lights are of two natures: one is called original, and the other derivative. I say original light is that which derives from a flame of fire, or from the light of the sun or the air. Derivative light shall be reflected light. But to return to the promised definition, I say that luminous reverberation will not come from that part of a body which is turned toward shadowy bodies, such as the dark areas of roofs of various heights, grass, or green or dry woods. Although the part of each branch turned toward the original light is clothed in the quality of that light, there are nonetheless so many shadows cast by each branch upon one another that, in the end, such darkness results that the light there is as nothing. Therefore, such objects cannot give any reflected light to opposing bodies.

->*On reflections.* CHAPTER LXXVII.<-

Let reflections participate more or less in the nature of the thing where they are generated, and of the thing that generates them, according to whether the surface where they are generated is more polished <note>original: "più pulita"; referring to a surface that is smooth or reflective like a mirror.</note> than the surface that generates them.

<summary>Leonardo discusses how frontal and overhead lighting creates three-dimensional relief on a face and defines the difference between direct (original) and reflected (derivative) light.</summary>
<keywords>relief, lighting, reverberation, primary light, reflected light, shadows, portraiture, optics</keywords>

<page-num>19</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>
<warning>Water damage and staining are visible at the bottom edge of the page.</warning>

### *On the reflections of lights that surround shadows. CHAPTER LXXVIII.*

The reflections from illuminated parts that bounce back into the opposing shadows, brightening or softening their darkness more or less according to whether they are closer or further away, and with more or less clarity—this consideration is put into practice by many, while many others avoid it, and these groups laugh at one another. But you, to avoid the criticisms of both, should put both into practice where they are necessary; but ensure their causes are clear—that is, let there be a manifest cause for the reflections and their colors, and likewise a manifest cause for the things that do not reflect. By doing this, you will not be entirely blamed nor praised by varying judgments; if those judgments are not born of complete ignorance, it will be necessary for both schools of thought to praise you entirely.

### *Where reflections of light have greater or lesser clarity. CHAPTER LXXIX.*

The reflections of light have less or more clarity and evidence according to whether they are seen against fields <note>In painting, the "field" refers to the background or surrounding color against which a subject is placed.</note> of greater or lesser darkness. This happens because if the field is darker than the reflection, then that reflection will appear strong and prominent because of the great difference between those colors; but if the reflection is seen against a field lighter than itself, then such a reflection will appear dark compared to the whiteness with which it borders, and thus such a reflection will be imperceptible.

### *Which part of the reflection will be brighter. CHAPTER LXXX.*

**THAT** part will be brighter or more illuminated by the reflection which receives the light within more equal angles. Let the light source be <term>N.</term> and let <term>A.B.</term> be the illuminated part of the body which reflects throughout the opposite concavity, which is in shadow. And let it be that the light reflecting at <term>F.</term> strikes within equal angles. <term>E.</term> will not create a reflection from a base of equal angles, as shown by angle <term>E.A.B.</term> which is more obtuse <note>An angle greater than 90 degrees.</note> than angle <term>E.B.A.</term>; but angle <term>A.F.B.</term>, even though it is within angles of a "lesser quality" <note>Meaning the angle is sharper or narrower than angle E.</note> than angle <term>E.</term>, has the base <term>B.A.</term> between more equal angles than those of angle <term>E.</term>; and therefore it will be brighter at <term>F.</term> than at <term>E.</term> Furthermore, it will be brighter because it is closer to the object that illuminates it, according to the sixth rule <note>A reference to Leonardo's specific scientific principles regarding optics and light.</note> which states: That part of the shaded body will be more illuminated which is closer to its light source.

### *On the reflected colors of flesh.* C A P. L X X X I.

Reflections on flesh that receive light from other flesh are redder and of a more excellent <term>flesh tone</term> <note>original: "incarnatione"; the specific way a painter renders the appearance of human skin</note> than any other part of the skin on a human. This occurs because of the 3rd rule of the 2nd book, which states: The surface of every opaque body participates in the color of its object; and it does so more the closer that object is to it, and less the more remote it is. Furthermore, if the opaque body is larger, it blocks the <term>images</term> <note>original: "spetie"; in Renaissance optics, these are the visual "species" or rays that carry the likeness of an object to a surface</note> of surrounding objects, which are often of various colors that would otherwise corrupt the primary, closer images when the bodies are small. Nevertheless, a small, nearby color will tint a reflection more than a large, distant color, according to the 6th rule of perspective, which says: Large things may be at such a distance that they appear much smaller than small things nearby.

### *Where reflections are most noticeable.* C A P. L X X X I I.

That reflection will be of more immediate evidence which is seen against a <term>field</term> <note>The background or surrounding area of a subject</note> of greater darkness; and that reflection will be less noticeable which is seen against a lighter field. This arises because when things of various levels of darkness are placed in contrast, the less dark makes the darker one appear even gloomier; and when things of various levels of whiteness are placed in contrast, the whitest makes the other appear less white than it truly is.

### *On double and triple reflections.* C A P. L X X X I I I.

Double reflections are of greater power than simple reflections, and the shadows that are interposed between the incident light and these reflections are of little darkness. For example, let A be the light source, and AN and AS be the direct rays; let S and N be...

the parts of these illuminated bodies O. E. are the parts of these bodies illuminated by reflections; and the reflection A. N. E. is the <term>simple reflection</term> <note>A reflection originating from a single source or surface</note>, while A. N. O. A. S. O. is the <term>double reflection</term>. A simple reflection is defined as that which is seen by only one illuminated body; the double reflection is seen by two illuminated bodies, as well as the simple one. Reflection E. is produced by the illuminated body B. D.; the double reflection O. is composed from the illuminated body B. D. and the illuminated body D. ### *How no reflected color is simple, but is mixed with the species of other colors.*
### C H A P T E R L X X X I V.

**NO** color reflecting onto the surface of another body tints that surface with its own pure color; rather, it will be mixed with the concurrence of other reflected colors that bounce to the same place. For example: if yellow color **A.** reflects onto a part of the spherical body **C. O. E.**, and at the same time blue color **B.** reflects onto that same place, I say that because of this mixed reflection of yellow and blue, the impact of their concurrence will tint the sphere. If the sphere was white in itself, it will become green, because it is proven that yellow and blue mixed together produce a most beautiful green.

<note>Leonardo is applying the principles of pigment mixing—where yellow and blue make green—to the physics of light reflection, suggesting that the "images" (species) of colors physically blend on a surface.</note>

### *How reflections are very rarely the color of the body where they meet.*
->C H A P T E R  L X X X V.<-

It happens very rarely that reflections are of the same color as the body itself, or of the object where they meet. For example, let the <term>spherical body</term> <note>original: "sferico"</note> **D. F. G. E.** be yellow, and let the <term>object</term> <note>The source of the reflection</note> that reflects its color onto it be **B. C.**, which is blue. I say that the part of the sphere that is struck by such a reflection will be tinted a green color, provided **B. C.** is <term>illuminated</term> <note>original: "alluminato"; lit up by a light source</note> by the air or the sun.

---

### *Where the reflection will be most visible.* C H A P T E R  L X X X V I.

Among reflections of the same shape, size, and strength, that part will appear more or less powerful which terminates against a darker or lighter background.

The surfaces of bodies take on more of the color of those objects which reflect their likeness into them at more <term>equal angles</term> <note>Leonardo refers here to the law of reflection, where the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection</note>.

Of the colors of objects that reflect their likenesses onto the surfaces of bodies placed before them at equal angles, that one will be most powerful which has a shorter <term>reflected ray</term> <note>The closer the object is to the surface, the stronger the color of its reflection</note>.

Among the colors of objects reflected at equal angles and from some distance onto the surface of opposite bodies, the one that is of a brighter color will be most powerful.

That object reflects its color more intensely onto the body in front of it when it has no other colors around it except those of its own kind. However, a reflection will be of a more confused color if it is generated by objects of various colors.

The color that is closest to the reflection will tint that reflection most with itself, and vice-versa.

Therefore, you, painter, in the reflections of the faces of your figures, should use the color of the parts of the <term>garments</term> <note>original: "vestimenti"</note> that are near the parts of the <term>flesh</term> <note>original: "carni"; refers to skin tones</note> that are closest to them; but do not separate them with too much definition unless it is necessary.

<summary>Leonardo explains that reflections rarely match the color of the surface they strike, instead mixing to form new hues (like blue reflecting on yellow to make green). He also provides rules for painters on how distance, angle, and surrounding colors affect the intensity of a reflection.</summary>
<keywords>optics, color mixing, reflection, law of reflection, painting technique, skin tones, light and shadow</keywords>

## On the colors of reflections. CHAPTER LXXXVII.

**ALL** reflected colors possess less luminosity than <term>direct light</term> <note>original: "lume retto"; light hitting an object directly from the source</note>, and the same proportion exists between <term>incident light</term> <note>original: "lume incidente"; light falling onto a surface</note> and reflected light as exists between the brightness of their respective causes.

## On the boundaries of reflections against their background. CHAPTER LXXXVIII.

**THE** boundary of a reflection against a background lighter than the reflection itself will cause that reflection to be <term>imperceptible</term> <note>original: "insensibile"; literally "insensible" or unable to be perceived by the eye</note>; but if such a reflection ends against a background darker than itself, then that reflection will be perceptible, and it will become more evident the darker the background is, and likewise conversely.

## On the placement of figures. CHAPTER LXXXIX.

**TO THE SAME EXTENT** that the part of the <term>nude figure</term> <note>original: "ignudo"</note> **D. A.** decreases in size due to resting its weight, the opposite side increases: that is, as much as the part **D. A.** diminishes from its measurement, the opposite part grows beyond its measurement. The <term>navel</term> <note>original: "bellico"</note> or the <term>virile member</term> <note>the genitals</note> never leaves its central height. This lowering occurs because when a figure stands upon one foot, that foot becomes the center of the weight placed above it. Because of this, the center of the shoulders aligns itself directly above it, moving out of its <term>perpendicular line</term> <note>the vertical axis of gravity</note>, which usually passes through the center of the body's surface. This line becomes more curved at its upper extremity over the foot that bears the weight; the transverse lines, forced into equal angles, have their ends lower on the side that rests, as appears in **A. B. C.**

<note>Leonardo is describing the classical pose known as "contrapposto," where the weight is shifted onto one leg (the "resting" or "standing" leg), causing the hips and shoulders to tilt in opposite directions to maintain balance. The diagram A. B. C. mentioned would illustrate these shifting axes of the torso.</note>

<summary>This page defines the relationship between direct and reflected light intensity and provides a technical anatomical guide to "contrapposto," explaining how the body's proportions shift when weight is placed on a single leg.</summary>
<keywords>light theory, reflection, luminosity, anatomy, contrapposto, weight distribution, figure drawing, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

# TREATISE ON PAINTING

## *On the method of learning well how to compose figures together in narrative scenes.*
## CHAP. LXXXX.

**WHEN** you have learned <term>perspective</term> <note>original: "prospettiua"</note> well, and have committed to memory all the limbs and bodies of things, seek out frequently in your walks to see and consider the positions of men as they talk, or argue, or laugh, or scuffle together; observe what actions are in them, and what actions are made by the bystanders, those who separate the fighters, and those who watch such things. Note these with brief marks in a small notebook of yours, which you ought always to carry with you. And let it be of <term>tinted paper</term> <note>original: "carte tinte"; paper prepared with a base color, often used for silverpoint drawings that cannot be erased</note>, so that you do not have to erase them, but rather move from the old to the new; for these are not things to be erased, but rather preserved with the greatest diligence. Because the infinite forms and actions of things are so many that the memory is not capable of retaining them; therefore, you shall keep these as your aids and teachers.

## *On first placing a figure in a narrative scene.* CAP. LXXXXI.

**THE** first figure in a <term>narrative scene</term> <note>original: "historia"; a complex multi-figure painting depicting a historical, biblical, or mythological event</note> you shall make as much smaller than life-size as the number of <term>braccia</term> <note>original: "braccia"; an Italian unit of measurement, approximately the length of an arm</note> you imagine it to be distant from the front line; then make the others in comparison to that one, according to the rule mentioned above.

## *The method of composing narrative scenes.* CAP. LXXXXII.

**OF** the figures that compose narrative scenes, that one will show the greatest <term>relief</term> <note>original: "rilieuo"; the illusion of three-dimensionality on a flat surface</note> which is feigned to be closest to the eye. This happens because of the second [rule] of the third [book], which says: that color shows itself with greater perfection which has a smaller quantity of air interposed between itself and the eye that judges it. For this reason, shadows—which show bodies to be in relief—appear even darker up close than from a distance, where they are corrupted by the air interposed between the eye and those shadows. This does not happen in shadows near the eye, where they show bodies with much greater relief because they are of greater darkness.

## *On composing narrative scenes.* CAP. LXXXXIII.

**REMEMBER**, painter, when you make a single figure, to avoid <term>foreshortenings</term> <note>original: "scorci"; the technique of depicting an object or figure in a picture in depth</note> of it, both in its parts and in the whole, because you would have to struggle against the ignorance of those unlearned in such an art. But in narrative scenes, use them in every way that occurs to you, and especially in battles, where by necessity there occur infinite foreshortenings and bendings among the participants of such discord, or rather, <term>most beastly madness</term> <note>original: "pazzia bestialissima"; Leonardo’s famous description of war</note>.

## *Variety of men in narrative scenes.* CAP. LXXXXIV.

**IN** narrative scenes there ought to be men of various temperaments, statures, complexions, attitudes, fatness, thinness, thick, slender, large, small, fat, lean, fierce, civil, old, young, strong and muscular, weak and with few muscles, cheerful, melancholy, and with hair curly and straight, short and long, movements prompt and languid, and likewise various garments, and colors, and whatever else such a narrative scene requires.

## *On learning the movements of man.* CAP. LXXXXV.

**THE** movements of man must be learned after the knowledge—

<meta>catchword: —ledge (continuation of "cognitione" / knowledge)</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>
<sig>D</sig>
<meta>Catchword: ac-</meta>

action of the limbs, and of the whole, in all the movements of the limbs and joints, and then with brief notation of a few marks, see the attitude of men in their various <term>circumstances</term> <note>original: "accidenti"; in Renaissance art theory, this refers to the spontaneous physical manifestations of emotion or external events</note>, without them realizing that you are observing them; because if they notice it, they will have their minds focused on you, and will abandon the intensity of their action, in which they were previously fully engaged. Like when two angry people contend together, where each seems to have right on their side, and they move their eyebrows, arms, and other limbs with great ferocity, with gestures appropriate to their intention and their words. This you could not do if you wanted to make them pretend such anger, or other circumstances, such as laughter, weeping, pain, wonder, fear, and the like. Therefore, be eager to carry with you a <term>small notebook of gesso-prepared paper</term> <note>original: "libretto di carte ingeſſate"; paper coated with a ground of bone ash or lead white, allowing for extremely fine, permanent marks</note>, and with a <term>silverpoint</term> <note>original: "stile d’argento"; a drawing tool made of silver that leaves a delicate, indelible line on prepared paper</note> note such movements with brevity, and similarly note the actions of the bystanders and their distribution. This will teach you how to compose <term>narrative scenes</term> <note>original: "historie"; large-scale paintings depicting a specific event or story</note>; and when you have filled your notebook, put it aside and keep it for your purpose. A good painter has two principal things to observe: the man, and the <term>concept of his mind</term> <note>original: "concetto ſuo della mente"; the internal thought or emotion that drives external movement</note> that he holds within, which is of the utmost importance.

---

## *On composing narrative scenes.* Chap. LXXXXVI.

The study of the compositions of narrative scenes must be to place the figures <term>roughly</term> <note>original: "diſgroſſatamente"; as a preliminary sketch or "blocking out"</note>, that is, sketched; and first knowing how to do them well in all their turns, and the foldings and extensions of their limbs. Then, take the description of two who are boldly fighting together, and let such an invention be examined in various actions and from various aspects. Then follow with a fight between a bold man and a cowardly, fearful one; and such actions and many other <term>accidents of the soul</term> <note>original: "accidenti dell’ animo"; the physical effects caused by internal emotions</note> should be speculated upon with great examination and study.

---

## *On variety in narrative scenes.* Chap. LXXXXVII.

The painter should delight in the abundance and variety of compositions for narrative scenes, and avoid repeating any part that has already been done, so that the novelty and abundance attract and delight the eye of the viewer. I say, therefore, that in a narrative scene, according to the setting, there are required men of diverse appearances, of different ages and dress, mixed together with women, children, dogs, horses, and buildings, fields, and hills. And let dignity and decorum be observed for the prince and the wise man, separating them from the common folk. Nor should you mix the melancholy and weeping with the cheerful and laughing; for nature dictates that the cheerful stay with the cheerful, and the laughing with the laughing, and so for the contrary.

---

## *On diversifying the appearance of faces in narrative scenes.* Chap. LXXXXVIII.

A common defect among Italian painters is that the appearance and figure of the <term>Emperor</term> <note>original: "Imperatore"; used here likely as a metaphor for a generic, idealized, or repeated facial type found in many paintings</note> can be recognized throughout many painted figures. Therefore, to avoid such an error, the same figures should never be made or repeated, neither in whole nor in part, nor should one face be seen in another narrative scene. And the more you observe in a scene that the ugly is near the beautiful, and the old near the young, and the weak near the strong, the more charming your narrative scene will be, as one figure by the other ac-

<summary>Leonardo advises artists to use silverpoint notebooks to surreptitiously capture authentic human emotions and advocates for narrative paintings filled with diverse ages, social classes, and contrasting physical types to achieve realism and visual interest.</summary>

...will increase in beauty. And because it often happens that painters, when drawing whatever they wish, want every slightest charcoal mark to be valid, they are mistaken in this; for it often occurs that the living figure depicted does not have the movements of its limbs suited to its <term>mental motion</term> <note>original: "moto mentale"; Leonardo’s term for the way an internal emotion or thought is expressed through physical gesture</note>. If the painter has already made a beautiful and pleasing arrangement of limbs, and finished it well, it will seem to him a shameful thing to have to change those limbs.

---

### *On matching colors with one another, so that one brings grace to the other.*
->CH. LXXXIX.<-

**If** you want to ensure that the proximity of one color brings grace to another that borders it, follow the same rule seen in the sun's rays in the composition of the <term>heavenly arch</term> <note>original: "arco celeste"; the rainbow</note>. These colors are generated in the movement of the rain, because each individual drop, in its descent, is transformed into each of the colors of that arch, as has been shown in its proper place.

**Now** take note: if you want to create an excellent darkness, contrast it with an excellent whiteness; and likewise, you will create an excellent whiteness with the greatest darkness. A pale color will make red appear a more fiery redness than it would appear by itself when compared to <term>violet</term> <note>original: "pauonazzo"; a deep purple or violet color, literally "peacock-colored"</note>. There is another rule which does not aim to make the colors themselves more supremely beautiful than they naturally are, but seeks to have their company give grace to one another, as green does to red, and similarly the opposite, such as green with blue. And there is a second rule that produces ungraceful company, such as blue with a yellow that tends toward white, or with white itself, and similar combinations, which will be discussed in their proper place.

---

### *On making colors vivid and beautiful on their surfaces.*
->CH. C.<-

**Always** for those colors which you wish to have beauty, first prepare a field of the purest white. I say this for colors that are transparent; for those that are not transparent, a bright field is of no use. We see an example of this in stained glass; when the pieces are placed between the eye and the luminous air, they show excellent beauty, which they could not do if they had the murky air or other darkness behind them.

---

### *On the colors of the shadows of any color.*
->CH. CI.<-

**The** color of the shadow of any color always participates in the color of its object, and more or less so depending on how near or remote it is from that shadow, and how much it is more or less luminous.

---

### *On the variety created by the colors of things distant and near.*
->CH. CII.<-

**Of** things darker than the air, that which is more remote will show less darkness; and of things brighter than the air, that which is more remote from the eye will show less whiteness. This is because when things both brighter and darker than the air change color over a long distance, the light object acquires darkness, and the dark object acquires brightness. <note>This principle is the foundation of "aerial perspective," where the atmosphere acts as a veil that equalizes colors toward a mid-tone blue or gray as they recede.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains the importance of color harmony, the use of white grounds for transparency, and the rules of atmospheric perspective where distance softens the contrast of light and dark objects.</summary>
<keywords>color theory, color harmony, rainbow, white ground, transparency, shadows, aerial perspective, mental motion</keywords>

### *At what distance the colors of things are entirely lost.*
->C H A P T E R  C I I I.<-

The colors of things are lost entirely at a greater or lesser distance, according to whether the eyes and the object seen are at a greater or lesser height. This is proven by the 7th <note>Leonardo refers to one of his earlier optical propositions regarding the density of the atmosphere.</note> of this treatise, which says: the air is more or less <term>thick</term> <note>original: "grossa"; referring to atmospheric density or opacity</note> as it is nearer to or further from the earth. Therefore, if the eye and the object seen are near the earth, then the thickness of the air interposed between the eye and the object will greatly hinder the color of the object seen by that eye. But if the eye, together with the object seen, is far from the earth, then such air will occupy little of the color of the said object. There are as many varieties of distances at which the colors of objects are lost as there are varieties of the time of day, and as there are varieties in the thickness or thinness of the air through which the <term>species</term> <note>original: "specie"; in Leonardo’s optics, these are the visual "images" or "likenesses" that travel from an object to the eye</note> of the colors of the said objects penetrate to the eye.

### *The color of the shadow of white.*  C H A P T E R  C I V.

The shadow of white seen under the sun and the open air has its shadows tending toward blue. This arises because white is not a color in itself, but is a <term>receptacle</term> <note>original: "ricetto"; Leonardo views white as a neutral surface that "receives" or reflects whatever colors surround it</note> for any color; and according to the 4th of this treatise, which says: "the surface of every body partakes of the color of its object," it is necessary that this part of the white surface partakes of the color of its object, which is the air <note>original: "aria"; here meaning the blue sky.</note>.

### *Which color will produce the blackest shadow.*  C H A P T E R  C V.

That shadow will partake most of black which is generated on the whitest surface; and this surface will have a greater tendency for variety than any other surface. This arises because white is not counted among the colors, but is receptive to every color; its surface partakes more intensely of the colors of its objects than any other surface of whatever color, and especially of its direct contrary, which is black (or other dark colors), from which white is most remote by nature. Because of this, there appears to be—and indeed is—a great difference between its primary shadows and its primary lights.

### *On the color that shows no variation in different thicknesses of air.*
->C H A P T E R  C V I.<-

It is possible that the same color does not change at various distances; this will happen when the proportion of the thickness of the air and the proportions of the distances that the colors have from the eye are the same, but inverse. It is proven thus: let **A** be the eye, and **H** be whatever color you wish, placed at one degree of distance from the eye in air of four degrees of thickness. But because the second level above, **A.M.N.L.**, has air half as thin, the air carrying that same color in it, it is necessary that such a color be twice as remote from the eye as it was before. Therefore, we shall place it at the two degrees **A. F.** and **F. G.** away from the eye, and it will be color **G.**, which then rising into the level of double thinness to the second at **A. M.**...

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

N. L. which will make the degree O. M. P. N.; it is necessary that it be placed at height E, and the entire line A. E. will be distant from the eye. This line is proven to be worth as much in <term>thickness of the air</term> <note>original: "grossezza d'aria"; Leonardo uses this term to describe atmospheric density. He observed that air is "thicker" or more opaque near the earth's surface due to moisture and dust.</note> as the distance A. G., and it is proven thus: If distance A. G., interposed by the same air between the eye and the color, occupies two degrees, and A. E. [occupies] two and a half degrees, this distance is sufficient to ensure that color G, raised to E, does not vary in its intensity. This is because the degree A. C. and the degree A. F., being the same thickness of air, are similar and equal; and the degree C. D., although equal in length to the degree F. G., is not similar in thickness of air, because it is positioned in air of double the thickness of the air above it. Of this [thicker air], a half-degree of distance occupies as much of the color as an entire degree of the air above, which is twice as thin as the air that borders it below.

---

Therefore, by first calculating the thickness of the air and then the distances, you will see colors varied in position that have not changed in their beauty <note>By "beauty," Leonardo refers to the clarity, saturation, and vibrancy of the color as it reaches the eye.</note>; and we shall speak thus for the calculation of the thickness of the air: color H is placed in four degrees of air thickness; color G is placed in air of two degrees of thickness; color E is found in air of one degree of thickness. Now let us see if the distances are in equal but inverse proportion. Color E is found at a distance of two and a half degrees from the eye. G is at two degrees. H is at one degree. This distance does not match the proportion of the thickness, but it is necessary to make a third calculation, and this is what you must say: The degree A. C., as was said above, is similar and equal to the degree A. F.; and the half-degree C. B. is similar but not equal to the degree A. F., because it is only a half-degree of length, which is worth one entire degree of the air above. Therefore, the calculation found satisfies the purpose, because A. C. is worth two degrees of the thickness of the air above, and the half-degree C. B. is worth one [degree] of that same air above, so that we have three degrees in the value of that thickness above, and there is one within, that is, B. E., which is the fourth. It follows that A. H. has four degrees of air thickness; A. G. also has four, that is, A. F. has two, and F. G. another two, which makes four. A. E. also has four.

<summary>Leonardo demonstrates through geometric calculation that an object's color remains equally clear at different distances if the change in distance is offset by a change in atmospheric density at different altitudes.</summary>

...because A.C. contains two [degrees], and C.D. contains one, which is half of A.C. and of that same air, and one whole degree is above in the thin air, which makes four. Therefore, if the distance A.E. is not double the distance A.G., nor quadruple the distance A.H., it is compensated for by C.D.—a half-degree of <term>thick air</term> <note>original: "aria grossa"; Leonardo refers to the denser, more moisture-laden air found closer to the ground.</note> which is worth one whole degree of the thinner air situated above it. And thus our proposition is concluded: that the color H.G.E. does not vary despite the different distances.

## On the perspective of colors. CHAPTER CVII.

For one and the same color placed at various distances and at the same height, the proportion of its lightening <note>original: "rischiaramento"; the process by which the atmosphere makes a color appear paler or less saturated.</note> will be the same as the proportion of the distances that each of those colors has from the eye that sees them. 

This is proven: let E.B.C.D. be the same color. Let the 1st be placed two degrees of distance from the eye A; let the 2nd, which is B, be four degrees away; the 3rd, which is C, be six degrees; the 4th, which is D, be eight degrees—as shown by the definitions of the circles that intersect on the line, as seen above line A.R. Then, let A.R.S.P. be one degree of thin air, and S.P.E.T. be one degree of thicker air. 

It will follow that the first color, E, will pass to the eye through one degree of thick air, E.S., and through one degree of less thick air, S.A. Color B will send its <term>likeness</term> <note>original: "similitudine"; in Renaissance optics, this refers to the "species" or visual image of an object traveling through the air to the eye.</note> to eye A through two degrees of thick air and through two of the less thick. Color C will send it through three degrees of the thick and three of the less thick. And color D will send it through four of the thick and four of the less thick. 

Thus we have proven here that the proportion of the <term>diminution of colors</term> <note>original: "diminutione de colori, o vuoi dire perdimenti"; Leonardo uses "losses" to describe how colors lose their intensity as they recede.</note> is the same as the proportion of their distances from the eye that sees them. This only happens with colors that are at an equal height; for those that are at unequal heights, the same rule is not observed, because they exist in air of various thicknesses, which create various obstructions to those colors.

---

## On color that does not change in various thicknesses of air. CHAPTER CVIII.

A color placed in different thicknesses of air will not change when it is...

<sig>D iij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo demonstrates that when an object stays at the same altitude, its color fades in direct mathematical proportion to its distance from the viewer. He concludes by noting that this simple rule changes if the object moves into different altitudes where the air density (thickness) varies.</summary>
<keywords>atmospheric perspective, color diminution, air density, optical science, visual distance, Leonardo da Vinci, Trattato della Pittura</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the mathematical demonstration from page 29 regarding how atmospheric density compensates for distance as an object rises. It then transitions into observations on the nature of shadow and the physiological response of the eye to light.</meta>

# TREATISE ON PAINTING

<warning>Water stains and discoloration visible on the left side of the page.</warning>

...the more remote from the eye one is than the other; as much as one is found in thinner air than the other. This is proven as follows. If the first <term>low air</term> <note>original: "aria bassa"; the denser, more moisture-heavy atmosphere found near the ground.</note> has four degrees of density, and the color is distant one degree from the eye, and the second higher air has three degrees of density (having lost one degree), it causes the color to acquire one degree of distance; and when the higher air has lost two degrees of density, and the color has acquired two degrees of distance, then the first color is equal to the third. To be brief: if the color rises so high that it enters the air that has lost three degrees of density, and the color has acquired three degrees of distance, then you can be certain that such a loss of color has made the high and remote color equal to the low and near color; because if the high air has lost three-quarters of the density of the low air, the color in rising has acquired three-quarters of the entire distance by which it is remote from the eye. And thus our intent is proven.

*Whether various colors can be, or appear to be, of a uniform darkness, by means of one and the same shadow.* CHAPTER CIX.

IT IS POSSIBLE that all the varieties of colors may appear transformed into the color of those shadows by one and the same shadow. This is manifested in the darkness of a cloudy night, in which no shape or color of a body can be understood. And because darkness is nothing other than the deprivation of incident and reflected light (by means of which all shapes and colors of bodies are understood), it is necessary that once the cause of the light is entirely removed, the effect and knowledge of the colors and shapes of the aforementioned bodies must also fail.

*Of the cause of the loss of colors and shapes of bodies by means of darkness that appears to be, but is not.* CHAPTER CX.

THERE ARE MANY locations that are in themselves illuminated and bright which appear dark, and entirely deprived of any variety of colors and shapes of the things found within them. This occurs because of the light of the illuminated air that is interposed between the things seen and the eye—as is seen inside windows that are remote from the eye, in which only a uniform and quite deep obscurity is perceived. If you then enter into that house, you will see that it is in itself strongly illuminated, and you will be able to readily perceive every minimal part of whatever might be found inside such a window. This manifestation arises from a defect of the eye, which, overcome by the excessive light of the air, greatly restricts the size of its pupil, and for this reason lacks much of its power. In darker places, the pupil dilates, and its power grows as much as it acquires size. This is proven in the 2nd [book] of my <term>perspective</term> <note>original: "prospettiua"; Leonardo refers here to his scientific studies on optics and the physiology of vision.</note>.

*How nothing shows its true color if it does not have light from another similar color.* CHAPTER CXI.

NOTHING WILL EVER show its own proper color unless the light that illuminates it is entirely of that same color; and this is manifested in the colors of...

<summary>Leonardo concludes his geometric proof on atmospheric perspective and explores how the eye's pupil dilation affects the perception of light and darkness, particularly when looking into windows. He also introduces the principle that an object's true color is only visible under light of a matching hue.</summary>
