<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

...cloths, of which the illuminated folds, which reflect or give light to the opposing folds, cause them to demonstrate their true color. The same occurs with gold leaf in giving light to one another, and the opposite happens when receiving light from another color.

### *On colors that appear to vary from their true essence, by means of comparison with their backgrounds.* CHAPTER CXII.

**N**O boundary of a uniform color will appear to be equal unless it ends against a background of a color similar to itself. This is clearly seen when black meets white, as each color appears more <term>noble</term> <note>Leonardo uses "nobile" (noble) here to mean more vivid, pure, or intense.</note> at the boundaries of its opposite than it will appear in its center.

### *On the mutation of transparent colors applied or placed over different colors, with their diverse relationship.* CHAPTER CXIII.

**W**HEN a transparent color is placed over another color different from itself, a mixed color is composed that is distinct from each of the simple colors that compose it. This is seen in smoke coming out of a chimney: when it is against the black of that chimney, it appears blue; and when it rises against the blue of the air, it appears <term>ash-grey</term> <note>original: "berretino"; a dull, brownish-grey color typical of coarse cloth.</note> or reddish. And so purple applied over blue becomes violet; and when blue is applied over yellow, it becomes green; and saffron applied over white becomes yellow; and light over darkness becomes blue—appearing more beautiful as the light and the dark are more excellent.

### *Which part of the same color will show itself as most beautiful in painting.* CHAPTER CXIV.

**H**ERE it is to be noted which part of the same color shows itself most beautiful in painting: whether that which has the luster, or that which has the light, or that of the middle shadows, or that of the dark ones, or in transparency. Here it is necessary to understand which color is being discussed, because different colors have their beauties in different parts of themselves. This is shown to us by black, which has its beauty in the shadows; white in the light; blue, green, and <term>tan</term> <note>original: "tanè"; a tawny or yellowish-brown pigment.</note> in the middle shadows; yellow and red in the lights; gold in the reflections; and <term>lake</term> <note>original: "lacca"; a red pigment made from organic dyes, often used in transparent glazes.</note> in the middle shadows.

### *How every color that lacks luster is more beautiful in its luminous parts than in its shadowy ones.* CHAPTER CXV.

**E**VERY color is more beautiful in its illuminated part than in its shadowy one; this arises because light vivifies and gives true notice of the quality of colors, while shadow stifles and obscures that same beauty and prevents the recognition of that color. And if, on the contrary, black is more beautiful in the shadows than in the lights, the answer is that black is not a color.

### *On the visibility of colors.* CHAPTER CXVI.

**T**HAT thing which is lighter appears more from a distance, and the darker thing does the contrary.

### *Which part of a color should reasonably be the most beautiful.*
### CAP. CXVII.

If **A** is the light, and **B** is illuminated by a direct line from that light: **E**, which cannot see the light itself, sees only the illuminated wall; let us say that this wall is red. This being the case, the light generated by the wall will resemble its cause, and will tint the face **E** in red. And if **E** is already red itself, you will see it appear much more beautiful than **B**. And if **E** were yellow, you would see an <term>iridescent</term> <note>original: "cangiante"; a color that appears to change hue depending on the light or angle.</note> color created between yellow and red.

<image-desc>A framed technical diagram showing a light source 'A' (depicted as a sun with a face) emitting rays towards a vertical rectangular surface 'B'. A second surface 'E' is positioned at an angle, shaded from direct light by surface 'B' but receiving reflected light from it. The number 117 is printed in the top right corner within the decorative border of the image.</image-desc>

### *How the beauty of color should be in the lights.* CAP. CXVIII.

If we see that the quality of colors is known by means of light, it is to be judged that where there is more light, there one sees more of the true quality of the illuminated color; and where there is more darkness, the color is tinted with the color of that darkness. Therefore you, painter, remember to show the truth of colors in such illuminated parts.

### *On the green color made from copper rust.* CAP. CXIX.

The green made from copper <note>original: "ruggine di rame"; referring to verdigris, a pigment made by exposing copper to acetic acid vapors.</note>, even if such a color is applied with oil, its beauty "goes up in smoke" <note>Meaning the color fades or loses its vibrancy.</note> if it is not immediately varnished. And not only does its beauty vanish, but if it were washed with a sponge dampened with simple common water, it would be lifted from the panel where it is painted, especially if the weather is humid. This occurs because such <term>verdigris</term> is made by the power of salt, and this salt dissolves with ease in rainy weather, and especially when being soaked and washed with the aforementioned sponge.

### *Increasing the beauty of verdigris.* CAP. CXX.

If <term>aloe cavallino</term> <note>A bitter, dark resin extracted from aloe plants, used as a transparent glaze to warm up green tones.</note> is mixed with the verdigris, the verdigris will acquire great beauty; and it would acquire even more from saffron, if it did not also vanish into

<meta>catchword: smoke.</meta>

<summary>Leonardo explains how reflected light enhances color intensity and warns of the chemical instability of verdigris, a green pigment that dissolves in water unless protected by varnish.</summary>
<keywords>reflected light, iridescent color, verdigris, pigment stability, aloe cavallino, saffron, varnish, copper rust</keywords>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

smoke. And the quality of this <term>horse-aloe</term> <note>original: "aloe cauallino"; a common, less refined grade of aloe resin used in the Renaissance as a yellow-brown glaze.</note> is recognized when it dissolves in <term>aqua vitae</term> <note>original: "acquauite"; distilled spirits or alcohol.</note> while it is warm; for it dissolves better than when it is cold. And if you have finished a work with a simple green, and then thinly glazed it with this aloe dissolved in water, then the work would become a most beautiful color. Furthermore, this aloe can be ground in oil by itself, or even together with verdigris, and with any other color you please.

### *On the mixing of colors with one another.* Chapter 121.

ALTHOUGH the mixing of colors with one another extends toward infinity, I will not refrain for this reason from making a brief discourse upon it. Setting down first a number of simple colors, I will mix each of those with every other one by one, then two by two, and three by three, and so continuing until the full number of all colors. Then I will begin again to mix colors two by two, and three by three, then by four, so continuing until the end. Upon those two simple colors one will put three, and with those three I will accompany another three, and then six, and then follow such mixing in all proportions. I call those "simple colors" that are not composed, nor can they be composed by way of mixing other colors: black and white—although these are not placed among the colors, because one is darkness and the other is light (that is, one is privation and the other is generative). Yet I do not wish to leave them behind for this reason, because in painting they are the principals, given that painting is composed of shadows and lights, that is, of light and dark. After black and white follow blue and yellow, then green and <term>tawny</term> <note>original: "lionato"; literally "lion-colored," a reddish-brown or tan hue.</note>, that is <term>tan</term> <note>original: "tanè"; a brownish-orange color.</note> or, if you prefer, <term>ochre</term>; then <term>mulberry</term> <note>original: "morello"; a dark purple-red.</note>, that is <term>purple</term> <note>original: "pauonazzo"; a deep violet like a peacock’s neck.</note>, and red. And these are eight colors, and no more exist in nature, from which I begin the mixing. And let it be first black and white, then black and yellow, and black and red; then yellow and black, and yellow and red. And because here I lack paper, the author says, I will leave the making of such distinctions to my work with a long process, which will be of great utility, indeed most necessary; and this description will be placed between theory and practice.

### *On the surface of every shaded body.* Chapter 122.

THE surface of every shaded body partakes of the color of its object <note>Here, "object" refers to whatever is casting light or reflections onto the surface.</note>. Shaded bodies demonstrate this with certainty, for none of the aforementioned bodies shows its shape or color if the medium interposed between the body and the luminous source is not illuminated. We shall say, therefore, that if the opaque body is yellow and the light source is blue, the illuminated part will be green, which green is composed of yellow and blue.

### *Which surface is receptive to the most colors.* Chapter 123.

WHITE is more receptive to any color than any other surface of any body that is not a mirror. This is proven by saying that every empty body is capable of receiving what bodies that are not empty cannot receive. We shall say for this reason that white is "empty," or if you prefer, deprived of any color; and being illuminated by the color of any light source, it partakes more of that light than would the...

black, which is like a broken vessel, which is deprived of every capacity for anything. <note>Leonardo is using the metaphor of a broken jar that can hold no liquid to describe how black, as "total darkness," cannot receive or reflect light in the same way colors do.</note>

### *Which body will be most tinted by the color of its object.* Cap. 124.

**The** surface of every body will participate more fully in the color of that object which is closest to it. This happens because the nearby object occupies a greater multitude of varieties of <term>species</term> <note>original: "spetie"; in Renaissance optics, these were the "images" or "likenesses" of objects that travel through the air to the eye or to other surfaces.</note> which, coming to the surface of such bodies, would corrupt the surface of that object more than they would if the color were remote. And by occupying such species, that color demonstrates its nature more completely in that opaque body.

### *Which body will show a more beautiful color.* Cap. 125.

**The** surface of that opaque body will show a more perfect color when it has a color similar to its own as a neighboring object.

### *On the flesh tones of faces.* Cap. 126.

**That** quality of bodies is better preserved over a long distance which exists in the greatest quantity. This proposition shows us that the face becomes dark in the distance, because shadow is the largest part of the face, and the lights are minimal; and therefore they fail at a short distance. And the smallest of all are their <term>specular highlights</term> <note>original: "lustri"; the bright glints of light reflected off a moist or smooth surface, like the tip of the nose or the eyes.</note>, and this is the reason why—since the darkest part remains—the face becomes and appears dark. And it will seem to draw even more toward black the more that face has something whiter upon the body or the head. <note>This refers to the law of simultaneous contrast: a face looks darker if framed by a white collar or veil.</note>

### *Method of drawing from relief, and of preparing papers for this.* Cap. 127.

**Painters**, in order to draw things in <term>relief</term> <note>original: "rilieuo"; three-dimensional objects or sculptures used as models.</note>, must tint the surface of the papers with a medium darkness, and then apply the darkest shadows, and finally the principal lights in small areas—which are those that are the first to be lost to the eye at a small distance.

### *On the variety of a single color at various distances from the eye.* Cap. 128.

**Among** colors of the same nature, that one varies the least which is moved the least from the eye. This is proven because the air that is interposed between the eye and the thing seen occupies the said thing; and if the interposed air is of a great amount, then the thing seen is strongly tinted with the color of that air; and if the air is of a thin quantity, then the object will be little hindered. <note>This is a foundational principle of "atmospheric perspective," where distance adds a veil of blue or gray light to objects.</note>

### *On the greenery seen in the countryside.* Cap. 129.

**Of** greenery seen in the countryside of equal quality, that which is in the plants of the trees will appear to be darker, and that of the meadows will show itself to be lighter.

### *Which greenery will appear more blue.* CHAPTER 130.

**Those** greens will show themselves to partake more in blue which have the darkest shadows; and this is proven by the 7th [proposition], which says that blue is composed of light and dark over a long distance. <note>Leonardo refers to his theory that the blue of the sky and distant objects is created by "white" light (from the atmosphere or sun) passing in front of "black" (the darkness of space or deep shadows).</note>

### *Which surface shows its true color less than others.* CHAPTER 131.

**That** surface will show its true color less which is more polished and smooth. We see this in the grass of the meadows and in the leaves of trees, which, having a smooth and glossy surface, take on a <term>specular highlight</term> <note>original: "lustro"; the bright, mirror-like reflection of a light source on a surface.</note> in which the sun or the air that illuminates them is reflected; and thus, in that part of the highlight, they are deprived of their natural color.

### *Which body will show its true color more.* CHAPTER 132.

**That** body will demonstrate its true color more whose surface is less smooth and even. This is seen in linen cloths, and in the leaves of plants and trees that are "hairy" <note>Leonardo is likely referring to "pubescent" or fuzzy leaves, such as those of the sage or mullein plant, which diffuse light rather than reflecting it.</note>, in which no highlight can be generated; therefore, by necessity, as they cannot reflect objects, they only return to the eye their true and natural color. This color is not corrupted by any body that illuminates it with an opposing color, such as the redness of the sun when it sets and tints the clouds with its own hue.

### *On the clarity of landscapes.* CHAPTER 133.

**The** colors, vibrancy, and clarity of painted landscapes will never have conformity with natural landscapes illuminated by the sun, unless those painted landscapes are also illuminated by the sun itself.

### *Common perspective on the diminishing of colors at a long distance.* CHAPTER 134.

**The** air will partake so much less in the color blue the closer it is to the horizon, and it is so much darker the further it is from that horizon. This is proven by the 3rd of the 9th, which shows that a body will be less illuminated by the sun when it is of a rarer quality. Therefore the <term>element of fire</term> <note>In the Aristotelian cosmology of Leonardo's time, the "sphere of fire" was thought to exist as a layer of the atmosphere above the air.</note>, which clothes the air, because it is rarer and thinner than the air, occupies the darkness above it less than the air does; and consequently, the air—a body less rare than fire—is illuminated more by the sun’s rays that penetrate it. And by illuminating the infinity of <term>atoms</term> <note>original: "atomi"; in this context, Leonardo refers to dust motes or tiny particles suspended in the air that reflect light.</note> that are infused through it, it renders itself bright to our eyes. Thus, as the image of the aforementioned darkness penetrates through that air, it necessarily makes that whiteness of the air appear blue, as is proven in the 3rd of the 10th; and it will appear to us as a lighter blue the greater the thickness of air that is interposed between that darkness and our eyes. For example, if the eye of the observer were P, and it looked above itself at the thickness of the air P R, and then, looking down somewhat, the eye saw the air through the line P S, this would appear brighter to it, because

<sig>E ij</sig>

### *Of things reflected in the water of landscapes, and first of the air.*
#### CHAPTER 135.

**Only** that air will produce its image <note>original: "simulacro"; a likeness or reflection.</note> on the surface of the water which reflects from the water’s surface to the eye at equal angles—that is, where the angle of <term>incidence</term> <note>The angle at which light or a sight-line hits a surface.</note> is equal to the angle of reflection.

### *The diminution of colors by a medium placed between them and the eye.*
#### CHAPTER 136.

**A** visible object will show less of its natural color in proportion to the greater thickness of the <term>medium</term> <note>In this context, the "medium" refers to the air or atmosphere through which we see.</note> placed between it and the eye.

### *Of the backgrounds that suit shadows and highlights.* CHAPTER 137.

**The** backgrounds that are suited to shadows and highlights, and to the illuminated and shadowed edges of any color, will create a greater separation from one another if they are more varied. That is, a dark color should not end against another dark color, but against something very different, such as white—or something partaking in white—just as you should place dark objects against light ones, or those tending toward dark.

### *How one must remedy the situation when white ends against white, and dark against dark.* CHAPTER 138.

**When** the color of a white body happens to end against a white background, the whites will either be equal or they will not. If they are equal, then the one that is closer to you will appear somewhat darker at the edge where it... <note>Leonardo is beginning to explain the technique of "relief," where a painter subtly darkens the edge of a foreground object to make it "pop" against a background of the same color.</note>

### *On the nature of the colors of backgrounds upon which white stands out.*
#### CHAPTER 139.

**A** white object will appear whiter when placed against a darker background, and it will appear darker when placed against a whiter background. We are taught this by the falling of snow; when we see it against the <term>field of the air</term> <note>the open sky</note>, it appears dark to us. But when we see it against the opening of a window, through which the darkness of the house's shadow is visible, then that same snow will appear extremely white. 

Furthermore, snow appears to move quickly when near to us, but slowly from a distance; when close, it appears as a continuous mass, like white cords, whereas from afar it appears discontinuous <note>Leonardo is describing the visual effect of motion blur—how falling flakes look like solid lines when they zip past the eye.</note>.

### *On the backgrounds of figures.* 
#### CHAPTER 140.

**Of** things of equal brightness, the one seen against a whiter background will appear to have less brightness; and that which stands out against a darker space will appear whiter. For example, a <term>flesh-tone</term> <note>original: "incarnata"; the colors used to paint skin.</note> will appear pale on a red background, but the same pale tone will appear reddish when seen against a yellow background. In the same way, colors will be judged to be what they are not, because of the backgrounds that surround them.

### *On the backgrounds of painted objects.* 
#### CHAPTER 141.

**The** discussion of backgrounds, in which opaque bodies <note>solid objects that do not let light through</note> clothed in shadows and lights are set, is of the greatest importance. For these, it is appropriate to have the illuminated parts set against dark backgrounds, and the dark parts against light backgrounds, as is demonstrated by the figure shown below.

<note>This principle is the foundation of "chiaroscuro," the use of strong contrasts between light and dark to give the illusion of three-dimensional volume on a flat surface.</note>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s technical advice on visual perception and the behavior of light and shadow, specifically focusing on how distance and environmental factors like air and water alter the appearance of colors. It follows a discussion on how backgrounds influence the perceived brightness of an object.</meta>

<header>A TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

### *Of those who represent objects in the countryside as becoming darker the further away they are.*
->C A P. C X L I I.<-

**There** are many who, in an open landscape, make figures appear darker the further they are from the eye. This is actually the opposite of the truth, unless the object being imitated happens to be white; for in that case, the rule proposed below would apply.

### *On the colors of things remote from the eye.*
->C A P. C X L I I I.<-

**The** <term>air</term> <note>Leonardo is discussing "aerial perspective," the effect the atmosphere has on how we see distant objects.</note> tints objects it separates from the eye with its own color in proportion to its thickness. Therefore, if the air separating the eye from a dark object is two miles thick, it will tint that object more than air that is only one mile thick. Here the adversary responds, saying that in landscapes, trees of the same species appear darker from a distance than from nearby. This is not true if the plants are of equal size and separated by equal spaces. However, it is true if the first trees are sparse, allowing the brightness of the meadows between them to be seen, while the distant trees are dense, as happens near the banks of rivers; in that case, no space of bright meadow is visible, but the trees appear joined together, casting shadows upon one another. 

Furthermore, it happens that the shaded part of a plant is much larger than the illuminated part, and through the <term>visual species</term> <note>original: "spetie"; a Renaissance optical term referring to the "images" or "likenesses" that objects were thought to radiate through the air to the eye.</note> that the plant sends to the eye, these shadows are visible at a great distance. The dark color, being present in greater quantity, maintains its "species" better than the less-dark parts; thus, the mixture carries with it the most powerful part over a longer distance.

### *The standards of painting.*
->C A P. C X L I V.<-

**That** which is beautiful is not always good. I say this for those painters who love the beauty of colors so much that they—not without a great lapse in judgment—give them only the weakest and almost imperceptible shadows, disregarding their <term>relief</term> <note>original: "rilieuo"; the quality of appearing three-dimensional or "popping out" from the flat surface.</note>. In this error, they are like those who speak eloquently but without any substance.

### *On the reflection and color of seawater seen from different perspectives.*
->C A P. C X L V.<-

**The** surging sea has no universal color. He who sees it from the mainland sees it as a dark color, and it becomes darker the closer it is to the horizon; one sees certain glimmers or highlights moving slowly, like white sheep in a flock. But he who sees the sea from the high ocean sees it as blue. This happens because from the shore, the sea appears dark because you see the waves reflecting the darkness of the earth; from the high sea, they appear blue because you see the blue of the air reflected in those waves.

### *On the nature of contrasts.*
->C A P. C X L V I.<-

**Black** garments make the flesh of human figures appear whiter than it truly is, while white garments make the flesh appear dark, and eve-

### *On the color of the shadow of any body.* CAP. CXLVII.

The color of a <term>shadow</term> <note>Leonardo often uses "ombra" to mean both the absence of light and the tonal value used in painting.</note> cast by any body will never be its true or proper color unless the object that casts the shadow is the same color as the body being shadowed. For example, suppose I have a room where the walls are green; I say that if a blue object is seen there, illuminated by a blue light, then that illuminated part will be a most beautiful <term>blue</term>, but the shadow will be ugly and not a true shadow of that beautiful blue, because it is corrupted by the green light reflecting from the walls. It would be even worse if the wall were <term>tawny</term> <note>original: "tanè"; a dark, brownish-orange or yellowish-brown color.</note>.

### *On the perspective of colors in dark places.* CAP. CXLVIII.

In places that are dimly and uniformly lit, even unto total darkness, that color will appear darkest which is most remote from the eye.

### *Perspective of colors.* CAP. CXLIX.

Primary colors <note>In this context, Leonardo refers to the colors as they appear in the foreground before being altered by distance.</note> ought to be simple, and the degrees of their fading must agree with the degrees of their distance. That is, the size of objects will participate more in the nature of the <term>vanishing point</term> <note>original: "punto"; the point in perspective where all lines converge.</note> the closer they are to it, and the colors must participate more in the color of the horizon the closer they are to it.

### *On colors.* CAP. CL.

The color found between the shaded part and the illuminated part of solid bodies will be of lesser beauty than the color that is entirely illuminated. Therefore, the primary beauty of colors is found in the principal highlights.

### *From what the blue of the air arises.* CAP. CLI.

The <term>blue</term> of the air arises from the thickness of the body of illuminated air interposed between the darkness above <note>Leonardo correctly intuited that space beyond our atmosphere is dark.</note> and the earth. Air in itself has no quality of smell, taste, or color, but it takes into itself the likenesses of the things placed beyond it. The blue will be more beautiful the greater the darkness behind the air, provided the air is not of too great a space nor of too great a density of moisture. This is seen in the mountains, which have deeper shadows; they appear as a more beautiful <term>blue</term> at long distances, whereas where the mountain is more illuminated, it shows more of the mountain’s own color than the blue tint of the air interposed between it and the eye.

### *On colors.* CAP. CLII.

Among colors that are not <term>blue</term>, that which at a long distance will participate most in blue is the one that is closest to black; and conversely, that color which is most unlike black will maintain its own proper color over a long distance. Thus, the green of the fields will transform more into a blu— <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("azzurro") as it reaches the end of the page.</note>

<sig>E iiij</sig>
<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, painting, shadow, blue, perspective, colors, air, light</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains how shadows are "corrupted" by reflections from nearby colored surfaces and describes the optical phenomenon of "aerial perspective," where distant objects take on a blue hue due to the atmosphere.</summary>
<keywords>color theory, atmospheric perspective, shadows, light reflection, blue sky, optics, painting techniques</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s discourse on the behavior of light and color. The opening fragment concludes a discussion on how different colors (green, red, yellow, white, and blue) are affected by environmental reflections or atmospheric conditions.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
->TRATTATO DELLA PITTVRA<-

...blue, which yellow or white do not do; and conversely, yellow and white are less transformed than green and red.

### *On colors.* CAP. CLIII.

COLORS placed in shadows will participate more or less in their natural beauty depending on whether they are in greater or lesser darkness. But if colors are situated in a luminous space, then they will show much greater beauty as the light is of greater splendor. An opponent might say: "The varieties of colors in the shadows are as many as the varieties of colors of the objects being shadowed." To this I say: colors placed in shadows will show less variety among themselves the darker the shadows are. This is witnessed by those who look from the public squares into the doors of shadowy <term>temples</term> <note>Leonardo frequently uses "tempio" (temple) to refer to churches or large public buildings.</note>, where paintings dressed in various colors appear entirely clothed in darkness.

### *On the backgrounds of figures of painted bodies.* CAP. CLIV.

The <term>background</term> <note>original: "campo"; the field or background space behind a subject.</note> that surrounds the figures of any painted thing must be darker than the illuminated part of those figures, and lighter than the shadowed part.

### *Why white is not a color.* CAP. CLV.

White is not a color, but is in a state of potential, receptive to every color. When it is in the open countryside, all its shadows are blue; and this arises from the fourth proposition, which says: "The surface of every <term>opaque body</term> <note>A solid object that does not let light pass through it.</note> participates in the color of its object <note>Here, "object" refers to the surrounding environment reflecting onto the surface.</note>." Therefore, such white being deprived of sunlight by the interposition of some object placed between the sun and that white surface, the whole of the white remains seeing the sun and the air, participating in the color of the sun and the air; and that part not seen by the sun remains in shadow, participating in the color of the air. And if that white did not see the greenery of the countryside as far as the horizon, nor see the whiteness of that horizon, without doubt that white would appear to be the simple color of the air.

### *On colors.* CAP. CLVI.

The light of a fire tinges everything in yellow; but this will not appear to be true except when compared to things illuminated by the <term>air</term> <note>Leonardo uses "aria" (air) here to mean natural daylight or the atmosphere.</note>. This comparison can be seen near the end of the day, or certainly after the dawn, and also in a dark room where a <term>vent</term> <note>original: "spiracolo"; a small opening or slit that allows a beam of light to enter.</note> of daylight falls upon the object, along with a vent of candlelight; in such a place, their differences will certainly be seen clearly and distinctly. But without such a comparison, their difference will never be known, except in colors that have the most similarity; they will be recognized, however, such as white from yellow, or light green from blue; because the yellow light illuminating the blue is like mixing blue and yellow together, which composes a beautiful green; and if you then mix yellow with green, it becomes much more beautiful.

<summary>Leonardo discusses how shadows diminish color variety, the ideal tonal balance for backgrounds, and how white surfaces reflect atmospheric blue. He also explains how firelight creates a yellow cast that is most visible when compared directly to daylight.</summary>
<keywords>color theory, shadows, background contrast, white as receptive, atmospheric reflection, firelight vs daylight, optical comparison</keywords>

# BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.

## On the colors of incident and reflected lights. Ch. CLVII.

When two lights surround an <term>opaque body</term> <note>original: "corpo ombroso"; a solid object that casts a shadow.</note>, they can only vary in two ways: that is, they will either be of equal power, or they will be unequal. Speaking of the lights themselves: if they are equal, they can vary in two other ways, namely according to their <term>splendor</term> <note>original: "splendore"; the brightness or intensity of the light hitting the surface.</note> upon the object, which will be either equal or unequal. It will be equal when they are at an equal distance; unequal, at unequal distances. At equal distances, they will vary in two other ways: that is, the object situated at an equal distance between two lights equal in color and splendor can be illuminated by those lights in two ways: either equally from every side, or unequally. It will be equally illuminated by those lights when the space remaining around the two lights is of equal color, darkness, and clarity; they will be unequal when those spaces around the two lights are varied in darkness.

## On the colors of shadow. Ch. CLVIII.

It often happens that the shadow of opaque bodies does not match the color of the lights; the shadows will appear <term>greenish</term> <note>original: "verdeggianti"</note> and the lights <term>reddish</term> <note>original: "rosseggianti"</note>, even though the body is of a uniform color. This happens when the light comes from the east onto the object and illuminates the object with the color of its own brightness; then, from the west, there is another object illuminated by that same light, which is of a different color than the first object. Thus, with its <term>reflected lights</term> <note>original: "lumi riflessi"</note>, it bounces back toward the east and strikes the parts of the first object facing it with its rays; these rays intersect and remain fixed together with their colors and brightness. I have often seen a white object with red lights and <term>bluish</term> <note>original: "azzurreggianti"</note> shadows; and this happens on <term>snowy mountains</term> <note>original: "montagne di neue"</note> when the sun sets on the horizon and appears fiery red.

<note>Translator's note: There is significant foxing and a large liquid stain on the bottom right margin of the page, though it does not obscure any text or the diagram.</note>

### *On things placed against a light background, and why such practice is useful in painting.*

->C H A P. CLIX.<-

When an <term>opaque body</term> <note>original: "corpo ombroso"; a solid object that blocks light and creates a shadow.</note> is bordered by a light and illuminated <term>background</term> <note>original: "campo"; the field or area surrounding a figure.</note>, it will necessarily appear <term>detached</term> <note>original: "spiccato"; standing out or appearing three-dimensional.</note> and distant from that background. This occurs because bodies with a curved surface inevitably become shadowed on the side opposite to where they are not struck by light rays, as that area is deprived of those rays. Because of this, the object contrasts greatly with the background. Furthermore, the illuminated part of that body will never meet the illuminated background with its maximum brightness; rather, between the background and the primary light of the body, a boundary edge of the body is positioned which is darker than both the background and the light of the body respectively.

...the other, and thus opposites, through one another, always show themselves to be more powerful.

### *On colors that result from the mixture of other colors, which are called secondary species.* CHAPTER CLXI.

**OF** the simple <term>colors</term> <note>original: "semplici colori"; what we might today call primary colors, though Leonardo’s list includes white and black.</note> the first is white, although philosophers do not accept either white or black in the number of colors, because one is the cause of colors and the other is their deprivation. But because the painter cannot do without these, we will place them in the count of the others, and we shall say that in this order white is the first among the simple colors, yellow the second; green the third, blue the fourth, red the fifth, and black the sixth. And we will set white for the light, without which no color can be seen; yellow for the earth; green for the water; blue for the air; and red for the fire; and black for the darkness that stands above the element of fire, because there is no matter or thickness there where the rays of the sun have to penetrate and strike, and consequently illuminate. 

If you wish to briefly see the variety of all compound colors, take colored glass, and through it look at all the colors of the countryside that are seen beyond it; and thus you will see all the colors of the things seen through such glass to be all mixed with the color of the aforementioned glass, and you will see which color is enhanced or spoiled by such a mixture. If the glass is yellow in color, I say that the <term>species</term> <note>original: "specie"; in this context, the visual image or appearance of an object.</note> of the objects that pass through it to the eye can either worsen or improve. This worsening in such a glass color will happen to blue, black, and white above all others, and the improvement will happen in yellow and green above all others; and thus you will go on scanning with your eye the mixtures of colors, which are infinite. In this way, you will make a choice of nine inventions of mixed and compound colors, and the same can be done with two glasses of various colors placed before the eye, and thus you may continue on your own.

### *On colors.* CHAPTER CLXII.

**BLUE** and green are not simple in themselves, because blue is composed of light and darkness, such as the color of the air—that is, a most perfect black and a most brilliant white. Green is composed of a simple and a compound color; that is, it is composed of blue and yellow.

**THE** mirrored object always participates in the color of the body that reflects it, and the <term>mirror</term> <note>original: "specchio"; here referring to any reflective surface, not just a glass mirror.</note> is partially tinted by the color it reflects; each participates in the other more or less according to whether the thing being reflected is more or less powerful than the color of the mirror, and that thing will appear of a more powerful color in the mirror the more it shares in the color of that mirror itself.

**AMONG** the colors of bodies, that which will be seen at the greatest distance will be the one of the most splendid whiteness. Therefore, that which is of the greatest darkness will be seen at the shortest distance.

**BETWEEN** bodies of equal whiteness and distance from the eye, that one will show itself to be whiter which is surrounded by greater darkness; and conversely, that darkness will show itself to be more somber which is seen against a more brilliant whiteness.

<summary>Leonardo defines his six primary colors and links them to the four elements, while explaining how to study color theory using colored glass. He also discusses how reflections and distances affect color perception and how contrast heightens the intensity of light and dark.</summary>

**OF** colors of equal quality, that one will show itself to be of greater excellence which is seen in the company of its direct opposite: pale with red, black with white—though neither of these is strictly a color—blue with yellow, and green with red. This is because every color is better recognized in its opposite than in its similar, just as darkness is best seen against light, and light against darkness.

**THAT** thing which is seen in dark or misty air, if it is white, will appear to be of a larger size than it truly is. This happens because, as was stated above, a light object expands when placed against a dark field, for the reasons previously assigned.

**THE** medium that exists between the eye and the thing seen transforms that thing into its own color; thus, the blue-tinted air causes distant mountains to appear blue. Red glass causes everything the eye sees through it to appear red; and the light that the stars emit around themselves is taken up by the darkness of the night that is found between the eye and the illumination of those stars.

**THE** true color of any body will show itself in that part which is not occupied by any quality of shadow, nor by a highlight, if it is a polished body.

**I SAY** that white which ends against a dark field makes the dark appear blacker at those edges, and the white appear more brilliant.

### *Of the color of mountains.* CHAPTER CLXIII.

**THAT** mountain will appear to the eye to be of a more beautiful blue which is naturally darker in itself; and that one will be darker which is higher and more heavily forested. This is because such woods cover many shrubs on their lower side, so that they are not seen by the sky; furthermore, the wild plants of the woods are inherently darker than domestic ones. Oaks, beeches, firs, cypresses, and pines are much darker than domestic trees and olives. That brightness of the air which is interposed between the eye and the blackness <note>In this context, Leonardo often uses "black" or "dark" to refer to deep shadows within dense foliage.</note> will appear as a more beautiful blue the thinner it is at the great height of the summit, and vice versa. And that plant will appear less distinct from its background when it ends against a field of a color more similar to its own, and vice versa. That part of a white object will appear most brilliant which is closest to the border of the black; and thus, those parts which are more remote from the dark will appear less white. And that part of the black will appear darkest which is closest to the white, and it will appear less dark the further it is from that white.

### *How the painter must put the perspective of colors into practice.* CHAPTER CLXIII.

**IN** wanting to put into practice this perspective of varying, losing, or indeed diminishing the proper essence of colors, you shall take objects placed in the countryside at intervals of one hundred <term>braccia</term> <note>original: "braccia"; an Italian unit of measurement roughly equal to a "cubit" or 23 inches (about 58 cm). One hundred braccia is approximately 60 yards.</note>, such as trees, houses, men, and sites. Regarding the first tree, you shall have a sheet of glass held very steady, and let your eye be equally steady; and on said glass, draw a tree over the form of that natural one. Then, move it sideways until the natural tree is almost side-by-side with your drawing. Then, color your drawing in such a way that, in color and form, one stands in comparison to the other—so that if you close one eye, both appear painted—and let it be...

He provides specific observations on the blue hue of distant mountains and a practical method using glass to master atmospheric perspective.</summary>

said glass at a single distance: and use this same rule for the second and third trees, at intervals of one hundred *braccia* <term>braccia: an Italian Renaissance unit of length based on the arm, approximately 23 inches</term> each; and let these serve as your assistants and masters, always employing them in your works where they are required, and they will make the work recede into the distance effectively. But I find by the rule that the second object diminishes by half <note>original: "÷"; Leonardo is describing the proportional reduction of objects as they double in distance</note> of the first, when it is twenty *braccia* distant from the first.

## _Of aerial perspective._ **CHAP. CLXV.**

THERE IS another kind of perspective, which is called aerial, because through the variations of the air one can recognize the different distances of various buildings that end at their bases on a single line—as would be the case when seeing many buildings beyond a wall, so that they all appear above the edge of said wall at the same size, and you wished in a painting to make one seem further away than the other. One must imagine a somewhat thick atmosphere. You know that in such an atmosphere, the furthest things seen in it, such as mountains, because of the great quantity of air found between your eye and said mountains, appear blue, almost the color of the air when the sun is in the east. Therefore, you shall make the first building above said wall in its own color; make the more distant one less defined in its profile and bluer; and that which you wish to be just as much further away, make it just as much bluer; and that which you want to be five times further away, make it five times bluer. This rule will ensure that the buildings located above a single line will appear to be of the same size, and it will be clearly understood which is more distant and which is larger than the other.

---

[An engraving illustrating aerial perspective. It shows a landscape with various classical structures—a dome, an obelisk, a tall tower, and ruins. The structures are arranged to demonstrate how objects of similar height appear at different depths, with the furthest elements becoming lighter and less distinct against a mountainous background.]

## _Of the various accidents and movements of man, and the proportion of limbs._ **CHAP. CLXVI.**

THE measurements of a man vary in each limb as he bends it more or less, and from different viewpoints, diminishing or increasing more or less on one side as they increase or decrease on the opposite side.

### *Of the changes in the measurements of man from his birth to his final growth.* CHAP. CLXVII.

A MAN in his first infancy has a shoulder width equal to the length of the face, and to the space from the joint of these shoulders to the elbow when the arm is bent; and it is similar to the space from the thumb of the hand to the said elbow; and it is similar to the space from the root of the member <note>original: "nascimento della verga"; Leonardo uses the genitals as a central anatomical landmark for proportional measurements</note> to the middle of the knee; and it is similar to the space from the joint of the knee to the joint of the foot. But when a man has reached his final height, every aforementioned space doubles its length, except for the length of the face, which, together with the size of the whole head, shows little variation. For this reason, a man who has finished his growth and is well-proportioned is ten of his faces tall, and the width of his shoulders is two of these faces, and thus all the other lengths mentioned above are two of these faces; the rest shall be discussed in the general measurement of man.

### *How infants have joints contrary to men in their thicknesses.* CHAP. CLXVIII.

SMALL children <note>original: "putti piccioli"</note> all have thin joints, while the spaces located between one joint and another are thick. This happens because the skin over the joints lies alone without other flesh, being of the nature of a tendon <note>original: "neruo"; in Renaissance anatomy, this could refer to nerves, tendons, or ligaments</note> that cinches and binds the bones together; and the fleshy humors <term>humors: refers to the bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed in the Renaissance to govern health and physical form</term> are found between one joint and the other, enclosed between the skin and the bone. But because bones are thicker at the joints than between the joints, as a man grows, the flesh begins to lose that superfluity that sat between the skin and the bone, whereby the skin draws closer to the bone and makes the limbs thinner. However, over the joints, there being nothing but the cartilaginous and tendinous skin, it cannot dry out, and not drying out, it does not diminish. For these reasons, infants are thin at the joints and thick between them, as seen in the joints of the fingers, arms, and shoulders, which are thin and concave <note>referring to the "dimples" seen on infants</note>; whereas a man, by contrast, is thick in all the joints of the arms and legs; and where infants have hollows, men have relief.

### *Of the difference in measurement between infants and men.* CHAP. CLXIX.

BETWEEN men and infants, I find a great difference in the length from one joint to another; for a man has two heads <note>Leonardo uses the "head" or "face" as a standard unit of measure</note> between the shoulder joint and the elbow, and from the elbow to the tip of the thumb, and from one shoulder to the other; whereas the infant has only one. This is because nature first composes the size of the house of the intellect <note>the skull/brain</note> before that of the vital spirits <term>vital spirits: the life-force believed to be generated in the heart and distributed through the arteries to give the body movement and heat</term>.

### *Of the joints of the fingers.* CHAP. CLXX.

THE fingers of the hand thicken at their joints from all sides when they bend; and the more they bend, the more they thicken; and likewise

...diminish the more they are straightened; the same occurs with the toes, and they will vary all the more as they are more fleshy.

### *Of the shoulder joints and their expansion.* Chap. CLXXI.

The joints of the shoulders and of the other flexible limbs will be discussed in their proper place in the treatise on anatomy, where the causes of the movements of all the parts of which man is composed are shown. <note>Leonardo frequently references his lost or projected "Treatise on Anatomy," which served as the scientific foundation for his artistic principles.</note>

### *Of the shoulders.* Chap. CLXXII.

The simple principal movements are the bending made by the shoulder joint—that is, when the arm attached to it moves up or down, or backward—though one might say such movements are infinite. For if one turns the shoulder toward a wall and marks a circular figure with the arm, one will have performed all the movements that exist in said shoulder. Because every continuous quantity is infinitely divisible, and such a circle is a continuous quantity created by the motion of the arm, which motion would not produce a continuous quantity if the movement itself did not lead it. Therefore, the motion of that arm has passed through all parts of the circle, and the circle being infinitely divisible, the variations of the shoulders are infinite.

### *Of the universal measurements of bodies.* Chap. CLXXIII.

I say that universal measurements of bodies should be observed in the lengths of the figures and not in their thicknesses, because among the laudable and marvelous things that appear in the works of nature is that never, in any species, does one individual precisely resemble another. Therefore, you, as an imitator of such nature, look and attend to the variety of features. It pleases me well that you avoid monstrous things, such as long legs with short torsos, narrow chests and long arms; take, therefore, the measurements of the joints and the thicknesses in which nature varies greatly, and you shall vary your own as well.

### *Of the measurements of the human body and the bending of limbs.* Chap. CLXXIV.

Necessity compels the painter to have knowledge of the supporting bones, the framework for the flesh that rests upon them, and of the joints which increase and decrease during their bending. For this reason, the measurement of the extended arm is not the same as the measurement of the bent arm. The arm increases and decreases between the variation of its final extension and its bending by one-eighth of its length. The lengthening and shortening of the arm comes from the bone that projects out of the joint of the arm <note>This refers to the olecranon, the bony point of the elbow.</note>, which, as you see in figure A. B., becomes long from the shoulders to the elbow when the angle of that elbow is less than a right angle; it increases as much as that angle decreases, and decreases as much as the aforementioned angle becomes greater. And the space from the shoulder to the elbow increases as the angle of the bend of that elbow becomes smaller than a right angle, and decreases as it becomes larger than a right angle.

<sig>F iv</sig>

<note>original: "Lionardo da Vinci, pittura, notomia, giunture, spalle, membra, proporzione, anatomia artistica, braccio, gomito"</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanics of the shoulder and elbow joints, noting how limbs change in length during movement and emphasizing that painters must study anatomy to avoid repetitive or "monstrous" proportions.</summary>

### *Of the proportionality of the limbs.* Chap. CLXXV.

All the parts of any animal should correspond to its whole; that is, one who is short and thickset must have every limb within themselves short and thick, and one who is long and slender should have long and slender limbs, and the average man should have limbs of that same average quality. I intend for the same to be said of plants that have not been <note>Leonardo suggests that nature has a perfect plan for proportion that is only disrupted by external forces.</note> stunted by man or by the winds, because these put forth new growth over old wood, and thus their natural proportionality is destroyed.

### *Of the joint of the hands with the arm.* Chap. CLXXVI.

The joint of the arm with its hand diminishes when clenching, and thickens when the hand is opened; the arm between the elbow and the hand does the opposite from every perspective. This occurs because in opening the hand, the <term>inner muscles (muscoli domestici)</term> <note>original: "muscoli domestici" — Leonardo uses "domestic" to refer to the muscles on the inner or palm side of the limb (flexors).</note> extend and thin the arm between the elbow and the hand; and when the hand clenches, the inner and <term>outer muscles (muscoli silvestri)</term> <note>original: "muscoli silvestri" — literally "sylvan" or "wild" muscles; Leonardo uses this to refer to the muscles on the outer or back side of the limb (extensors).</note> retract and thicken. However, the outer muscles only move away from the bone because they are pulled by the bending of the hand.

### *Of the joints of the feet, and their thickening and thinning.* Chap. CLXXVII.

Only the thinning and increasing of the foot joint occurs in the view of its outer part, marked D. E. F. <note>These letters refer to a specific anatomical diagram, likely the engraving included on the page.</note> which increases when the angle of such a joint becomes more acute, and diminishes as much as the angle becomes more obtuse; that is, we are speaking of the front joints A. C. B.

<summary>Leonardo explains that limb proportions must match the overall body type and describes how the musculature of the wrist and ankle changes size during movement.</summary>
<keywords>proportionality, anatomy, wrist joint, musculature, flexors, extensors, foot joints, Leonardo da Vinci, biomechanics</keywords>

### *Of the limbs that decrease when they bend, and increase when they extend. <term>Chapter CLXXVIII.</term>*

**A**mong the limbs that have flexible joints, only the knee is that which decreases in its thickness when bending, and increases when extending.

### *Of the limbs that thicken at their joint when they bend. <term>Chapter CLXXIX.</term>*

**A**ll the limbs of man thicken at the folds of their joints <note>Leonardo is observing how skin and muscle bunch together when a joint like the elbow or finger is flexed.</note>, except for the joint of the leg <note>By "joint of the leg," Leonardo refers specifically to the knee, as clarified in the preceding chapter.</note>.

### *Of the limbs of naked men. <term>Chapter CLXXX.</term>*

**T**he limbs of naked men who exert themselves in various actions should only reveal their muscles on the side where those muscles move the limb for the task; and the other limbs should have their muscles more or less pronounced according to how much or how little they are exerted. <note>This is a key instruction for artists to avoid "anatomical exaggeration," where every muscle is shown bulging regardless of whether it is actually in use.</note>

### *Of the powerful movements of the human limbs. <term>Chapter CLXXXI.</term>*

**T**hat arm will have a more powerful and longer movement which, having been removed from its natural position, has a more powerful attachment to the other limbs to pull it back to the position where it wishes to move. Like the man A, who moves the arm with the stroke E, and brings it to the opposite position by moving with his whole person to B. <note>Leonardo is describing the mechanical advantage gained by using the weight and momentum of the entire body rather than just the strength of the arm alone.</note>

## *On the movement of man.* <term>Chapter CLXXXII.</term>

**T**he highest and most principal part of art is the investigation into the composition of all things; the second part deals with movements, requiring that attention be paid to their actions. These should be performed with a readiness suited to the condition of the subjects, whether in a state of lethargy or intense activity. Furthermore, the intensity of ferocity must be of the highest caliber required by the one performing the action. For instance, when one must throw darts, stones, or similar objects, the figure should display its full preparation for such an action. Shown here are two figures in different modes of action and potential: the first in terms of effectiveness is figure A, and the second is movement B. <note>In the accompanying engraving, figure A illustrates the superior mechanical stance for throwing, while figure B shows an inefficient one.</note> However, figure A will cast the object further from himself than figure B. This is because, even though both appear to intend to throw their weight toward the same target, figure A, having his feet pointed toward the target as he twists and bends away from it into an opposing position—where he prepares his power—is able to return with speed and ease to the point where he releases the weight from his hands. Conversely, figure B, having the toes of his feet pointed in the opposite direction from the intended target, twists toward that target with great difficulty. Consequently, the result is weak, and the motion suffers from its flawed cause. For the preparation of force in any movement should involve twists and bends of great intensity <note>original: "violenza." Leonardo uses this term to describe physical tension or mechanical strain, like a spring being wound.</note>, while the return stroke should be fluid and natural. In this way, the action achieves its full effect. Just as with a crossbow, if it is not drawn with great tension, the motion...

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanics of physical force, demonstrating through two figures that maximum power in a throw is achieved by twisting the body away from the target while keeping the feet planted toward it.</summary>
<keywords>biomechanics, human movement, physics of throwing, artistic composition, torque, physical tension, Leonardo da Vinci, Treatise on Painting</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion from page 50 regarding the mechanical advantages of Figure A's posture over Figure B's when throwing a projectile, and transitions into general rules for variety in artistic composition.</meta>

<page-num>51</page-num>
->OF LEONARDO DA VINCI.<-

of the object moved by him will be short, or nothing at all: because where there is no dissipation of force <note>original: "violenza"; here Leonardo uses the term to describe the potential energy or tension stored in the body's twist</note> there is no motion, and where there is no force, it cannot be destroyed. For this reason, a bow that has no tension cannot create motion unless it acquires that tension, and in acquiring it, its shape will vary from its natural state. Likewise, the man who does not twist or bend has not acquired power. Therefore, when Figure A has thrown his dart, he will find himself twisted and weak in the direction toward which he threw the object, having acquired a potential <note>original: "potenza"</note> that serves only to return him in the opposite motion.

### On postures, movements, and their limbs. <term>Chapter CLXXXIII.</term>

The same movements should not be repeated in the same figure, neither in its limbs, nor hands, nor fingers; nor should the same postures be repeated within a narrative painting <term>original: "historia"; the Renaissance term for a complex scene depicting a historical, biblical, or mythological event</term>. And if the narrative were very large, such as a battle or a slaughter of soldiers, where there are only three ways of striking—namely, a thrust, a backhand blow, and a downward slash—in this case, you must take care that all the downward slashes are shown from various viewpoints. For example, some might be turned backward, some seen from the side, and some from the front; and likewise for all the other views of those same three postures. By this method, we can represent all the others as participating in one of these three. However, "composite motions" <term>original: "moti composti"</term> in battles are signs of great skill, vitality, and movement. Those are called composite which a single figure demonstrates to you—for instance, if the legs are seen from the front, while the shoulders are seen partly in profile. These will be discussed in another place.

## *On the joints of the limbs. Chapter CLXXXIV.*

In the joints of the limbs and the variety of their bends, one must consider how the flesh increases on one side while it diminishes on the other. This is particularly observable in the necks of animals, because their movements are of three natures. Two of these are simple, and one is a compound motion that participates in both simple ones. Of these simple motions, the first occurs when the neck bends toward one shoulder or the other, or when it raises or lowers the head resting upon it. The second is when the neck twists to the right or left without curving, remaining straight while the face is turned toward one of the shoulders. The third motion, which is called "compound," is when a twisting is added to its bending—as when the ear inclines toward one of the shoulders and the face turns toward that same side, or toward the opposite shoulder with the face turned toward the sky.

## *On the proportioning of man. Chapter CLXXXV.*

Measure the proportions of your own body <term>original: "membrificatione"; refers to the structural arrangement of limbs</term> on a canvas, and if you find it discordant in any part, take note of it. You must guard yourself strictly against using those same proportions in the figures you compose, because it is a common vice among painters to delight in making figures that resemble themselves. <note>Leonardo frequently warned that artists often unconsciously project their own physical defects or features onto their subjects, leading to a lack of variety.</note>

## *On the movements of human limbs. Chapter CLXXXVI.*

All limbs should perform the specific office for which they were intended. That is, in dead or sleeping figures, no limb should appear alive or awake. Likewise, a foot that receives the weight of a man should appear flattened, not with playful toes, unless it is resting only upon the heel.

## *On the movements of the parts of the face. Chapter CLXXXVII.*

The movements of the parts of the face, caused by "mental accidents" <term>original: "accidenti mentali"; internal emotional states that "happen" to the mind and manifest physically</term>, are many. The principal ones are laughing, crying, shouting, singing in various high and low voices, admiration, anger, joy, melancholy, fear, pain, and the like. We shall mention these, starting first with laughter and weeping, which are very similar in the shape of the mouth and the cheeks and the closing of the eyes, varying only in the eyebrows and the space between them. We will discuss all of this in its proper place—namely, the variations taken by the face, the hands, and the whole person for each of these "accidents." Knowledge of these is necessary for you, the painter; otherwise, your art will truly show bodies that are "twice dead." <note>A figure that is "twice dead" lacks both physical life and the "life of the mind" (expression).</note> Furthermore, I remind you that movements should not be so wild and frantic that peace appears like a battle or a drunken folk dance <term>original: "moresca"; a stylized, vigorous dance</term>. Above all, ensure that the bystanders in the scene <term>original: "historia"; a narrative or historical painting</term> are intent, with gestures that show wonder, reverence, grief, suspicion, fear, or joy, according to what the specific occasion requires for the gathering or assembly of your figures. And do not place your narrative scenes one above the other on the same wall with different horizons, so that it looks like a mercer's shop with its little boxes arranged in squares.

<summary>This page outlines the mechanics of neck movement, warns artists against repeating their own physical likeness in their work, and emphasizes that gestures and facial expressions must authentically reflect internal emotions.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, neck movement, self-portraiture, emotional expression, mental accidents, composition, narrative painting, realism</keywords>

## On the members and the description of a likeness. CHAPTER CLXXXVIII.

The parts that flank the bulb <note>original: "globo"; referring to the rounded tip of the nose</note> of the nose vary in eight ways: that is, they are either equally straight, equally concave, or equally convex: 1st. Or they are unequally straight, concave, and convex; 2nd. Or they are straight in the upper parts and concave below; 3rd. Or straight above and convex below; 4th. Or concave above and straight below; 5th. Or concave above and convex below; 6th. Or convex above and straight below; 7th. Or convex above and concave below.

The attachment of the nose to the brow is of two kinds: it is either concave or it is straight.

The forehead has three varieties: it is either flat, concave, or full <term>original: "colma"; meaning rounded or protuberant</term>. The flat forehead is divided into two parts: that is, it is either convex in the upper part, or in the lower part, or both above and below, or flat both above and below.

### *How to memorize and create a human likeness in profile with only a single glance. CHAPTER CLXXXIX.*

In this case, you must commit to memory the variety of four different members in profile: the nose, mouth, chin, and forehead. First, we shall speak of noses, which are of three sorts: straight, concave, and convex. Of the straight ones, there are no more than four varieties: long, short, high at the tip, and low. Concave noses are of three kinds, of which some have the concavity in the upper part, some in the middle, and some in the lower part. Convex noses likewise vary in three ways: some have the hump in the upper part, some in the middle, and some below. The protrusions that flank the hump of the nose vary in three ways: they are either straight, concave, or convex.

### *How to memorize the form of a face. CHAPTER CLXXXX.*

If you wish to easily remember the look <term>original: "aria"; referring to the general character or "air" of a person's expression</term> of a face, first learn many types of heads, mouths, eyes, noses, chins, throats, necks, and shoulders; let us take an example. Noses are of ten kinds: straight, humped, hollowed, with a protrusion higher or lower than the middle, aquiline <note>eagle-like, or hooked</note>, snub, round, and sharp. These are good for the profile. From the front, they are of eleven kinds: even, thick in the middle, thin in the middle, the tip thick and thin at the attachment, thin at the tip and thick at the attachment, with wide nostrils, with narrow ones, with high ones, with low ones, with the openings exposed, or with the openings hidden by the tip. And so you will find diversity in other features, which things you must draw from nature and commit to memory. Or, when you must draw a face from memory, carry with you a small notebook where such features are noted. When you have taken a glance at the face of the person you wish to portray, look afterward in private at which nose or mouth resembles them, and make a small mark to recognize it later at home and put it all together.

<sig>G iij</sig>

This early form of "feature mapping" emphasizes the importance of observation and the use of a reference notebook for anatomical accuracy.</summary>

### On the beauty of faces. CHAPTER CLXXXI.

**M**uscles should not be depicted with harsh definitions, but rather let soft lights end imperceptibly in pleasant and delightful shadows; from this arises grace and beauty. <note>Leonardo is advocating for "sfumato," his signature technique of soft, blurred transitions between colors and tones.</note>

### On posture. CHAPTER CLXXXII.

**T**he pit of the throat <note>original: "fontanella della gola"</note> falls directly over the foot [supporting the weight]; if an arm is thrown forward, the pit of the throat moves outside the line of the feet; and if the leg is thrown backward, the pit of the throat moves forward, and thus it changes with every posture.

### *On the movements of the limbs, ensuring they are appropriate to the figure’s actions. CHAPTER CLXXXIII.*

**A** figure whose movement does not match the internal state <note>original: "accidente"; referring to the emotion or intent in the subject's mind</note> imagined to be in the figure's mind, shows that its limbs are not obedient to its own judgment, and that the artist’s judgment is of little value. Therefore, a figure must display great emotion and fervor, showing that its motions can signify nothing other than the purpose for which they were made.

### *On the muscular definition of nudes. CHAPTER CLXXXIV.*

**T**he limbs of nude figures should have their muscles more or less evident according to the greater or lesser exertion of said limbs; show only those muscles that are most employed in the motion or action, and let the one most heavily used be most manifest, while those not used at all remain relaxed and soft.

### *On the motion and gait of man and other animals. CHAPTER CLXXXV.*

**W**hen a man moves with speed or slowness, the part that is above the leg supporting the body will always be lower than the other side.

### *When there is a greater difference in the height of the shoulders in human actions. CHAPTER CLXXXVI.*

**T**hose shoulders or sides of a man (or other animals) will have the greatest difference in height when their overall movement is slowest; the opposite follows: those parts of the animal will have the least difference in their heights when the overall movement is fastest. This is proven by the 9th law of local motion, which states: Every heavy body weighs along the line of its motion. Therefore, when the whole moves toward a certain place, the part joined to it follows the shortest line of the motion of the whole, without exerting weight upon the lateral parts.

### Reply to the contrary. CHAPTER CLXXXVII.

**T**he adversary says, regarding the first part above, that it is not necessary for a man who stands still, or who walks with slow motion, to continuously use the aforementioned balancing <term>original: "ponderatione"; the artistic principle of weight distribution or contrapposto</term> of the limbs over the center of gra—

### *How a folded arm changes a man's entire balance from its initial state when that arm is extended. Chapter CLXXXXVIII.*

The extension of a folded arm moves the person's entire weight distribution <term>ponderatione: Leonardo’s term for the science of balance and the distribution of weight in a body at rest or in motion</term> over the foot that serves as the support <note>original: "sostentacolo"</note> for the whole body. This is similar to what is seen in a tightrope walker who, with arms outstretched, maintains balance without the need for a balancing pole.

### *On man and other animals who, in moving slowly, do not have their center of gravity too far from the center of their supports. Chapter CLXXXXIX.*

An animal will move more slowly the closer the center of its legs (its support) is to the vertical line <note>original: "perpendicolo"</note> of its center of gravity. Conversely, the animal that has its center of support further from the vertical line of its center of gravity will move with greater speed.

### *Of the man who carries a weight upon his shoulders. Chapter CC.*

The shoulder of a man who supports a weight is ALWAYS higher than the shoulder without weight, and this is demonstrated in the figure placed below, through which passes the center line of the entire weight of the man and the weight carried by him. If this combined weight <note>original: "peso composto"</note> were not divided in equal measure over the center of the leg that provides support, the entire mass would necessarily collapse. However, necessity provides that as much of the man's natural weight <term>natural weight: the body's own weight, inherent to its mass</term> is thrown to one side as there is accidental weight <term>accidental weight: an external load added to the body, such as a stone or timber</term> added to the opposite side. This cannot be achieved unless the man bends and lowers himself on his lighter side with enough of a tilt to compensate for the accidental weight he carries; and this cannot be done unless the shoulder bearing the weight is raised, and the unburdened shoulder is lowered. This is the mechanism that artful necessity <note>Leonardo views "necessity" as the mathematical and physical laws that govern the universe, which the artist must "artfully" observe to achieve realism</note> has discovered for such an action.

---

### *Of the man’s balance upon his feet. Chapter CCI.*

The weight of a man who rests upon a single leg will ALWAYS be divided with equal opposing parts over the center of gravity that supports him.

### *Of the man who moves. Chapter CCII.*

The man who moves will have his center of gravity <term>center of gravity: the specific point where the body’s weight is perfectly balanced in all directions</term> over the center of the leg that rests upon the ground. <note>Leonardo is describing the fundamental principle of walking: as we move, we constantly shift our weight so that our vertical axis remains supported by whichever foot is currently planted, preventing a fall.</note>

### *Of the balancing <term>balancing: from the Italian 'bilicatione,' referring to the distribution of weight to achieve equilibrium</term> of the weight of any animal standing still upon its legs.*

#### CHAPTER CCIII.

**The** absence of motion in any animal that rests upon its feet arises from the absence of inequality between the opposing weights that are supported by their bases. <note>Leonardo views movement as a result of "inequality" or imbalance. Therefore, he defines stillness as a state where all opposing forces are perfectly equalized.</note>

### *Of the bendings and turnings of the man. Chap. CCIV.*

**A** man’s side decreases in the bending of one of his sides as much as he increases in his other opposite side; and such a bend will ultimately be <term>subdupla: a mathematical term from the Latin 'subduplus,' meaning a ratio of 1 to 2, or half</term> to the part that extends. And a particular treatise will be made regarding this.

### *Of bendings. Chap. CCV.*

**In** proportion to how much one of the sides of the bendable members is made longer, so much will its opposite part be diminished. The external central line of the sides that do not bend, in bendable limbs, never decreases or increases in its length. <note>Leonardo is observing that while the skin and muscle on the "inside" of a curve compress and the "outside" stretch, the structural midline of the limb remains constant in length.</note>

### *Of equipoise <term>equipoise: 'equiponderantia' in Italian, referring to the distribution of weight to achieve a state of balance or equilibrium</term>. Chap. CCVI.*

**A** figure that supports a weight outside of itself and away from the central line of its mass must always throw as much natural or accidental weight from the opposite side as is necessary to create a balance of weights around the central line. This line starts from the center of the part of the foot that rests upon the ground and passes through the entire mass of the weight above that part of the feet placed on the earth. One naturally sees a person who takes a weight in one of their arms throw out the opposite arm; and if this is not enough to create the balance, he provides even more weight of his own self by bending until he is sufficient to resist the applied weight. One also sees in someone who is about to fall over on one of their sides that they always throw out the arm of the opposite side.

<summary>Leonardo explains the geometric proportions of the body during movement, specifically how one side contracts as the other expands, and details the instinctive physical compensations humans make to maintain balance when carrying heavy objects.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, equilibrium, center of gravity, weight distribution, contrapposto, human mechanics, bending, proportions</keywords>
