<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of human mechanics and equilibrium. It shifts from the internal proportions of a bending body to the external dynamics of how a human moves weights and maintains balance while standing, specifically defining the relationship between the center of gravity and the point of support.</meta>

TREATISE ON PAINTING

### *Of human motion. CHAP. CCVII.*

**WHEN** you wish to depict a man as the mover of any weight, consider that the motions must be made along various lines; that is, either from low to high with a simple motion—as does one who, by bending down, takes up a weight that he wishes to raise by straightening himself—or when he wishes to drag something behind him, or push it forward, or pull downward with a rope passing through a pulley. Here, remember that the weight of the man pulls as much as his center of gravity is outside the center of his support. <note>Leonardo is explaining that to move a heavy object, a person must shift their body weight so that their own gravity acts as a lever.</note> To this is added the force exerted by the legs or the bent back as they straighten.

**NEVER** does one descend or ascend, nor ever walk along any line, without the back foot raising the heel.

### *Of the motion created by the loss of balance. CHAP. CCVIII.*

**THE** motion created by the loss of balance <note>original: "deſtruttione del bilico," literally the destruction of the equilibrium</note>, that is, by inequality: because nothing moves by itself that does not depart from its balance, and that motion becomes faster the further it moves from its said balance.

### *Of the balance of figures. CHAP. CCIX.*

**IF** the figure rests upon one of its feet, the shoulder on the side that rests shall always be lower than the other, and the <term>pit of the throat: the 'fontanella della gola,' the hollow at the base of the neck between the collarbones</term> will be positioned directly above the middle of the leg that supports the weight. The same will occur regardless of the line from which we view the figure, provided it does not have arms extending far out from the body, nor a weight on its back, in its hand, or on its shoulder, nor an extension of the non-supporting leg either forward or backward.

[An engraving depicts a muscular nude male figure standing in <term>contrapposto: a pose where the weight is shifted onto one leg, creating naturalistic curves and a sense of potential movement</term>. He stands between a stone pedestal holding a vessel and a gnarled, broken tree trunk. His body is subtly twisted, illustrating the anatomical balance and weight distribution described in the text.]

<summary>Leonardo explains the physics of human movement, noting that motion is achieved by purposefully losing one's balance and shifting the center of gravity. He also provides a fundamental rule for artists: in a resting figure, the pit of the throat must align vertically with the weight-bearing foot.</summary>
<keywords>human motion, center of gravity, equilibrium, pit of the throat, contrapposto, weight distribution, mechanics of walking, anatomical balance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on human movement, transitioning from the mechanics of balance and weight-bearing to the aesthetic principles of "grace" and "ease" in artistic composition. ->BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.<-

### *Of the grace of the limbs.* Cap. CCX.

The limbs of the body must be arranged with grace to suit the intended effect you want the figure to produce: and if you want to create a figure that displays elegance <note>original: "leggiadria," a term implying light, airy beauty</note>, you must make the limbs slender and extended, without showing too many muscles. Those few muscles that you do find necessary to show, make them soft—that is, with little definition and with shadows that are not harsh. The limbs, and especially the arms, should be "unknotted"—that is, no limb should be in a straight line with the part that connects to it. And if the hip-axis of the man is positioned, as a result of his pose, so that the right side is higher than the left, you shall make the joint of the upper shoulder fall in a perpendicular line over the most prominent point of the hip; that right shoulder should be lower than the left, and the <term>pit of the throat: "fontanella," the hollow at the base of the neck</term> should always be higher than the center of the joint of the foot that supports the weight of the leg. The leg that does not support the weight should have its knee lower than the other, and close to the other leg.

The attitudes of the head and arms are infinite; therefore, I will not expand upon giving any specific rules for them. I will only say that they should be easy and pleasing, with various twists, so that they do not look like pieces of wood.

### *Of the ease of the limbs.* Cap. CCXI.

Regarding the ease <note>original: "commodità," referring to the natural facility or comfort of movement</note> of these limbs, you must consider that when you want to depict someone who, by some accident, has to turn backward or to the side, you must not make them move their feet and all their limbs in the same direction the head turns. Instead, you shall depict this turning motion by distributing it across four joints: that of the foot, the knee, the hip, and the neck. And if the weight rests on the right leg, make the left knee bend inward, and let its foot be raised slightly on the outside; the left shoulder should be somewhat lower than the right, and the nape of the neck should align with the same spot where the outer <term>ankle bone: "noce di fuori," literally the 'nut' of the ankle</term> of the left foot is located. The left shoulder will be positioned perpendicularly over the tip of the right foot. Always ensure that where the figures turn their heads, they do not turn their chests, as nature for our convenience has given us a neck that can easily serve different directions when the eye wishes to turn toward various sites; the other joints are also partially obedient to this same principle. If you depict a man sitting, and his arms must be used for something across his body, make the chest turn above the hip joint.

### *Of a single figure outside of a narrative.* Cap. CCXII.

Furthermore, do not repeat the same motion in the limbs of a figure that you represent alone. For example, if the figure is shown running alone, do not make both hands reach forward; instead, put one forward and the other behind, because otherwise one cannot run. And if the right foot is forward, the right arm should be behind and the left arm forward, because without such a disposition one cannot run well. If another figure is shown following him, who has one leg thrown somewhat forward, make the other leg return under the head, and

### *What are the principal elements of importance belonging to the figure.*
->CHAPTER CCXIII.<-

AMONG the principal important things required in the depiction of animals <note>Leonardo often uses "animals" to refer to all living creatures, including humans, in a biological context</note>, is to situate the head well upon the shoulders, the torso upon the hips, and the hips and shoulders upon the feet.

### *Of balancing the weight around the center of gravity of bodies.*
->CHAPTER CCXIV.<-

A figure that stands without motion upon its feet will distribute equal opposing weights around the center of its support. I say that if the figure is at rest upon its feet, and if it casts one arm forward in front of its chest, it must cast as much natural weight backward as it casts both natural and accidental weight <note>original: "naturale & accidentale"; "natural" weight refers to the body itself, while "accidental" refers to an external weight being carried</note> forward: and I say the same for every part that projects out from the whole beyond the usual.

### *Of figures that have to manage and carry weights.*
->CHAPTER CCXV.<-

A man will never lift or carry a weight without throwing out more than an equal amount of his own weight on the side opposite to where he lifts said weight.

### *Of the attitudes of men.*
->CHAPTER CCXVI.<-

Let the attitudes <note>original: "attitudini," referring to the overall pose or posture of a figure</note> of men be arranged with their limbs in such a way that they demonstrate the intention of their mind.

### *Variety of attitudes.*
->CHAPTER CCXVII.<-

Let the actions in men be expressed according to their age and dignity, and let them vary according to the species—that is, between males and females.

### *Of the attitudes of figures.*
->CHAPTER CCXVIII.<-

I say that the painter must observe the attitudes and movements of men as they arise immediately from any occurrence <note>original: "accidente," meaning a spontaneous or unplanned event</note>; these should be noted or fixed in the mind, rather than waiting for the act of weeping to be performed by someone as a test without a great cause for tears, and then portraying it. For such an act, not being born from a true cause, will be neither prompt nor natural; but it is quite good to have first noted it from a natural occurrence, and then to have someone stand in that pose to see certain specific parts, and then portray it.

### *Of the actions of bystanders at a noteworthy event.*
->CHAPTER CCXIX.<-

All the bystanders of any event worthy of being noted stand with diverse admiring acts to consider that event; as when justice punishes wrongdoers: and if the case is of a devout nature, all the bystanders...

<summary>Leonardo explains the physical laws of balance and center of gravity in the human form, while emphasizing that a painter must capture authentic emotional "attitudes" observed from real-life events rather than staged ones.</summary>

direct their eyes with various acts of devotion toward that event, such as the showing of the host during the sacrifice <note>This refers to the elevation of the Eucharist during the Catholic Mass.</note>, and the like: and if it is an event worthy of laughter, or of weeping, in this case it is not necessary that all the bystanders turn their eyes toward the event, but rather they should react with diverse movements, and a great part of them should rejoice or grieve together: and if the event is frightening, the terrified faces of those who flee should make a great display of fear and flight, with varied movements, as will be discussed in the book of movements <note>Leonardo frequently planned to organize his observations into specific books or treatises; here he refers to his studies on kinetics.</note>.

### *The quality of nudes. CHAPTER CCXX.*

Never make a figure that is lean with muscles in too much relief; because lean men never have much flesh over their bones, but are lean due to a scarcity of flesh, and where there is little flesh, there cannot be thickness of muscles.

### *How muscles are short and thick. CHAPTER CCXXI.*

The muscular have thick bones and are stocky and short men, and they have a scarcity of fat, because the fleshiness of the muscles, by their growth, constricts together, and the fat that is usually interposed between them has no room; and as the muscles in such lean men are entirely squeezed together and cannot expand [outward], they grow in thickness, and they grow most in that part which is furthest from their ends—that is, toward the middle of their width and length.

### *How fat people do not have thick muscles. CHAPTER CCXXII.*

Even though fat people are themselves short and thick, like the aforementioned muscular ones, they have thin muscles, but their skin covers much spongy and empty thickness, that is, full of air <note>Leonardo uses "air" here to describe the lightness or buoyancy of fatty tissue compared to dense muscle.</note>; therefore, fat people support themselves better upon the water than do muscular ones, who have a smaller quantity of air enclosed within their skin.

### *Which muscles disappear in the various movements of man. CHAPTER CCXXIII.*

In the raising and lowering of the arms, the breasts <note>Referring here to the pectoral muscles.</note> disappear, or they stand out in higher relief: the same happens to the relief of the flanks when bending outward or inward; and the shoulders, the hips, and the neck show more variety than any other joint, because they have more variable movements: and regarding this, a specific book will be made.

### *Of muscles. CHAPTER CCXXIV.*

The limbs in youth should not have pronounced muscles, because it is a sign of aged strength, and in young men it is neither the time nor the mature strength: rather, let the definitions of the limbs be pronounced more or less evidently according to how much or how little they have been exerted: and the muscles that are exerted are always higher and thicker than those that remain in—

rest, and the intrinsic central lines of the limbs that bend never remain at their natural length.

## *That a nude depicted with great prominence of the muscles should be without motion.*
### CHAPTER CCXXV.

The nude depicted with great evidence of all its muscles should be motionless, because it cannot move if one part of the muscles does not relax while the opposing muscles pull; and those that relax lose their definition, while those that pull reveal themselves strongly and become evident.

## *That nude figures should not have their muscles overly defined.*
### CHAPTER CCXXVI.

Nude figures should not have their muscles entirely articulated, because they result in being difficult and ungraceful. Depending on the direction a limb turns for its action, let its muscles be more clearly pronounced in that same direction. A muscle often expresses its specific parts through action, in such a way that without such action, they were not previously visible in it.

## *On the broadening and shortening of the muscles.* CHAPTER CCXXVII.

The muscle of the back of the thigh shows greater variety in its extension and contraction than any other muscle in man. The second is that which composes the buttock. The third is that of the back. The fourth is that of the throat. The fifth is that of the shoulders. The sixth is that of the stomach, which originates under the "pomegranate" <note>original: *pomo granato*. Leonardo uses this term for the xiphoid process at the base of the sternum.</note>, and ends under the pubic bone <note>original: *pettignone*. An archaic Italian term for the pubic region.</note>, as will be stated of all of them.

## *Where a "cord" is found in men without muscles.*
### CHAPTER CCXXVIII.

Where the arm ends at the palm of the hand, about four fingers' distance away, one finds a "cord" <term>corda: here referring to a strong ligament or tendon</term> the largest in man, which is without muscle; it originates in the middle of one of the "fucili" <note>original: *fucili del braccio*. Literally "rifles of the arm," Leonardo's term for the two long bones of the forearm, the radius and the ulna.</note> of the arm, and ends in the middle of the other bone, and it has a square shape, and is about three fingers wide and half a finger thick; this serves only to hold the two aforementioned bones of the arm tightly together so that they do not spread apart.

## *On the eight pieces that arise in the middle of the tendons in various joints of man.* CHAPTER CCXXIX.

There arise in the joints of man certain pieces of bone which are fixed in the middle of the tendons <note>original: *corde*.</note> that bind certain joints, such as the kneecaps, and those of the shoulders, and of the feet, which are eight in total: one for each shoulder, one for each knee, and two for each foot under the first joint of the big toes toward the heel; and these become very hard toward a man's old age.

BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.

### *On the muscle that is between the xiphoid process and the pubic bone.* CHAPTER CCXXX.

There originates a muscle between the xiphoid process <note>original: *pomo granato*, literally "pomegranate apple"; a term Leonardo used for the cartilaginous tip of the breastbone.</note> and the pubic bone <note>original: *pettignone*; an archaic term for the pubic region or bone.</note> (I say it ends in the pubic bone) which consists of three "powers" <note>sections or segments</note>. It is divided along its length by three cords <note>tendinous intersections</note>: first is the upper muscle, followed by a cord as wide as the muscle itself; then follows the second muscle lower than this, to which the second cord is joined; finally, the third muscle follows with the third cord, which is joined to the pubic bone. These three stages of three muscles with three cords were created by nature because of the great range of movement a man has when bending and stretching with this muscle. If it were made of a single piece, it would show too much variation in its expansion and contraction as the man bends and stretches. It adds more beauty to the human form to have little variation in this muscle during action; for if the muscle must extend nine inches and then retract just as many, it only amounts to three inches for each [segment of the] muscle. These smaller movements create very little change in their shape and do little to distort the beauty of the body.

### *On the extreme twisting a man can perform to look behind himself.*
->CHAPTER CCXXXI.<-

The extreme twist <note>original: *svoltamento*</note> that a man can perform occurs when his heels are showing behind him while his face is turned forward. This cannot be achieved without difficulty, and only if the leg is bent and the shoulder facing the nape of the neck is lowered. The cause of such a twist, and which muscles move first and last, will be demonstrated in the [section on] anatomy.

I

<page-num>66</page-num>

### *How close one arm can be brought to the other from behind.*
### CHAPTER CCXXXII.

Regarding the arms when they are moved behind the back, the elbows shall never be brought closer together than the point where the longest fingers pass the elbows of the opposite hands. That is to say, the closest proximity the elbows can have behind the small of the back <note>original: *reni*, literally "kidneys," commonly used by Leonardo to refer to the lumbar region.</note> will be equal to the distance from one's elbow to the tip of the longest finger; these arms then form a perfect square. As for how far the arms can cross over the chest, it is such that the elbows meet in the center of the chest; these elbows, along with the shoulders and the arms, form an equilateral triangle.

---

### *On the preparation of force in a man who intends to generate a great strike.*
### CHAPTER CCXXXIII.

When a man prepares to create motion with force, he bends and twists as much as he can in the direction opposite to where he intends to deliver the strike <note>original: *percussione*; this refers to the physical impact of a blow, such as a blacksmith hitting an anvil or a man throwing a stone.</note>. There, he prepares himself with all the strength possible, which he then carries through and releases upon the object he strikes by means of a compound motion <note>original: *moto del composto*; a term describing a movement that involves multiple joints and muscle groups working in sequence to increase power.</note>.

<note>painting, anatomy, arm, elbow, motion, force, strike, perfect square, equilateral triangle</note>

<summary>Leonardo describes the geometric limits of arm placement—forming a square behind the back and a triangle across the chest—and explains the mechanical necessity of "winding up" in the opposite direction to deliver a forceful blow.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, biomechanics, arm proportions, percussion, compound motion, Leonardo da Vinci, artistic theory</keywords>

BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.
<page-num>67</page-num>

### *On the compound force of man, and first we shall speak of the arms.*

### CHAPTER CCXXXIV.

The muscles that move the larger bone of the forearm <note>original: *maggior fucile*; Leonardo uses this term to refer to the radius or ulna, the bones of the lower arm.</note> in the extension and retraction of the arm, originate around the middle of the bone called the *humerus* <note>original: *adiutorio*; a common medieval and Renaissance anatomical term for the upper arm bone.</note>, one behind the other; at the back is born the one that extends the arm, and in front the one that bends it.

If a man is more powerful in pulling than in pushing, it is proven by the ninth proposition of *On Weights* <note>original: *de ponderibus*; this refers to the *Liber de Ponderibus*, a medieval treatise on statics often attributed to Jordanus de Nemore, which Leonardo studied extensively.</note>, where it says: "Among weights of equal power, that which is more remote from the axis <note>original: *polo*; here used to mean the fulcrum or center of rotation.</note> of their balance will prove to be more powerful." It follows that, muscle N. B. and muscle N. C. being of equal power among themselves, the front muscle N. C. is more powerful than the back muscle N. B., because it is fixed to the arm at point C, a site more remote from the axis of the elbow A than is point B, which is on the other side of that axis; and thus the intent is concluded. 

But this is simple force, and not compound, as was proposed to be treated, and we must go further. Compound force is that when, in performing an operation with the arms, a second power is added from the weight of the person and the legs—as in pulling and pushing, where beyond the power of the arms, one adds the weight of the person and the force of the back and the legs, which is found in the desire to extend oneself. This would be like two men at a single column, where one pushes it and the other pulls it.

<sig>I ij</sig>

<note>compound force, arm, muscles, humerus, on weights, balance, elbow, extension, retraction</note>

<summary>Leonardo applies the principles of medieval mechanics—specifically the law of the lever—to human anatomy, explaining why the arrangement of arm muscles makes pulling more effective than pushing. He also introduces the concept of "compound force," where the weight and strength of the entire body augment the action of the limbs.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, mechanics, leverage, biceps, triceps, compound force, biomechanics, Leonardo da Vinci, physics of movement</keywords>

### *Which is the greater power of man, that of pulling, or that of pushing.*
### CHAPTER CCXXXV.

**MUCH** greater power has a man in pulling than in pushing, because in pulling there is added the power of the muscles of the arms, which are created only for pulling and not for pushing. For when the arm is straight, the muscles that move the elbow cannot have any more action in pushing than if a man were simply leaning his shoulder against the object he wishes to remove from its place. In such a case, one only employs the nerves <note>original: *nerui*; in Renaissance anatomy, this term was often used interchangeably for nerves, tendons, and sinews that transmit force.</note> that straighten the curved back, and those that straighten the bent leg—which are located under the thigh and in the calf behind the leg. Thus, it is concluded that pulling adds the power of the arms to the powerful extension of the legs and the back, together with the man’s chest, in the position required by his lean <note>original: *obliquità*; the specific angle or slant of the body needed to leverage weight.</note>. In pushing, the same elements contribute, but the power of the arms is lacking; for pushing with a straight arm without motion is the same as having a piece of wood placed between the shoulder and the object being pushed.

<summary>Leonardo explains why pulling is a more efficient use of human strength than pushing, noting that pulling engages the arm muscles in addition to the legs and back, whereas pushing with a straight arm relies almost entirely on the lower body and torso.</summary>
<keywords>biomechanics, anatomy, pulling vs pushing, arm muscles, leverage, kinetics, Renaissance science</keywords>

->OF LEONARDO DA VINCI.<-

### *On the limbs that bend, and what function the flesh that covers them performs in that bending.* CHAPTER CCXXXVI.

The flesh that covers the joints of the bones, and other parts near the bone, increases and decreases in thickness according to the bending or extension of the said limbs. That is, it grows <note>Leonardo refers here to the way flesh bunches up or compresses.</note> on the inner side of the angle generated in the bending of the limbs, and it thins and stretches on the outer side of the exterior angle. The middle area that lies between the convex and concave angles participates in this increase or decrease, but more or less depending on whether the parts are closer to or further from the angles of the said bent joints.

### *On turning the leg without the thigh.* CHAPTER CCXXXVII.

It is impossible to turn the leg from the knee down without turning the thigh with an equal amount of motion. This occurs because the joint of the knee bone is in contact with the thigh bone, nested and joined <note>original: *commesso*; referring to how the bones are articulated or fitted together.</note> with the bone of the leg, and this joint can only move forward or backward, in the manner required for walking and kneeling. It can never move laterally, because the contact points that compose the knee joint do not allow for it. Indeed, if such a joint were bendable and capable of rotation, like the <term>humerus bone</term> <note>original: *osso del' adiutorio*; a Renaissance anatomical term for the bone of the upper arm.</note> which is joined to the shoulder, or like the thigh bone joined to the hips, a person would always have legs that could bend to their sides just as easily as they do from the front to the... <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page.</note>

<sig>I iij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo describes the physiological changes in muscle and skin thickness during joint movement and explains the hinge-like mechanical limitation of the human knee.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, kinesiology, knee joint, humerus, muscle compression, joint articulation, Renaissance science</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the anatomical analysis of the knee joint from page 69, explaining why the structure of the human leg prevents it from bending backward and how this mechanism facilitates the act of standing up from a kneeling position.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>

->TREATISE ON PAINTING<-

<warning>Significant water staining on the left margin and lower left corner; text remains legible.</warning>

from behind, and such legs would always be crooked; and furthermore, such a joint cannot exceed the straightness of the leg, and is only bendable forward, and not backward, because if it bent backward, a man could not stand up when he was kneeling. For in rising from a kneeling position, of the two knees, first the load of the torso is placed over one of the knees, and the weight of the other is relieved; and in that time the other leg feels no weight other than its own, whereby it easily raises the knee from the ground and places the sole of the foot flat on the earth; then it shifts the entire weight onto that planted foot, leaning the hand upon its knee, and at the same time extends the arm which carries the chest and the head upward, and thus extends and straightens the thigh with the chest, and stands upright over that planted foot until it has raised the other leg.

### *On the folding of the flesh.* C A P. CCXXXVIII.

The folded flesh always wrinkles on the side opposite to that from which it is pulled. <note>Leonardo is observing that when a limb bends, the flesh on the inside of the curve bunches up into wrinkles, while the flesh on the outside is stretched smooth.</note>

### *On the simple motion of man.* C A P. CCXXXIX.

Simple motion is that which one performs in bending simply, either forward or backward.

### *Compound motion.* C A P. CCXL.

Compound motion is that which is called for when an action requires bending downward and sideways at the same time. Thus, the painter must be careful to make compound movements that are integral to their compositions: that is, if one performs a compound act necessitated by a specific action, do not imitate it in a contrary way by making them perform a simple act, which would be more remote from that action.

### *On motions appropriate to the effects of men.* C A P. CCXLI.

The motions of your figures should be demonstrative of the amount of force which is appropriate for them to use in various actions; that is, you should not show the same force in one who lifts a small stick as in one who lifts a heavy beam. Therefore, make the demonstrations of force vary according to the quality of the weights handled by them.

### *On the motions of the figures.* C A P. CCXLII.

Never make the heads straight upon the shoulders, but turned sideways, to the right or to the left, even if they are looking up or down or straight ahead; because it is necessary to make their motions show alert vitality, and not sleepiness. And do not make the midsections of the whole person, from the front or back, show their alignment directly above or below the other upper or lower midpoints; <note>Leonardo is advising against a "stacked" or rigid vertical alignment of the body segments (head, torso, hips) to avoid a stiff, unnatural appearance.</note> and if you must use it, do so for the elderly. And do not repeat the movements of the arms or the legs, not only within a single figure, but also not in those nearby or surrounding, unless the necessity of the case you are depicting compels you.

<summary>Leonardo details the mechanical process of rising from a kneeling position and provides instructions for painters on depicting realistic movement, emphasizing the need for varied poses and the correct representation of physical effort.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, kneeling, compound motion, simple motion, physical force, figure drawing, vitality, wrinkles, posture</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physical mechanics of the leg and joints (page 70) to the psychological expression of movement, specifically focusing on how internal thoughts and external stimuli manifest in the human body—a concept Leonardo calls "mental motions."</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

## BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.

### *On demonstrative acts.* Cap. CCXLIII.

**IN** expressive <note>original: "affettionati," referring to gestures that convey emotion or intent</note> demonstrative acts, things that are close in time or location ought to be shown with the hand not too far from the demonstrator; and if the aforementioned things are distant, the hand of the demonstrator must also be distant, and the face must be turned toward that which is being shown.

### *On the variety of faces.* Cap. CCXLIV.

**LET** the character <note>original: "l'aria," meaning the general expression or spirit of the face</note> of the faces be varied according to the accidents <note>a technical term in Renaissance art referring to changing circumstances or emotional states</note> of the person in toil, in rest, in weeping, in laughter, in shouting, in fear, and similar things; and furthermore, the limbs of the person together with their entire posture must correspond to the altered expression of the face.

### *On motions appropriate to the mind of the mover.* Cap. CCXLV.

**THERE ARE** some mental motions without the motion of the body, and some with the motion of the body. Mental motions without the motion of the body cause the arms, hands, and every other part that shows life to drop; but mental motions with the motion of the body hold the body and its limbs with the motion appropriate to the motion of the mind: and regarding this discourse many things will be said. There is also a third motion which participates in both; and a fourth which is neither one nor the other; and these last are senseless, or rather lacking in sense: and they belong in the chapter on madness or of buffoons in their *moresche* <note>A "moresca" was a stylized, often grotesque pantomime dance; Leonardo suggests these movements lack the rational connection between mind and body found in "sensible" people.</note>.

### *How mental acts move the person in the first degree of ease and convenience.* Cap. CCXLVI.

**THE** mental motion moves the body with simple and easy acts, not shifting here and there, because its object is in the mind, which does not move the senses when it is occupied with itself.

### *On motion born from the mind by means of an object.* Cap. CCXLVII.

**WHEN** the motion of a man is caused by an object, such an object either arises immediately or not: if it arises immediately, the one who moves turns first toward the object the most necessary sense, which is the eye, leaving the feet in their original place, and only moves the thighs together with the hips and knees toward that part where the eye is turned; and thus in such accidents there will be great discussion.

### *On common motions.* Cap. CCXLVIII.

**THE** motions of men are as varied as the variety of accidents that run through their minds: and each accident in itself moves those men more or less, according to how much power they possess, and according to their age; because a young man will make a different motion than an old man in response to the same situation.

<sig>I iiij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo explains his theory of "mental motions," arguing that physical gestures and facial expressions must be logically and proportionally linked to a person's internal thoughts and the distance of external stimuli.</summary>
<keywords>mental motions, demonstrative acts, facial expressions, physiognomy, moresca, body language, artistic theory, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<meta>catchword: will be [fiano]</meta>

### *On the motion of animals.* CHAPTER CCXLIX.

**EVERY TWO-LEGGED ANIMAL**, in its motion, lowers that part which is above the lifting foot more than that which is above the foot resting on the ground; and its highest part does the opposite. This is seen in the hips and shoulders of a man when he walks, and likewise in birds with their heads and their rumps.

### *That every limb should be proportioned to its whole body.*
->CHAPTER CCL.<-

**ENSURE** that a part of a whole is proportioned to its whole: as when a man is of a stout and short figure, ensure that every one of his limbs is the same in itself—that is, short and stout arms, wide and thick hands, and short fingers, with their joints in the manner described above. And so with the remainder of the body.

### *On the observance of decorum.* CHAPTER CCLI.

**OBSERVE** decorum <term>Decorum: a central Renaissance principle (from the Italian *decoro*) meaning that every element of a painting—gestures, clothing, and setting—must be appropriate to the social status and character of the person depicted.</term>, that is, the suitability of the actions, clothing, setting, and surroundings to the dignity or lowliness of the things you wish to depict. That is to say: a king must be of grave beard, expression, and attire, and the setting must be ornate, and the bystanders should stand with reverence, admiration, and in dignified clothing suitable to the gravity of a royal court. Conversely, lowly people should appear unadorned and abject, and those around them should share a likeness with lowly and presumptuous actions, and all the limbs must correspond to such a composition. The actions of an old man should not be similar to those of a youth, nor those of a woman to those of a man, nor those of a man to those of a child.

### *On the age of the figures.* CHAPTER CCLII.

**DO NOT** mix a quantity of children with as many old men, nor youths with infants, nor women with men, unless the specific event you wish to depict requires them to be joined together.

### *The quality of men in the composition of historical scenes.*
->CHAPTER CCLIII.<-

**ORDINARILY**, in common compositions of historical scenes <note>original: "historie," referring to narrative paintings of significant classical, biblical, or historical events</note>, make old men rare and separated from the youths, because old men are rare, and their habits do not harmonize with the habits of the young; and where there is no conformity of habits, friendship is not formed, and where there is no friendship, there is separation. And where you make compositions of scenes portraying gravity and councils, include few young men, because the young willingly shun councils; and other similar things.

### *On depicting one who speaks to several people.* CHAPTER CCLIV.

**YOU SHOULD** represent one whom you wish to be seen speaking among many people in the act of considering the matter he has to treat, and you should accommodate in him the gestures belonging to that matter; that is, if the matter is persuasive, let the gestures <note>The sentence breaks off here; the catchword "fiano" suggests the text continues on the next page with "will be..."</note>

<summary>Leonardo outlines the rules for "decorum" and proportion, emphasizing that a figure's physical traits, age, and social standing must be reflected in their gestures and the composition of the scene.</summary>
<keywords>animal motion, proportion, decorum, historical painting, composition, social status, age groups, gestures</keywords>

will be suited to the purpose. And if the subject involves the explanation of various reasons, have the speaker take one finger of the left hand with two fingers of the right, having closed the two smaller ones <note>The ring and pinky fingers.</note>; his face should be alert toward the people, with the mouth slightly open, so that he appears to be speaking. And if he is sitting, let him appear to be raising himself somewhat upright, with his head forward. And if you depict him standing, make him lean slightly with his chest and head toward the people, whom you shall represent as silent and fully intent on watching the orator’s face with expressions of admiration. Depict the mouth of some old man closed in wonder at the sentences heard, and at the lower extremities of the mouth, let many folds be drawn back in the cheeks, with the eyebrows high at the bridge, creating many wrinkles across the forehead. Some should be sitting with the fingers of their hands interlaced, holding their left <note>original: "stanco" — in this context, an archaic term for the left side.</note> knee; others with one knee over the other, upon which they hold a hand that supports the elbow, while that hand goes to sustain the bearded chin of an old man.

### *How an angry figure should be made.* Chapter CCLV.

**FOR AN ANGRY FIGURE**, you shall have him holding someone by the hair with the head twisted to the ground, and with one of his knees upon the ribs, and with the right arm raising a fist high; let this man have his hair standing up, his eyebrows low and narrow, and his teeth clenched at the sides of the arched mouth, his neck thick, and the front of it full of wrinkles from bending over the enemy.

### *How a desperate man is depicted.* Chapter CCLVI.

**FOR THE DESPERATE MAN**, you shall have him stabbing himself with a knife, and with his hands having torn his garments, and let one of those hands be at work tearing the wound; you shall make him with his feet stationary, the legs somewhat bent, and the body similarly inclined toward the ground, with his hair torn.

### *On laughter and weeping, and their difference.* Chapter CCLVII.

**BETWEEN ONE WHO LAUGHS AND ONE WHO WEEPS**, there is no variation in either the eyes, the mouth, or the cheeks, but only in the rigidity of the eyebrows, which are joined together in one who weeps, and are raised in one who laughs. To the one who weeps, one also adds the hands tearing at the clothes; and there are variations in the various causes of weeping, because some weep with anger, some with fear, some for tenderness and joy, some out of suspicion, and some for pain and torment, others for pity and grief for lost relatives or friends. Among these mourners, some appear desperate, some moderate; some weep, some scream; some turn their faces toward heaven with their hands down, having the fingers interlaced; others, fearful, with their shoulders raised to their ears; and so they follow according to the aforementioned causes. He who sheds tears raises the eyebrows at their junction and squeezes them together, creating wrinkles above, and turns the corners of the mouth downward; but he who laughs has them high, and the eyebrows open and wide.

<summary>Leonardo provides detailed instructions on the "physiognomy of emotion," describing the specific muscular movements and poses required to accurately depict orators, anger, despair, and the subtle facial differences between laughter and weeping.</summary>
<keywords>oratory gestures, expressions of anger, depiction of despair, anatomy of laughter, anatomy of weeping, facial wrinkles, Renaissance painting techniques, Leonardo da Vinci, Trattato della pittura</keywords>

<page-num>74</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

### *On the posing of children.* Chapter CCLVIII.

In children <note>original: "putti" — a term often used in art for infants or cherubs.</note> and in the elderly, there should be no agile or quick movements performed with their legs.

---

### *On the posing of women and youths.* Chapter CCLX.

In women and young men, there should be no poses with legs straddled or too wide apart, because such positions demonstrate boldness or a total lack of modesty; conversely, legs held close together demonstrate modesty.

---

### *On those who jump.* Chapter CCLIX.

Nature operates and teaches the jumper, without any conscious reasoning on his part, that when he wishes to jump, he must raise his arms and shoulders with force. These limbs, following the momentum, move together with a large part of the body and rise upward until their momentum is spent. This momentum is accompanied by the sudden extension of the body, which had been bent at the back and at the joints of the hips, knees, and feet. This extension is made diagonally—that is, forward and upward. Thus, the motion intended for moving forward carries the jumping body ahead, while the upward motion lifts the body, causing it to describe a great arc and increasing the distance of the jump.

---

### *On a man who wishes to throw something away from himself with great force.*
### Chapter CCLXI.

A man who wishes to throw a dart, a stone, or any other object with forceful motion can be depicted in two primary ways: he can be shown either when he is preparing for the <term>initiation of movement</term> <note>original: "creatione del moto" — the moment of potential energy before the release.</note>, or indeed when that movement is completed. If you depict him at the initiation of the movement, then the inner side of the foot will be on the same line as the chest, but he will have the opposite shoulder over that foot. That is, if the right foot is supporting the man's weight, the left shoulder will be positioned directly above the toes of that right foot.

<summary>Leonardo describes the decorum of poses for different ages and genders and provides a mechanical analysis of the physics involved in jumping and throwing.</summary>
<keywords>human anatomy, decorum, children, modesty, physics of movement, jumping, throwing mechanics, Leonardo da Vinci, mechanics of the body</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s technical analysis of human movement and balance, moving from the explosive action of jumping and throwing described on the previous page to the specific mechanics of thrusting objects into the ground and the foundational principles of "counterpoise" in stationary figures.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

### *Why a person who wishes to throw, or to thrust by throwing, an iron bar into the ground, raises the opposite leg in a curve. Chapter CCLXII.*

A person who, by throwing, wants to drive or thrust a rod <note>original: "cannone" — in this context, it refers to a heavy iron rod or pipe-like tool, rather than a piece of artillery.</note> into the ground, raises the leg opposite to the arm that is pulling, bending it at the knee. He does this to balance himself <note>original: "belicarfi" (bilicarsi) — to poise or find one's center of gravity.</note> over the foot that remains on the ground. Without this bending or twisting of the legs, the action could not be performed; nor could he pull if that raised leg did not eventually extend itself to provide leverage.

### *Counterpoise of bodies that do not move. Chapter CCLXIII.*

The counterpoises <term>ponderatione: the distribution of weight in a figure to maintain balance, a core concept in Renaissance art theory.</term> or balances of men are divided into two types: simple and composite.

**Simple balance** is that made by a man standing on immobile feet. In this state, whether the man opens his arms at various distances from his center, or leans while standing on only one of his feet, the center of his gravity always remains in a perpendicular line over the center of the foot that is supporting him. If he rests on both feet equally, then the center of the man’s chest will be positioned perpendicularly over the midpoint of the line measuring the space between the centers of his feet.

**Composite balance** is understood to be that made by a man who supports a weight upon himself through various movements. This is seen in the figure of **Hercules** crushing **Antaeus** <note>In Greek mythology, Hercules defeated the giant Antaeus by lifting him off the ground, as the giant's strength came from contact with his mother, the Earth. This was a popular subject for artists to demonstrate anatomical mastery.</note>. When Hercules suspends him from the ground between his chest and arms, you must position as much of Hercules's figure behind the central line of his feet as Antaeus has his center of gravity in front of those same feet.

<sig>K ij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo explains the physical mechanics of thrusting a heavy object into the ground and defines the two types of balance—simple and composite—necessary for an artist to depict a figure standing or lifting a heavy weight accurately.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics of movement, center of gravity, balance, counterpoise, Hercules and Antaeus, anatomical drawing, weight distribution</keywords>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>
<warning>Water stain on the lower left corner of the page.</warning>

### *Of the man who rests on both feet, and gives more weight to one than the other. Chapter CCLXIV.*

When, from standing for a long time, a man has tired the leg upon which he is resting, he shifts part of his weight onto the other leg. However, this type of resting should only be depicted for those of a decrepit age, or in infancy, or truly in someone who is exhausted, because it demonstrates weariness or a lack of vitality <note>original: "valetudine" — referring to the physical strength or health of the limbs.</note> in the limbs. Therefore, one always sees a young man who is healthy and vigorous resting upon only one of his legs; and if he does place some weight upon the other leg, he does so only when he intends to begin a necessary movement, without which all motion is impossible—for motion is generated by inequality <note>This is a fundamental principle of Leonardo's physics: balance (equality) is static, while movement (inequality) occurs when the center of gravity is shifted.</note>.

### *Of the posing of figures. Chapter CCLXV.*

Figures that are at rest must always vary their limbs; that is, if one arm goes forward, the other should remain still or go backward. If the figure rests upon one leg, the shoulder that is above that leg should be lower than the other <note>This is a classic description of "contrapposto," where the hips and shoulders tilt in opposite directions to maintain balance.</note>. This is observed by men of good sense, who always naturally attend to the balancing <note>original: "bilicare" — to poise or find a center of gravity.</note> of a man upon his feet so that he does not collapse. Because when resting on one foot, the opposite leg—being bent—does not support the man; it is as if it were dead <note>original: "come se fusse morta" — meaning it carries no weight.</note>. Therefore, necessity dictates that the weight of the body from the legs upward must shift its center of gravity over the joint of the leg that supports it.

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, anatomy, contrapposto, balancing, gravity, Hercules, Antaeus, painting</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanics of the "contrapposto" stance, noting that weight distribution is a sign of health or fatigue and that a figure's shoulders and hips must shift to maintain a natural center of gravity.</summary>

# BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.

### *Of the weight-balancing of a man when standing upon his feet.*

->C H A P T E R CCLXVI.<-

THE MAN who stands upon his feet will either distribute his weight equally upon those feet, or he will load himself with unequal weight. If he loads himself equally upon those feet, he will do so with natural weight mixed with accidental weight, or he will load himself with simple natural weight <note>Leonardo distinguishes between "natural weight"—the body itself—and "accidental weight," which refers to any external load or object being carried.</note>. If he loads himself with natural weight mixed with accidental weight, then the opposite extremes of the limbs are not equally distant from the centers <note>original: "poli" — literally poles, referring here to the pivot points or axes of the joints.</note> of the joints of the feet. But if he loads himself with simple natural weight, then such extremes of opposite limbs will be equally distant from the joints of the feet; and thus, a separate book shall be made concerning this balancing <note>original: "ponderatione" — a technical term in Renaissance art referring to the distribution of weight in a figure to ensure it looks physically stable.</note>.

### *Of local motion, more or less fast.*

->C H A P T E R CCLXVII.<-

THE local motion made by a man, or by any other animal, will be of greater or lesser speed according to how much the center of their gravity is more remote or near to the center of the foot where they are supported <note>Leonardo identifies that movement is essentially a controlled fall; the further the center of gravity moves from the supporting foot, the faster the resulting motion.</note>.

### *Of four-footed animals, and how they move.*

->C H A P T E R CCLXVIII.<-

THE TOTAL height of four-footed animals varies more in animals that walk than in those that stand still; and it varies more or less depending on whether these animals are of greater or lesser size. This is caused by the slant <term>obliquity: the angle of the limbs relative to the ground during a stride</term> of the legs that touch the ground, which raises the figure of that animal when those legs lose their slant and place themselves perpendicularly over the earth.

<sig>K iij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo explains the relationship between the center of gravity and balance in humans, as well as the mechanical changes in height that occur in quadrupeds as they move from a stride to a vertical stance.</summary>
<keywords>weight distribution, center of gravity, kinematics, animal movement, quadruped anatomy, equilibrium, locomotion</keywords>

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

# TREATISE ON PAINTING

### *On the correspondence that one half of a man’s thickness has with the other half. Chapter CCLXIX.*

NEVER will one half of the thickness and width of a man be equal to the other, if the limbs attached to it do not perform equal and similar movements. <note>Leonardo is observing that as soon as a body moves, its anatomical symmetry—from a visual perspective—is lost because muscles flex and joints shift differently on each side.</note>

### *How three motions are found in a man’s jumping upward. Chapter CCLXX.*

WHEN a man jumps upward, the head moves three times faster than the heel of the foot before the tip of the foot leaves the ground, and twice as fast as the hips. This happens because three angles are undone <note>original: "disfanno" — Leonardo uses this term to describe the straightening or extension of joints from a flexed position.</note> at the same time: the first (upper) is where the torso joins the thighs at the front, the second is where the thighs at the back join the legs, and the third is where the front of the leg joins the bone of the foot.

### *That it is impossible for any memory to retain all the aspects and changes of the limbs. Chapter CCLXXI.*

IT IS impossible that any memory could retain all the aspects or changes of any limb of any animal whatsoever. We shall exemplify this case with the demonstration of a hand. And because every continuous quantity <term>continuous quantity: a mathematical concept of a thing that can be divided infinitely, such as a line, time, or motion</term> is infinitely divisible, the motion of the eye looking at the hand, moving from point A to point B, moves through a space A-B which is also a continuous quantity, and consequently infinitely divisible. In every part of that motion, the aspect and shape of the hand varies in its appearance, and so it will be as it moves through the entire circle. The same will occur with a hand that rises during its motion: that is, it will pass through a space which is a quantity.

### *Of the practice sought with great diligence by the painter. Chapter CCLXXII.*

AND YOU, painter, who desire the greatest practice <note>original: "pratica" — here meaning technical mastery or professional skill acquired through experience.</note>, must understand that if you...

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the mechanics of jumping as a series of unfolding angles and argues that the infinite nature of motion makes it impossible for a painter to memorize every possible view of a limb.</summary>

Leonardo moves from how a body moves to how an artist must "see" and evaluate their representation of that body.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>
<sig>K iiij</sig>

...if you do not build upon a good foundation of natural things, you will produce works with very little honor and even less profit; but if you build it well, your works will be many and good, to your great honor and utility.

---

### *On the painter judging his own works and those of others. Chapter CCLXXIII.*

<margin>See above, Chapter II.</margin>
When the work is equal to the judgment, it is a sad sign for that judgment; and when the work exceeds such judgment, that is the worst, as happens to those who marvel at having performed so well. But when the judgment exceeds the work, this is a perfect sign. And if a youth is of such a disposition, without doubt he will be an excellent practitioner; though he will be a composer of few works, they will be of such quality that men will stop in admiration to contemplate them. <note>Leonardo believes that an artist who is too easily satisfied with their work will stop growing; the ideal state is for one's critical standards to be higher than one's current technical ability.</note>

---

### *On the painter judging his own painting. Chapter CCLXXIV.*

We know that errors are recognized more easily in the works of others than in one's own; therefore, see that you first become a good perspectivist, then have a complete knowledge of the measurements of man, and be a good architect—at least regarding the form of buildings and other things—and where you lack experience, do not refuse to draw them from life. But you must hold a flat mirror when you paint, and often look inside at your work, which will be seen there in reverse, and it will seem to be by the hand of another master; thus, you will better judge your own errors. Furthermore, it will be good to rise often and take some recreation, because upon returning, your judgment is improved; staying fixed upon the work causes you to be greatly deceived. <note>The "mirror trick" is still used by artists today to reset their visual perception and spot anatomical or compositional mistakes.</note>

---

### *How the mirror is the master of painters. Chapter CCLXXV.*

When you want to see if your painting as a whole has conformity with things portrayed from nature, take a mirror and have it reflect the living object; compare the reflected object with your painting, and consider well your subject in both. You see a flat mirror demonstrate things that appear in relief, and painting does the same. Painting has but a single surface, and the mirror is the same. The mirror and the painting show the likeness of things surrounded by shadow and light, and both seem to exist far beyond their surface. And if you recognize that the mirror, by means of outlines and shadows, makes things appear to stand out, and since you have among your colors shadows and lights more powerful than those in the mirror, certainly if you know how to compose them well together, your painting will also seem like a natural thing seen in a large mirror. Your master <note>The "master" here refers to the mirror itself.</note> shows you the <term>chiaro e oscuro: literally "light and dark," the use of strong contrasts between light and dark to give the illusion of three-dimensional volume</term> of any object; among your colors, there is one that is brighter than the illuminated parts of the image of such an object, and similarly among those colors, some are found that are darker than any darkness of that object. Thus it follows that you, the painter, will make your paintings similar to those in such a mirror when viewed with a single eye, because two eyes surround an object smaller than the eye. <note>Leonardo is noting that both a mirror and a painting present a two-dimensional translation of the world, which is best evaluated by mimicking the monocular (one-eyed) perspective of the mirror.</note>

<vocab>painting, mirror, judgment, Leonardo da Vinci, perspective, architecture, light and dark</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo emphasizes that a painter's critical judgment must always surpass their technical skill to ensure progress and explains how mirrors serve as an essential "master" for verifying the realism of light, shadow, and depth.</summary>
<keywords>artistic judgment, mirror, chiaroscuro, self-criticism, perspective, realism, optical theory</keywords>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>
<warning>Slight water staining visible on the left margin.</warning>

### *Which painting is most praiseworthy.* Chapter CCLXXVI.

<margin>See above, chapter 167.</margin>
**THAT** painting is most praiseworthy which has the greatest conformity with the object it imitates. This comparison is meant to shame those painters who wish to "fix" the things of nature, such as those who imitate a one-year-old child—whose head fits five times into its total height—yet they make it fit eight times. And where the width of the shoulders should be equal to the head, they make it double. Thus, they reduce a small one-year-old child to the proportions of a thirty-year-old man. They have used and seen this error used so many times that they have turned it into a habit, and this habit has so deeply penetrated and established itself in their corrupted judgment that they convince themselves that nature, or whoever imitates nature, makes grand errors by not doing as they do. <note>Leonardo is criticizing the "mannerist" tendency to apply idealized adult proportions to all figures regardless of their actual age or nature.</note>

### *What is the primary object and intention of the painter.* Chapter CCLXXVII.

**THE** primary intention of the painter is to make a simple flat surface appear as a <term>raised body</term> <note>original: "rilevato"; referring to the illusion of three-dimensional relief</note> that stands out from that surface. He who excels more than others in this art deserves the greatest praise. This investigation—or rather, the crown of this science—is born from <term>shadows</term> and <term>lights</term>, or what you might call <term>chiaroscuro</term> <note>original: "chiaro e oscuro"; the use of strong contrasts between light and dark to model three-dimensional forms</note>. Therefore, if you avoid shadows, you avoid the glory of the art in the eyes of noble minds, and you gain it only among the ignorant masses, who desire nothing more than the beauty of colors, not recognizing the "relief" or depth of a figure.

### *Which is more important in painting: shadow or its outlines.* Chapter CCLXXVIII.

**SHADOWS** in painting require much greater investigation and speculation than its outlines. The proof of this is that outlines can be traced using veils or flat glass placed between the eye and the object to be traced. But shadows are not governed by such a rule because of the "insensibility" of their edges, which are most often blurred, as is shown in the book on light and shadow. <note>Leonardo notes that while anyone can trace a shape, the subtle graduation of light into shadow (which he famously called "sfumato") requires deep scientific understanding.</note>

### *How light should be given to figures.* Chapter CCLXXIX.

**LIGHT** must be used according to the natural setting where you imagine your figure to be. That is, if you imagine the figure in the sun, make the shadows dark and the areas of light large, and let the shadows of all surrounding bodies be imprinted on the ground. And if the figure is in "sad weather" <note>original: "tristo tempo"; referring to overcast or cloudy skies</note>, make little difference between the lights and shadows, and do not give any shadow at the feet. And if the figure is indoors, make a great difference between the lights and shadows, with shadows on the ground. And if you depict a window with a paper screen and a white dwelling, make little difference between lights and shadows. And if the figure is illuminated by fire, make the lights reddish and powerful, and the shadows dark; and the cast shadows on the walls or ground should be sharp-edged; and the further the shadow gets from the body, the wider it should become. And if said figure is illuminated partly by the air and partly by fire, make the light caused by the air more powerful, and that of the fire almost red, in the likeness of fire.

<meta>catchword: And above</meta>

<summary>Leonardo argues that the highest goal of painting is the accurate imitation of nature, specifically through the mastery of light and shadow (chiaroscuro) to create the illusion of three-dimensional relief on a flat surface.</summary>
<keywords>proportions, anatomy, chiaroscuro, relief, shadows, light, optics, realism, sfumato</keywords>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>
<sig>L</sig>

**AND** above all, ensure that your painted figures have a large <term>light</term> <note>original: "lume"; refers to the illumination or light source</note> coming from above—that is, the living model you are portraying—because the people you see in the streets all have the light from above. Know that there is no acquaintance of yours so familiar that you would not struggle to recognize them if they were lit from below.

### *Where the observer of a painting should stand.* CHAPTER CCLXXX.

**LET US SUPPOSE** that A. B. is the <term>painting</term> <note>original: "pittura"</note> being viewed, and that D. is the <term>light source</term> <note>original: "lume"</note>. I say that if you position yourself between C. and E., you will perceive the painting poorly, especially if it is executed in oil or indeed varnished, because it will have a luster and be almost like a mirror. For these reasons, the closer you approach point C., the less you will see, because here the rays of light sent from the window to the painting bounce back. But if you position yourself between E. and D., your vision will be well served, and even more so the closer you get to point D., because that location is less affected by the percussion of said <term>reflected rays</term>.

---

### *How high the vanishing point should be placed.* CHAPTER CCLXXXI.

**THE** <term>point</term> <note>original: "punto"; referring here to the central point of the horizon or vanishing point</note> must be at the eye level of an average man, and the furthest edge of the plain bordering the sky must be placed at the height of that boundary where the flat earth meets the sky—except for mountains, which are exempt from this rule.

---

### *Why small figures should not, by reason, be highly finished.* CHAPTER CCLXXXII.

**I SAY** that when things appear small in form, it is because those things are far from the eye. This being so, it is necessary that between the eye and the object there is much <term>air</term>. This large volume of air hinders the clarity of the forms of that object, making the tiny parts of those bodies indiscernible and unrecognizable. Therefore you, painter, shall make small figures only sketched out and not finished; if you do otherwise, it will be contrary to the effects of nature, your mistress. The object remains small due to the great distance between the eye and the object; that great distance encloses much air within itself; this much air creates a <term>dense body</term> <note>original: "grosso corpo"; referring to the physical presence of the atmosphere</note>, which obstructs and takes away from the eye the tiny details of the objects.

<summary>This page outlines practical rules for realism, instructing painters to use top-down lighting, account for glare/reflections when positioning viewers, and use atmospheric perspective to blur distant, small figures.</summary>
<keywords>lighting, reflection, perspective, vanishing point, atmospheric perspective, realism, viewer position, horizon line</keywords>

### *What background the painter should use for his figures.*
### CHAPTER CCLXXXIII.

<margin>
See
above,
chapter 141.
</margin>
**SINCE** experience shows that all bodies are surrounded by shadows and lights, I want you, painter, to arrange the illuminated part so that it ends against something dark, and likewise, let the shadowed part of the body end against light things. This rule will be of great help in giving <term>relief</term> <note>original: "rileuare"; the illusion of three-dimensional depth on a flat surface</note> to your figures.

### *A precept of painting.* CHAPTER CCLXXXIV.

**WHERE** the shadow borders the light, pay attention to where it is lighter or darker, and where it is more or less <term>smoky</term> <note>original: "sfumosa"; relating to Leonardo's famous "sfumato" technique of soft, blurred transitions</note> toward the light. Above all, I remind you that in painting young people, you should not make the shadows hard-edged like those on stone, because flesh has a bit of transparency. This can be seen by looking at a hand held between the eye and the sun, for it appears reddish and transparently luminous. If you want to see what kind of shadow your flesh requires, cast a shadow there with your own finger; depending on whether you want it lighter or darker, hold your finger closer to or further from your painting, and imitate that.

### *On depicting a wilderness.* CHAPTER CCLXXXV.

**TREES** and plants that are more branched with thin twigs should have less dense shadows, while those trees and plants that have larger leaves will be the cause of larger shadows.

### *How to make an imaginary animal appear natural.*
### CHAPTER CCLXXXVI.

**YOU KNOW** that it is impossible to create an animal that does not have its own limbs, and that each limb, in its likeness, must resemble those of some other animal. Therefore, if you wish to make an <term>imaginary animal</term> <note>original: "animal finto"; a creature of fantasy or a monster</note> appear natural—let us say it is a serpent—take for its head that of a mastiff or a hound, give it the eyes of a cat, the ears of a porcupine, the nose of a greyhound, the eyebrows of a lion, the temples of an old rooster, and the neck of a water turtle.

### *On faces that should be made to have graceful relief.*
### CHAPTER CCLXXXVII.

**IN STREETS** facing west, when the sun is at midday, the walls should be high enough so that the wall facing the sun does not reflect light into the shadowed bodies. It would be good to have <term>diffuse light</term> <note>original: "aria senza splendore"; literally "air without splendor," referring to soft, overcast, or indirect lighting</note>, so that the sides of the faces share in the darkness of the opposite walls. Thus, the sides of the nose and the entire face turned toward the mouth of the street will be illuminated. Because of this, an eye positioned in the middle of the street's opening will see such a face, with all parts turned toward it, as being illuminated, while those sides turned toward the walls will be in shadow.
To this will be added the grace of shadows with a pleasing <term>softness</term> <note>original: "perdimento"; a "losing" or softening of edges</note>, entirely deprived of any sharp boundaries; and this will occur because of the...

H. which extends as far as under the chin; and here the nose remains strongly luminous, because it is seen by the entire light A. B. C. D. E.

### *On separating and making figures stand out from their backgrounds. Chapter CCLXXXVIII.*

You must place your figure against a light background if it is dark; and if it is light, place it against a dark background; and if it is both light and dark, place the dark part against the light background, and the light part against the dark background.

### *On the difference of lights placed in various positions. Chapter CCLXXXIX.*

A small light source creates large and sharply defined shadows upon shadowed bodies. Large light sources create small shadows with blurred edges upon shadowed bodies. When a small and powerful light is enclosed within a large and less powerful light—as the sun is in the open air—then the less powerful light will remain in the place of shadow upon the bodies illuminated by it. <note>Leonardo is explaining that even in daylight, the direct sun acts as a "small" powerful point, while the atmosphere acts as a "large" weak light.</note>

### *On avoiding the disproportion of surroundings. Chapter CCXC.*

A very great fault is seen in many painters, namely, making the dwellings of men and other surroundings in such a way that the doors do not even reach the knees of their inhabitants, even though they are closer to the eye of the viewer than the man who appears to want to enter them. We have seen porticos crowded with men, and one of the...

<page-num>84</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>
<warning>Water stain on the lower left margin of the page.</warning>

...supporting columns being in the fist of a man who leans upon them as if they were a thin stick, and similar things that are to be avoided with every effort. <note>Leonardo is criticizing artists who depict architectural elements with impossible proportions, making heavy structures look like toys.</note>

### *On the edges of bodies called lineaments, or outlines. CHAPTER CCXCI.*

The edges of bodies are of such minimal visibility that at even a small distance between the object and the eye, the eye cannot perceive the features of a friend or relative; one does not recognize him except by his clothing, and thus the mind receives knowledge of the whole figure along with its parts.

### *On the superficial details that are first lost when moving away from shaded bodies. CHAPTER CCXCII.*

The first things lost when moving away from shaded bodies are their edges. Secondly, at a greater distance, the shadows that divide the parts of the bodies where they touch are lost. Third, the thickness of the legs and feet, and thus successively the smallest parts are lost, so that at a long distance there remains only a mass of confused shape.

### *On the superficial details that are first lost due to distance. CHAPTER CCXCIII.*

The first thing lost from colors at a distance is the <term>luster</term> <note>original: "lustro"; the bright glint of light reflecting off a surface</note>, which is their smallest part and the "light of lights." The second is the light, because it is smaller than the shadow. Third are the principal shadows, and finally there remains a mediocre, confused darkness.

### *On the nature of the edges of bodies against other bodies. CHAPTER CCXCIV.*

When bodies with a convex surface <note>a surface that curves outward, like a sphere</note> end against other bodies of the same color, the edge of the convex body will appear darker than the surface it is set against. The edge of <term>parallel shafts</term> <note>original: "haste equigiacenti"; horizontal poles or bars</note> will appear very dark against a white background; and against a dark background, that same edge will appear brighter than any other part of the shaft, even though the light falling upon the shafts is of uniform brightness throughout.

---

<note>The illustration below demonstrates these principles of light and geometry. It depicts a building alongside a large sphere, showing how light hits curved versus flat surfaces and how shadows are cast onto the surrounding environment.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains how distance causes the human eye to lose sight of specific details in a sequence: first outlines, then secondary shadows, and finally the distinction of limbs, eventually leaving only a blurred mass. He also analyzes how the perceived darkness or brightness of an object's edge changes depending on the background it is placed against.</summary>

### *On the figure that moves against the wind.* Chap. CCXCV.

ALWAYS the figure that moves against the wind, along any line, does not maintain its center of gravity with the proper alignment above the center of its support <note>In this context, the "support" refers to the feet or the base of the person standing.</note>.

---

### *On the window where the figure is portrayed.* Chap. CCXCVI.

LET the window of the painters’ rooms be made of <term>cloth frames</term> <note>original: "impannate"; frames covered in linen or paper used before glass became common, which provided a soft, diffused light</note> without crossbars, and shaded step by step toward its edges with shades of black, so that the edge of the light is not joined directly to the edge of the window.

### *Why measuring a face, and then painting it in that same size, will make it appear larger than life.* Chap. CCXCVII.

A. B. is the width of the area <note>Leonardo is referring to a geometric diagram illustrating how the eye perceives the depth of a head</note>, and it is placed at the distance of the paper C. F. where the cheeks are; it would have to be set back as far as A. C., and then the temples would be brought to the distance O. R. of the lines A. F. B. F. Thus, there is the difference C. O. and R. D. It is concluded that the line C. F. and the line D. F., being shorter, must meet the paper where the entire height is drawn—that is, the lines F. A. and F. B. where the truth <note>The "truth" refers to the actual physical dimensions of the subject before perspective distortion</note> lies; and the difference is made, as I have said, between C. O. and R. D.

## *Whether the surface of every opaque body participates in the color of its surroundings.*

### CHAP. CCXCVIII.

You must understand that if a white <term>object</term> <note>original: "obbietto"; in Leonardo’s scientific vocabulary, this refers to any physical entity being studied or painted</note> is placed between two walls, one of which is white and the other black, you will find the same proportion between the shadowed part and the illuminated part of that object as exists between those walls. And if the object is blue, the result will be similar; therefore, when you have to paint, you should proceed as follows.

Take black to shade the blue object, choosing a black similar to the blackness or shadow of the wall that you imagine is <term>reflecting</term> <note>original: "riuerberare"; Leonardo often uses this term to describe light bouncing or "reverberating" off one surface onto another</note> onto your object. If you wish to work with certain and true science, you should act in this manner: when you make your walls of whatever color you please, take a small spoon—slightly larger than those used for cleaning the ears—the size of which depends on whether the work you are doing is large or small. This spoon should have its edges at an equal height <note>meaning a level measuring spoon</note>, and with this, you will measure the degrees of the quantities of colors that you use in your <term>mixtures</term> <note>original: "mistioni"</note>.

For example, if for those walls you have made the first shadows using three degrees of darkness and one degree of lightness—that is, three level spoonfuls (just as one measures grain) of simple black and one spoonful of <term>white lead</term> <note>original: "biacca"; a common white pigment made of lead carbonate</note>—you would have made a composition of a certain quality without any doubt. Now, having made one white wall and one dark wall, and needing to place a blue object between them, if you want it to have the true shadow and light that befits such a blue, place on one side the blue that you want to remain without shadow, and place the black beside it. Then take three spoonfuls of black and mix them with one spoonful of the luminous blue, and with this, apply the darkest shadow.

Once this is done, observe if the object is <term>spherical, cylindrical</term> <note>original: "colonnare"; literally "column-shaped"</note>, or square, or whatever it may be. If it is spherical, draw lines from the edges of the dark walls to the center of that spherical object; and where those lines intersect the surface of the object, therein the greatest shadows terminate, in—

<summary>Leonardo explains how the color and intensity of shadows on an object are dictated by its surrounding environment, proposing a precise, scientific method for mixing pigments using measured spoonfuls to ensure accuracy.</summary>
<keywords>color theory, reflected light, pigment mixing, white lead, geometry of shadow, artistic technique, optics</keywords>
