### *On the movement of animals.* &nbsp; Chap. CCXCIX.

That figure will appear to be running faster which is more on the point of falling forward.

<margin>See above, chapter 269.</margin>
The body that moves by itself will be all the more swift as the center of its gravity is more distant from the center of its <term>support</term> <note>original: "sostentacolo"; the point or surface upon which the weight of the body rests</note>. This is said regarding the movement of birds, which move by themselves without the flapping of wings or the favor of the wind. This happens when the center of gravity is outside the center of its support—that is, outside the middle of its position between the two wings. For if the middle of the wings is further back than the middle or center of the said gravity of the entire bird, then that bird will move forward and downward. It will move more or less forward than downward depending on how far the center of gravity is from the middle of its wings: a center of gravity far from the middle of the wings makes the bird’s descent very <term>oblique</term> <note>slanting or diagonal</note>, and if that center is near the middle of the wings, the descent of such a bird will have little obliquity.

---

*To make a figure that appears to be 40 <term>braccia</term> high in a space of 20 braccia, and has corresponding limbs, and stands upright on its feet.* <note>A "braccio" (plural "braccia") was a Florentine unit of measurement, roughly equivalent to 23 inches or 58 centimeters. Leonardo is describing a trick of perspective.</note>

In this and in every other case, it should not trouble the painter how the wall where he paints is situated, especially if the eye that views such a painting is meant to see it from a window or other <term>aperture</term> <note>original: "spiracolo"; a small opening or vent</note>. This is because the eye does not have to attend to the <term>flatness</term> <note>original: "planitie"</note> or curvature of those surfaces, but only to the things...

->*To make a figure on a wall 12 <term>braccia</term> high that appears to be 24 braccia in height.*<-
<note>A "braccio" (plural "braccia") was a common Italian unit of measurement, approximately the length of an arm or about 23 inches / 58 cm. Here, Leonardo is explaining a technique for anamorphosis—a distorted projection that looks normal when viewed from a specific point.</note>

### CHAPTER CCCI.

**I**f you want to make a figure or another object that appears to be 24 braccia in height, you shall do it this way. First, represent the wall M. N. with half of the man you wish to create; then, you will place the other half on the vault <note>a curved ceiling</note> M. R. But first, draw on the floor of a hall the exact shape of the wall and the vault where you are to make your figure. Then, behind this wall, draw the intended figure in profile at whatever size you please, and draw all your lines to the point F. In the same way that these lines intersect on the wall N. R., you will represent it on the actual wall that matches that shape. Thus, you will have all the heights and <term>foreshortenings</term> <note>original: "sporti"; the visual contraction of an object to create the illusion of depth</note> of the figure. As for the widths, or thicknesses, found on the straight wall M. N., you will make them in their own proper shape, because as the wall recedes, the figure diminishes by itself. However, the part of the figure that goes on the vault must be diminished as if it were on a straight surface. You must calculate this <term>diminution</term> <note>the reduction in size of objects as they appear further away</note> on a very flat floor, and there create the figure that you will take from wall N. R. with its true thicknesses, and then re-diminish them onto a relief wall; this will be an excellent method.

<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

### Warning regarding shadows and lights. CHAPTER CCCII.

**O**bserve that at the boundaries of shadows, light and shadow are always mixed; and the **derivative shadow** <note>original: "ombra deriuatiua"; this refers to the shadow cast by an object onto a surface, as opposed to the "attached shadow" on the object itself</note> mixes more with the light the further it is from the shaded body. But color will never be seen in its simple state: this is proven by the ninth [proposition], which states: The surface of every body participates in the color of its object <note>meaning its surroundings or the objects reflecting onto it</note>, even if it is the surface of a transparent body, such as air, water, and the like; because the air takes its light from the sun, and its darkness from the deprivation of that sun. Therefore, it is tinted in as many various colors as there are things placed between the eye and itself, because air in itself has no more color than water has; but the moisture that mixes with it from the **middle region** <note>referring to the lower layers of the atmosphere</note> downward is what thickens it, and as it thickens, the solar rays that strike it illuminate it, while the air from the middle region upward remains dark. And because light and darkness together compose the color blue, this is the blue with which the air is tinted, with greater or lesser darkness as the air is mixed with greater or lesser moisture.

### Painting, and universal light. CHAPTER CCCIII.

**A**lways make the parts of the figures or bodies in a multitude of men and animals darker the lower they are, and the closer they are to the middle of that multitude, even if they are of a uniform color in themselves. This is necessary because a smaller quantity of the sky—the illuminator of bodies—is seen in the lower spaces placed between the said...

<summary>Leonardo explains the physics of light and shadow, theorizing that the sky's blue color results from the mixture of illuminated moisture and the darkness of outer space. He also provides practical advice for artists on how to paint crowds by reducing light in the lower and central areas where the sky is obstructed.</summary>

<keywords>optics, shadows, atmospheric perspective, color of the sky, blue, painting crowds, reflection, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

T. R. H.** placed between them. In this space, it is clearly seen that position **F.** (being illuminated only by the part of the sky **C. D.**) is lit by a smaller portion of the sky than position **E.**, which is seen by the part of the sky **A. B.**, which is larger than sky **D. C.**; therefore, there is more light at **E.** than at **F.**

---

### *On backgrounds proportioned to the bodies that stand out against them, and first of flat surfaces of uniform color.* CHAPTER CCCIV.

The backgrounds of any flat surface of uniform color and light will not appear separated from that surface if they are of the same color and light. Therefore, conversely, they will appear separated if they are of different color and light.

### *Painting of figure and body.* CHAPTER CCCV.

Regular bodies are of two kinds: one is covered by a curved, oval, or spherical surface; the other is surrounded by flat-faced surfaces <note>original: "superficie laterate"; refers to polyhedral shapes with distinct sides</note>, whether regular or irregular. Spherical or oval bodies always appear separated from their backgrounds, even if the body is the same color as its background, and the same will happen with flat-faced bodies. This occurs because they are disposed to generate shadows on some of their sides, which cannot happen on a flat surface.

### *In painting, the part of a body that is smallest will be the first to lose clarity.* CHAPTER CCCVI.

Of the parts of those bodies that move away from the eye, that which is of smaller shape will be the first to lose clarity <note>original: "notitia"; in Leonardo's optical theory, this refers to the distinctness or "noticeability" of a detail to the human eye</note>. From which it follows that the part of greater quantity is the last to lose its clarity. Therefore, you, the painter, do not finish the tiny limbs of those things that are very remote, but follow the rule given in the sixth <note>Leonardo likely refers here to the "sixth book" or section of his compiled notes regarding perspective and the disappearance of form over distance</note>.

<summary>Leonardo explains how the volume of the sky affects the intensity of light in narrow spaces and provides rules for distinguishing objects from their backgrounds based on light, shadow, and distance.</summary>
<keywords>optics, light source, ambient light, geometry, background, shadow, perspective, clarity, visual perception</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of perspective and optics. While the previous page focused on how the sky illuminates objects and how bodies stand out against backgrounds, this page shifts to the "perspective of disappearance"—how distance and the atmosphere blur outlines and distort the perceived size of objects.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>OF LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>
<sig>M ij</sig>

How many there are who, in depicting cities and other things remote from the eye, make the outlines <note>original: "termini"; refers to the edges or boundaries of a form</note> of the buildings very distinct, no differently than if they were in the closest proximity. This is impossible in nature, because no sight, however powerful, is able to see the aforementioned outlines with true clarity at such a great distance. This is because the outlines of these bodies are the boundaries of their surfaces, and the boundaries of surfaces are lines—which lines are not a part of the quantity of that surface, nor even of the air that clothes such a surface. Therefore, that which is not a part of anything is invisible, as is proven in geometry. Thus you, painter, if you make these outlines sharp and distinct as is the custom, your depicted distance will not seem remote; rather, through this defect, it will appear very close. Furthermore, the angles of buildings are things that should not be depicted in distant cities, because it is impossible to see them from afar, given that these angles are the meeting of two lines in a point, and the point has no parts; therefore, it is invisible.

### *Why the same landscape appears at times larger or smaller than it is. Chapter CCCVII.*

Landscapes appear at times larger or smaller than they actually are because of the intervention of air that is thicker or thinner than usual, which comes between the horizon and the eye that sees it.

Among horizons at an equal distance from the eye, the one seen through thicker air will appear more remote, and the one seen in thinner air will appear closer.

<margin>See above, chapter 106.</margin>
Things of unequal size will appear equal at equal distances if the thickness of the air placed between the eye and those things is unequal—that is, if the thicker air is placed before the smaller thing. This is proven through the perspective of colors <note>This refers to "aerial perspective," where colors fade and shift toward blue or gray as they recede into the distance</note>, which causes a great mountain to appear small to the measurement, yet it seems larger than a small object near the eye; just as one sees that a finger near the eye covers a great mountain far from the eye.

### *Various observations. Chapter CCCVIII.*

Among things of equal darkness, magnitude, shape, and distance from the eye, the one seen against a field of greater brilliance or whiteness will appear smaller. This is taught by the sun seen behind trees without leaves: all their branches that are positioned against the body of the sun are so diminished that they remain invisible. A spear <note>original: "hasta"</note> placed between the eye and the solar body will do the same.

Parallel bodies placed vertically, when seen through fog, must appear thicker at the top than at the bottom. This is proven by the ninth [rule], which says: fog or thick air, when penetrated by the sun's rays, will appear whiter the lower it is.

Things seen from a distance are disproportionate; this arises because the brighter part sends its image <note>original: "simulacro"; the visual species or likeness traveling through the air</note> to the eye with a more vigorous ray than the darker part does. And I once saw a woman dressed in black with a cloth... <note>The text ends mid-sentence as the page concludes.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains why distant objects must have blurred outlines to appear realistic and how atmospheric density and light contrast (the "irradiation" effect) can distort the perceived size and thickness of objects.</summary>
<keywords>perspective of disappearance, atmospheric perspective, outlines, geometry of vision, optical illusions, thickness of air, aerial perspective</keywords>

...white on the head, which appeared twice as large as the thickness of its shoulders, which were clothed in black.

### *Of cities and other things seen in thick air.* Chapter 309.

Buildings of cities seen from above in times of fog, or in air thickened by the smoke of their fires or other vapors, will always be less distinct the lower they are; and conversely, they will be more clear and distinct the higher up they are seen. This is proven by the fourth proposition of this work, which says: The air is thicker the lower it is, and thinner the higher it is. This is demonstrated by the same fourth proposition placed below: we shall say the tower A. F. is seen by the eye N. in thick air, which is divided into four degrees <note>Referring to the horizontal layers or gradients of air density illustrated in the diagram</note>, becoming thicker the lower they are.

The smaller the quantity of air that is interposed between the eye and the thing seen, the less the color of that thing will take on the color of that air. It follows that the greater the quantity of air interposed between the eye and the thing seen, the more that thing takes on the color of the intervening air. It is demonstrated thus: Let there be the eye N., to which converge the five <term>species</term> <note>original: "spetie"; in Renaissance optics, these are the visual "images" or rays that objects were thought to emit to reach the eye</note> of the five parts of the tower A. F., namely A. B. C. D. E. I say that if the air were of uniform thickness, then the proportion of the air's color acquired by the base of the tower F. compared to the color acquired by the part of the tower B. would be the same as the proportion of the length of the line M. F. to the line B. S. But because of the previous rule, which proves that air is not uniform in its thickness, but rather thicker the lower it is, it is necessary that the proportion of the colors with which the air tints the parts of the tower B. and F. be of a greater proportion than that mentioned above; because the line M. F., in addition to being longer than the line S. B., passes through air that has a thickness that is uniformly varying <note>original: "vniformemente difforme"; a scholastic term used by Leonardo to describe a gradient where density changes at a constant, predictable rate</note>.

<summary>Leonardo explains atmospheric perspective by demonstrating that air is denser near the ground, causing the base of distant buildings to appear more obscured by atmospheric color than their tops.</summary>
<keywords>atmospheric perspective, air density, optics, color theory, Leonardo da Vinci, species, gradient</keywords>

### Of the solar rays that penetrate the gaps in the clouds.
#### CHAPTER 310.
The solar rays penetrating the <term>spiracles</term> <note>original: "spiracoli"; here Leonardo refers to the narrow gaps or vents between cloud formations</note> interspersed among the various densities and rounded shapes of the clouds, illuminate all the sites where they intersect. They also illuminate the darkness and tint all the obscure places that lie behind them with their own color. These obscurities are seen within the intervals between these solar rays.

### Of the things the eye sees below itself, mixed within fog and thick air.
#### CHAPTER 311.
The closer the air is to the water or the earth, the thicker it becomes. This is proved by the 19th proposition of the second book, which says: A thing rises less the more weight it has within itself; it follows that the lightest thing rises higher than the heavy one. <note>Leonardo is applying a principle of fluid dynamics to the atmosphere, suggesting that the "heavy" vapors and dust settle near the ground while the "pure" air rises.</note>

### Of buildings seen in thick air.
#### CHAPTER 312.
That part of a building will be less evident which is seen in air of greater thickness; and conversely, that part will be more visible which is seen in thinner air. Therefore, the eye **N**, looking at the tower **A. D.**, will see, in every degree of its lower part, a portion that is less distinct and lighter in color; and in every degree of its height, it will see a part that is more distinct and less light.

<image-desc>An illustration of a tall, square-shaped stone tower (labeled 'A' at the top, 'D' at the bottom) with several windows. To its left, there's a smaller, single-story building with a tiled roof. In the background, there's a hay-like stack and trees, suggesting a rural landscape. From the upper right corner of the frame, diagonal lines extend towards the top of the tower and the smaller building, indicating rays or lines of sight.</image-desc>

### Of things shown from a distance.
#### CHAPTER 313.
A dark object will appear lighter the further it is from the eye. It follows by the converse that a dark object will show greater darkness the closer it is found to the eye. Therefore, the lower parts of any object placed in <term>thick air</term> <note>original: "aria grossa"; the hazy, moisture-laden atmosphere near the ground</note> will appear more remote from the viewer's feet than their summits; for this reason, the low base of a mountain will appear more distant than the peak of that same mountain, even though the peak itself is physically further away.

<summary>Leonardo explains how atmospheric density—which is thicker near the ground—creates optical illusions, making the bases of buildings and mountains appear lighter and more distant than their tops.</summary>
<keywords>atmospheric perspective, air density, solar rays, cloud gaps, visibility, distance, mountain peaks, optical illusion</keywords>

# TREATISE ON PAINTING

### *Of the view of a city in thick air. CHAPTER 314.*

**THE EYE** that sees a city below itself in <term>thick air</term> <note>original: "aria grossa"; Leonardo’s term for air saturated with moisture or dust, which creates atmospheric perspective</note> sees the tops of the buildings as darker and more distinct than their bases. It sees these summits against a light-colored background because it views them against the low, thick air. This occurs for the reasons mentioned previously.

### *Of the lower boundaries of remote things. CHAPTER 315.*

**THE** lower boundaries of distant objects will be less perceptible than their upper boundaries. This is especially true of mountains and hills, whose peaks are seen against the sides of other mountains located behind them. On these mountains, one sees the upper outlines more clearly than their bases because the upper part is darker, being less obscured by the thick air that settles in low-lying places. It is this air that blurs the outlines of the bases of the hills. The same happens with trees, buildings, and other things that rise up into the air. From this, it often happens that tall towers seen at a great distance appear wide at the top and narrow at the bottom. This is because the upper part reveals the angle of the sides where they meet the front, as the thin air does not hide them as the thick air does. This occurs because of the 7th proposition of the first book, which states that the thick air interspersed between the eye and the sun is brighter at the bottom than at the top; and where the air is whiter, it obscures dark objects from the eye more than if that air were blue, as is seen at a long distance. The <term>battlements</term> <note>original: "merli"; the solid parts of a parapet with gaps for firing</note> of fortresses have spaces between them equal to the width of the battlements themselves, and yet the space appears much larger than the battlement. At an even remoter distance, the space occupies and covers the entire battlement, and such a fortress usually appears as a straight wall without any battlements at all.

### *Of things seen from far away. CHAPTER 316.*

**THE** outlines of an object will be less distinct the further away they are seen.

### *Of the blue color that appears in distant landscapes. CHAPTER 317.*

**OF** things remote from the eye, whatever color they may be, the one that is darkest—whether by its nature or by accident—will appear to be the most blue. "Natural" darkness belongs to a thing that is dark in itself; "accidental" darkness is that which is dark because of the shadow cast upon it by other objects.

<margin>
See above, chapters 292 and 306.
</margin>

### *Which parts of bodies are lost to notice due to distance. CHAPTER 318.*

**THOSE** parts of bodies that are of smaller quantity will be the first of which perception is lost over a long distance. This happens because the <term>visual images</term> <note>original: "spetie"; in Renaissance optics, these were the formal qualities or "likenesses" of an object that traveled through the air to the eye</note> of smaller things, at an equal distance, come to the eye with a smaller angle than larger things, and the recognition of remote things diminishes as their quantity decreases. It follows, therefore, that when...

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

At a great distance, the larger quantity appears to the eye at a minimum angle and is almost lost to awareness, while the smaller quantity loses its recognition entirely.

### *Why things are less recognizable the further they are removed from the eye. CHAPTER 319.*

**THAT** object which is more remote from the eye will be less recognizable. This happens because those parts that are smaller are lost first; then, the second-smallest parts are also lost at a greater distance, and so it continues, gradually consuming the parts until the awareness of the remote object is consumed entirely. In the end, all the parts are lost along with the whole. Furthermore, the color fails due to the thickness of the air that is interposed between the eye and the object seen.

### *Why faces appear dark from a distance. CHAPTER 320.*

**WE** see clearly that all the <term>likenesses</term> <note>original: "similitudini"; in Renaissance optics, this refers to the "species" or visual images emitted by objects that travel to the eye</note> of evident things that serve as our objects, both large and small, enter the sense through the small opening of the eye. If the likeness of the vastness of the sky and the earth passes through such a small entrance—a human face being almost nothing among such large images—it occupies so little of that opening due to the distance that diminishes it, that it remains incomprehensible. Furthermore, having to pass from the surface to the <term>impressive organ</term> <note>original: "impreſſiua"; Leonardo’s term for the part of the brain that receives and processes sensory impressions</note> through a dark medium—that is, the <term>hollow nerve</term> <note>original: "neruo voto"; the optic nerve, which Renaissance anatomists believed was a hollow tube for the passage of visual spirits</note>, which appears dark—that image, not being of a powerful color, is tinted by the darkness of the path, and upon reaching the impressive organ, it appears dark.
<margin>*</margin> No other cause can be assigned in any way to that point and the nerve that stays in the light; and because it is filled with a transparent humor like air, it performs the same function as a hole made in a wooden board, which appears black when looked at, and things seen through light and dark air become confused in the darkness.

### *Which parts are first lost to awareness in bodies that recede from the eye, and which are better preserved. CHAPTER 321.*

**THAT** part of the body which recedes from the eye is the one that least preserves its clarity and which is of a smaller shape. This occurs in the highlights of spherical or columnar bodies, and in the thinner limbs of bodies; for example, a deer will cease to send the images or likenesses of its legs and horns to the eye before those of its torso, which, being thicker, is preserved longer in its image. But the first thing lost at a distance are the <term>lineaments</term> <note>original: "lineamenti"; the fine outlines or contour lines</note> that define the surface and the figure.

### *Of linear perspective. CHAPTER 322.*

**LINEAR** perspective concerns the function of visual lines, proving by measurement how much the second object is smaller than the first, and the third smaller than the second, and so on, step by step, to the end of the things seen. I find by experience that if the second object is as far from the first as the first is from your eye, although they are of equal

<meta>Catchword: equal</meta>

He also introduces the concept of linear perspective as a mathematical measurement of this visual reduction.</summary>

size, the second will be half the size of the first; and if the third thing is at the same distance from the second as the second is from the first, it will be two-thirds smaller, and so, step by step at equal distances, they will always undergo a proportionate diminution, provided that the interval does not exceed the number of 20 <term>braccia</term> <note>An Italian unit of measurement based on the length of an arm; one Florentine *braccio* is approximately 58 centimeters or 23 inches.</note>. Within those 20 braccia, a figure equal to your own height will lose 1/2 of its size; within 40 it will lose 3/4; and then 5/6 at 60 braccia; and so, little by little, its diminution will progress. This assumes you make the <term>wall</term> <note>Leonardo uses "parete" (wall) to refer to the vertical plane of the painting or the imaginary glass through which the artist views the scene.</note> twice your height away from you, for making it only one height away creates a great difference between the first braccia and the second.

### On bodies seen in the fog. Chapter 323.

**THOSE** things which are seen in the fog will appear much larger than their true size. This arises because the perspective of the medium <note>The "medium" is the substance between the eye and the object, in this case, the moisture-laden air.</note> interposed between the eye and such an object does not match its color with the magnitude of that object. This is because such fog is similar to the blurred air interposed between the eye and the horizon in clear weather; thus, a body seen near the eye through the nearby fog appears to be at the distance of the horizon, where a very large tower would appear smaller than the aforementioned man standing nearby.

### On the height of buildings seen in the fog. Chapter 324.

<margin>See above, chapters 313 and 315.</margin> **THAT** part of a nearby building appears more blurred which is further from the ground; this happens because there is more fog between the eye and the top of the building than there is between the eye and its base. Furthermore, a <term>parallel tower</term> <note>A tower with perfectly vertical, parallel sides, like a cylinder or rectangular prism.</note> seen at a great distance through the fog will appear narrower as it gets closer to its base. This arises from the rule mentioned previously, which says: The fog will appear whiter and thicker the closer it is to the earth; and from the second rule of this section, which says: A dark object will appear to be of a smaller shape the more it is seen against a field of more powerful whiteness. Therefore, since the fog is whiter at the bottom than at the top, the darkness of such a tower must necessarily appear narrower at the bottom than at the top.

### On cities and other buildings seen in the evening or morning in the fog. Chapter 325.

**IN** buildings seen at a great distance in the evening or morning in fog or thick air, only the brightness of their parts illuminated by the sun is visible, specifically those facing the horizon; the parts of said buildings that are not seen by the sun remain almost the same medium darkness as the fog.

### Why higher things placed at a distance are darker than lower ones, even if the fog is uniform in thickness. Chapter 326.

**AMONG** things placed in fog, or other thick air, or in vapor, smoke, or at a distance, that which is higher will be more distinct. Among things of equal height, that one appears darker which is set against darker fog, as happens to the eye *H*, which, seeing towers *A*, *B*, and *C* of equal height to one another, sees *C*, the summit of the first tower, at *R*, a depth of two degrees of...

->*On the patches of shadows that appear on bodies from a distance.*<-

### C H A P T E R 3 2 7.

**A**LWAYS the throat, or any other vertical surface that has an overhang <note>Italian: *sporto*; a projection such as a chin, a nose, or a roof ledge.</note> above it, will be darker than the vertical face of that overhang itself. It follows that a body will appear more illuminated when it is seen by a greater portion of the same light source. Observe in point A <note>Referring to the accompanying diagram of the towers and the sky.</note> that no part of the sky between F and K illuminates it; in B, the sky between H and K illuminates it; in C, the sky between G and K; and in D, the sky between F and K illuminates it entirely. Therefore, the chest will be of equal brightness to the forehead, nose, and chin. 

But what I must remind you regarding faces is to consider how, at different distances, various qualities of shadows are lost, and only the primary patches remain—namely, the hollows of the eyes and other similar features. Ultimately, the face remains dark, because the highlights on it are faded, being small in comparison to the <term>middle shadows</term> <note>Italian: *umbra mezzana*; the intermediate tones that occupy the most surface area on a rounded form.</note>. For this reason, over a long distance, the quality and quantity of the principal lights and shadows are diminished, and every quality blends into a middle shadow. 

And this is the reason why trees, and every object at a certain distance, appear to become darker in themselves than they appear when close to the eye. But then the air interposed between the eye and the object causes that object to brighten and lean toward blue; however, it turns blue more readily in the shadows than in the luminous parts, where the <term>truth of the colors</term> <note>The local color, or the "true" color of the object as it appears without atmospheric interference.</note> is more visible.

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

### ->*Why at sunset the shadows of bodies cast upon a white wall are blue.* **Chapter 328.**<-

The shadows of bodies produced by the redness of the sun near the horizon will always be blue. This occurs because of the eleventh proposition, which states: *The surface of every opaque body partakes of the color of its object.* <note>Leonardo refers to his own numbered "propositions" or rules of optics found elsewhere in his notebooks. Here, "object" refers to whatever source of light or color is facing the surface.</note> Therefore, since the whiteness of the wall is entirely devoid of any color of its own, it is tinted by the colors of the objects facing it, which in this case are the sun and the sky. 

And because the sun turns red toward evening and the sky appears blue, in the area where the shadow "cannot see" the sun—according to the eighth rule of shadow, which says: *No light source ever saw the shadows of the body illuminated by it* <note>A poetic way of stating that a shadow is, by definition, an area where the light from a specific source is blocked.</note>—that area will instead be "seen" by the sky. Therefore, following the aforementioned eleventh proposition, the cast shadow <term>cast shadow (ombra derivativa): the shadow projected by an object onto another surface, as opposed to the shadow on the object itself.</term> will strike the white wall with a blue color, while the field surrounding that shadow, being struck by the redness of the sun, will partake of that red color.

<summary>Leonardo explains the optical phenomenon where shadows appear blue at sunset, attributing it to the way surfaces reflect the colors of their environment—the blue sky illuminating the shadowed area while the red sun illuminates the rest.</summary>
<keywords>optics, shadows, sunset, color theory, cast shadow, blue shadows, atmospheric light, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s analysis of atmospheric effects on light and color, shifting from the blue shadows of sunset on the previous page to the behavior of smoke, dust, and fog, and concluding with general rules for representing distance and depth in painting.</meta>

<page-num>99</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

### ->*Where smoke appears brightest.* **Chapter 329.**<-

Smoke viewed between the sun and the eye will appear brighter and more lucid than in any other part of the landscape where it originates. The same occurs with dust and fog; however, if you are positioned between the sun and them <note>That is, if the sun is behind you, shining onto the smoke or fog</note>, they will appear dark to you.

### ->*Of dust.* **Chapter 330.**<-

The higher the dust raised by the movement of an animal rises, the brighter it appears; conversely, it appears darker the less it rises, provided it is positioned between the sun and the eye.

### ->*Of smoke.* **Chapter 331.**<-

Smoke is more transparent and darker toward the edges of its billowing plumes than toward its center.

Smoke moves with greater slant the more powerful the wind that drives it.

Smokes are of as many different colors as there are varieties of things that generate them.

Smokes will not cast distinct shadows, and their boundaries are less discernible the further they are from their source. Objects placed behind them are less evident the denser the clusters of smoke are; the smoke is whiter the closer it is to its origin, and bluer toward its end.

Fire will appear darker to us the greater the amount of smoke that is interposed between the eye and said fire.

Where the smoke is more distant, objects are less obscured by it.

Render the landscape blurred like a thick fog, in which smoke is seen in various places with flames at their origins illuminating the densest plumes <term>plumes (globulenze): Leonardo uses this term to describe the rounded, bubbling shapes of rising smoke or clouds.</term> of said smoke; let the highest mountains be more evident than their bases, as is seen to happen in fogs.

### ->*Various precepts of painting.* **Chapter 332.**<-

The surface of every opaque body partakes of the color of the transparent medium <note>In this context, the "medium" is the air or atmosphere between the viewer and the object.</note> interposed between the eye and that surface; and it does so more intensely the denser that medium is, and the greater the distance between the eye and the said surface.

The edges of opaque bodies should be less distinct the further they are from the eye that sees them.

That part of an opaque body will be more shadowed or illuminated which is closer to the darkness that obscures it, or to the light that illuminates it.

The surface of every opaque body partakes of the color of its object <note>Here "object" refers to any surrounding surface or light source reflecting onto the body.</note>, but with a greater or lesser impression depending on whether that object is closer or more remote, or of greater or lesser intensity.

Things seen between light and shadow will show greater relief <term>relief (rilievo): the illusion of three-dimensional depth and volume on a flat surface.</term> than those that are entirely in the light or in the shadow.

When in long distances you make things recognizable and clear, they

<summary>Leonardo details how smoke, dust, and fog change in appearance based on their position relative to the sun, and provides rules for using atmospheric perspective and light to create the illusion of three-dimensional depth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of aerial perspective and the optical effects of the atmosphere. <page-num>100</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>
<warning>Water damage (staining) at the bottom left corner, though text remains legible.</warning>

...objects will appear nearby rather than distant. Therefore, in your imitations <note>Leonardo uses "imitations" to mean paintings or artistic representations</note>, ensure that objects possess that degree of clarity that indicates their distance. And if the object that serves as your subject has blurred and doubtful boundaries, you must do the same in your representation.

**A** distant object appears with blurred and doubtful boundaries for two different reasons. The first is that it reaches the eye at such a small angle and diminishes so much that it functions like minute things which, even when close to the eye, the eye cannot discern the shape of—such as fingernails, ants, or similar things. The second is that between the eye and distant objects, so much air is interposed that it becomes thick and dense; by its whiteness, it tints the shadows and veils them with its paleness, changing them from dark to a color between black and white, which is blue <note>original: "azzurro". Leonardo is describing the phenomenon of Rayleigh scattering, where the atmosphere scatters shorter blue wavelengths of light, making distant mountains look blue.</note>.

**ALTHOUGH** at long distances one loses the recognition of the nature of many things, those that are illuminated by the sun will nevertheless present a more certain appearance, while others will seem wrapped in blurred shadows. And because at every lower level the air gains density, objects that are lower will appear more blurred, and vice versa.

**WHEN** the sun makes the clouds on the horizon turn red, those things that were dressed in blue due to distance will share in that redness. Thus, a mixture will occur between blue and red, which will make the countryside very cheerful and pleasant. All solid things illuminated by such redness will be very evident and turn red; and because the air is transparent, it will have that redness infused throughout it, appearing the color of lilies <note>original: "fior de' gigli". In this context, Leonardo likely refers to a pale violet or iris-like hue resulting from the mixture of atmospheric blue and sunset red.</note>.

**THE** air situated between the sun and the earth, when the sun rises or sets, will always obscure the things behind it more than any other part of the air. This occurs because that air is more brilliant.

**OUTLINES** or profiles should not be made for one body placed against another; rather, the body should stand out by itself <note>This is a foundational principle of Leonardo’s "sfumato" technique—avoiding harsh outlines to create a more realistic, three-dimensional effect.</note>.

**IF** the boundary of a white object meets other white things, if it is curved, it will naturally create a dark edge, forming the darkest part of the illuminated side. But if it stands against a dark place, that same boundary will appear as the brightest part of the illuminated side.

**THAT** object will appear more remote and distinct from another when it is set against a background that differs most from its own color.

**IN** the distance, the boundaries of bodies with similar colors are lost first, specifically when the edge of one is against another, such as the edge of one oak tree against another similar oak. Second, at greater distances, the boundaries of bodies with medium colors set against one another will be lost, such as trees, cultivated land, walls, or other ruins of mountains or rocks. Last to be lost are the boundaries of bodies where light meets dark or dark meets light.

**AMONG** objects of equal height situated above the level of the eye, the one most remote from the eye will appear lower. And if it is situated below the eye, [it] will [be...] <note>The sentence continues on the next page.</note>

<meta>catchword: will be (fia)</meta>

<summary>Leonardo explains how atmospheric density and light conditions blur the outlines of distant objects and shift their colors toward blue or violet. He provides rules for painters on how to represent depth by avoiding harsh outlines and understanding how backgrounds affect the visibility of an object's edges.</summary>
<keywords>aerial perspective, sfumato, optics, atmospheric color, sunset effects, outlines, Leonardo da Vinci, light and shadow</keywords>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

the [line] closest to the eye will appear lower, and the parallel side lines will converge at a single point.

**LESS** evident in distant locations are those things surrounding rivers than those that are remote from such rivers and marshes. <note>Leonardo observes that the moisture rising from water creates a localized haze that obscures nearby objects more than those in drier areas.</note>

**AMONG** things of equal thickness, those that are closer to the eye will appear thinner [more rare], and the more remote ones will appear thicker [denser]. <note>This refers to visual compression; as objects recede, the space between them appears to shrink, making them look more crowded or "thick."</note>

**THE EYE** that has a larger pupil will see an object as having a larger form. This is demonstrated by looking at a celestial body through a tiny pinhole made with a needle in a piece of paper; because only a small part of its light is able to act, that body seems to diminish in its size by as much as the portion of light seeing it is missing from its whole.

**THE AIR** that is thickened and interposed between the eye and the object renders the edges of that object uncertain and confused, and makes the object appear larger than it actually is. This arises because <term>linear perspective</term> <note>The mathematical system of creating the illusion of depth on a flat surface.</note> does not diminish the angle that carries its species <note>original: "spetie". In Renaissance optics, "species" were the invisible "images" or "simulacra" that objects were thought to emit to reach the eye.</note> to the eye, and the perspective of colors pushes and removes it to a greater distance than it is, so that one removes it from the eye, and the other preserves its magnitude.

**WHEN** the sun is in the west, the falling mists thicken the air, and things that are not seen by the sun remain dark and confused, and those that are illuminated by the sun turn red and yellow, according to how the sun appears on the horizon. Furthermore, the things illuminated by this are highly evident, especially the buildings and houses of cities and villas, because their shadows are dark, and it seems that their clear appearance arises from confused and uncertain foundations, because everything is of one color unless it is seen by the sun.

**THE** object illuminated by the sun is also illuminated by the air, in such a way that two shadows are created, of which the one that has its central line pointing straight to the center of the sun will be darker. The central line of the <term>primary light</term> <note>The direct light from the source, like the sun.</note> and <term>derivative light</term> <note>Secondary or reflected light.</note> will always align with the central line of the <term>primary shadows</term> or <term>derivative shadows</term>.

**A BEAUTIFUL** spectacle is made by the sun when it is in the west [the setting sun], which illuminates all the tall buildings of cities and castles, and the tall trees of the countryside, and tints them with its color. All the rest from there downward remains with little relief, because being illuminated only by the air, there is little difference between the shadows and the lights, and for this reason, they do not stand out much. The things that rise highest among these are touched by the solar rays and, as has been said, are tinted in their color; therefore, you must take the color with which you make the sun, and you must add it to whatever light color you use to illuminate those bodies.

IT ALSO frequently occurs that a cloud will appear dark without being shadowed by another cloud separated from it; this happens according to the position of the eye, because for one nearby cloud, only the shadowed part is visible, while for others, both the shadowed and the luminous parts are seen.

AMONG things of equal height, the one that is more distant from the eye will appear lower. You see that the first cloud, although it is lower than the second, appears higher than it, as demonstrated on the wall by the intersection of the <term>visual pyramid</term> <note>The "visual pyramid" is a core concept in Renaissance perspective; it refers to the cone of light rays extending from the object to the observer's eye.</note> of the first low cloud at M. A. and the second higher one at N. M. This occurs when you seem to see a dark cloud higher than a light cloud because of the sun's rays in the east or the west.

<meta>Figure 333</meta>

->*Of backgrounds.* CHAPTER CCCXXXIV.<-

A MOST PRINCIPAL part of painting consists of the backgrounds of the painted objects. In these backgrounds, the boundaries of natural bodies that have a convex curvature are always discernible, as are the figures of such bodies against these backgrounds, even if the colors of the bodies are the same as the color of the aforementioned background. This arises because the convex boundaries of the bodies are not illuminated in the same way that the background is illuminated by the same light, because such a boundary will often be brighter or darker than that background. But if such a boundary is of the same color as the background, without doubt that part of the painting will prevent the recognition of the figures at that boundary. Such a choice in painting is to be avoided by the minds of good painters, since the intention of the painter is to make his bodies appear to stand out in front of the backgrounds; and in the aforementioned case the opposite happens, not only in painting, but in objects in <term>relief</term> <note>Relief (rilievo) refers to the illusion of three-dimensionality and depth on a flat surface.</note>.

->*Of the judgment one must make upon the work of a painter.* CHAPTER CCCXXXV.<-

THE FIRST point is that you consider whether the figures have the <term>relief</term> required by their position and the light that illuminates them; ensure that the shadows are not the same at the edges of the <term>composition</term> <note>original: "hiſtoria". In Renaissance art theory, the *istoria* or narrative is the complex arrangement of figures to tell a story.</note> as they are in the middle, because it is one thing to be surrounded by shadow, and another to have shadow on only one side. Those figures are surrounded by shadow which are toward the middle of the composition, because they are overshadowed by the figures interposed between them and the light; and those are shadowed on only one side which are interposed between the light and the composition, because where the light does not reach, the composition is seen, and the darkness of that composition is represented there; and where the composition is not seen, the splendor of the light is seen, and its clarity is represented there.

<meta>catchword: its</meta>

<summary>Leonardo discusses the optical failure of flat perspective to perfectly mimic depth and provides rules for using backgrounds and shadows to create the illusion of three-dimensional relief in complex compositions.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, backgrounds, relief, shadow, composition, optics, visual pyramid, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>
<warning>Visible water staining and foxing in the lower-left corner of the page; however, the text remains legible.</warning>

Second is that the arrangement, or distribution of figures, should be according to the event <note>original: "hiſtoria"; in Renaissance art theory, this refers to the narrative or subject matter of the painting</note> which you wish to depict.

Third, that the figures should be alert and focused on their particular roles.

### *On the relief of figures far from the eye.* Chapter CCCXXXVI.

That opaque body will show itself to have less <term>relief</term> <note>the quality of appearing three-dimensional or "standing out" from the surface</note> the further it is from the eye; and this happens because the air interposed between the eye and that opaque body—being brighter than the shadow of said body—corrupts that shadow and lightens it, stripping away the power of its darkness. This loss of darkness is the reason the body loses its relief.

### *On the boundaries of illuminated parts.* Chapter CCCXXXVII.

The boundary of an illuminated part <note>original: "membro alluminato," literally "illuminated member" or limb</note> will appear darker when seen against a lighter background, and similarly, it will appear lighter when seen against a darker background. And if such a boundary is flat and seen against a background similar to its own brightness, the boundary will be <term>insensible</term> <note>imperceptible or invisible to the eye</note>.

### *On boundaries.* Chapter CCCXXXVIII.

The boundaries of secondary objects will never be as discernible as those of the primary ones. Therefore, you, painter, should not define the boundaries of objects in the fourth or fifth distance <note>objects deep in the background</note> as sharply as those in the first or second. For the boundary of one thing against another is in the nature of a <term>mathematical line</term> <note>a line that has length but no thickness</note>, but it is not actually a line; because the end of one color is the beginning of another color, it should not be called a line, for nothing is placed between the end of one color and the start of another except the boundary itself, which is imperceptible up close. Therefore, you, painter, do not emphasize it in distant things.

### *On flesh tones and things far from the eye.* Chapter CCCXXXIX.

In figures and objects far from the eye, the painter should only apply <term>patches</term> <note>original: "macchie"; soft blurs or spots of color rather than detailed shapes</note> that are not sharply defined, but have blurred boundaries. The choice of such figures should be made when it is cloudy or toward evening. Above all, take care, as I have said, to avoid sharply defined lights and shadows, because from a distance they appear like mere tints <note>stains or flat dyes</note>, and they result in works that are difficult and without grace. And you must remember that shadows should never be of such a quality that, through their darkness, you lose the color where they are created, unless the place where the bodies are situated is already pitch black. Do not draw profiles, do not separate individual hairs, and do not apply white highlights except on white things; these highlights should demonstrate the "first beauty" <note>the purest or most vibrant version</note> of the color where they are placed.

### *Various precepts of painting.* Chapter CCCXXXX.

The boundaries and shapes of any part of shaded bodies are poorly discerned in their shadows and in their highlights; but in the parts interposed between the highlights and the shadows, these bodies are perceived with the highest degree of clarity.

Perspective, as it is applied in painting, is divided into three principal parts: the first of which concerns the diminution that occurs in the size of bodies...

<summary>Leonardo explains how atmospheric perspective lightens shadows and blurs the edges of distant objects, advising painters to avoid sharp outlines and detailed highlights on background figures to maintain realism.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s systematic rules on perspective. Having previously discussed the "diminution" or fading of shapes, he now transitions into the second and third parts of his theory: the fading of colors (aerial perspective) and the loss of distinct edges over distance, followed by a scientific explanation of why paintings can never fully replicate the three-dimensional depth perceived by two eyes.</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>
<warning>Water staining at the bottom of the page; text is clearly legible.</warning>
<sig>O</sig>

at various distances. The second part is that which treats the <term>diminution of colors</term> <note>the way colors lose their intensity and change hue as they recede into the distance</note> of such bodies. The third is that which diminishes the <term>clarity</term> <note>original: "notitia"; the degree to which an object’s details are recognizable</note> of the figures and the boundaries that these bodies have at various distances.

**THE BLUE** of the air is a color composed of light and darkness. I say "light" because of the air illuminated within the particles of moisture infused within said air. By "darkness," I mean the pure air, which is not divided into atoms—that is, particles of moisture—into which the solar rays might strike. And an example of this is seen in the air that is interposed between the eye and the shaded mountains, due to the shadows of the great abundance of trees found upon them; or else the air is shaded in that part where it is not struck by the solar rays. This air becomes blue, but it does not become blue in its luminous part, and even less so in the part covered with snow.

**AMONG** things equally dark and at an equal distance, that which is seen against a whiter background will appear darker, and likewise the reverse.

**THAT** thing which is most painted with white and black will appear to have better <term>relief</term> <note>the illusion of three-dimensional depth on a flat surface</note> than any other. Therefore, remember, painter, to dress your figures in the lightest colors you can; for if you make them in dark colors, they will have little relief and little visibility from a distance. This is because the shadows of all things are dark, and if you make a dark garment, there will be little difference between the light and the shadow; but in light colors, there will be a great difference.

->*Why things portrayed perfectly from nature do not appear with the same relief as nature itself.* CHAPTER CCCXLI.<-

<margin>See above, chapter 53.</margin>
**IT IS IMPOSSIBLE** that a painting, even if imitated with the highest perfection of line, shadow, light, and color, can appear with the same relief as nature itself, unless that natural object is seen at a long distance with only a single eye. To prove this: let the eyes be A and B, which see the object C with the meeting of the central lines of the eyes A-C and B-C. I say that the lateral lines of these central ones see the space G-D behind the object; eye A sees the whole space F-D, and eye B sees the whole space G-E. Therefore, the two eyes see the entire space F-E behind the object C. For this reason, the object C remains transparent—according to the definition of transparency, behind which nothing is hidden. This cannot happen to one who sees an object larger than the eye with only a single eye. And from what has been said, we can conclude our inquiry; for a painted thing occupies all the space behind itself, and in no way is it possible to see any part of the background that its circumferential line has behind it. <note>Leonardo is explaining that because we have two eyes, we "see around" objects in the real world. A painting is flat and seen by both eyes as a flat surface, so it can never truly replicate the way a 3D object allows us to see the background behind its edges.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains the optical causes of the sky's blue color and argues that painting cannot perfectly replicate 3D depth because binocular vision allows us to see "around" real objects.</summary>
<keywords>aerial perspective, atmospheric blue, relief, binocular vision, optical contrast, light and shadow, perception of depth</keywords>

### *On making objects appear detached from their backgrounds; that is, from the wall where they are painted. CHAPTER 342.*

**O**bjects will show much more <term>relief</term> <note>original: "rilieuo"; the illusion of three-dimensional depth on a flat surface</note> against a light and illuminated background than against a dark one. The reason is that if you wish to give relief to your figure, you make that part of the body which is furthest from the light participate less in said light, whereby it becomes darker. If this dark side then ends against a dark background, it results in blurred boundaries. For this reason—unless a <term>reflection</term> <note>light bouncing back from another surface into the shadow</note> occurs—the work remains without grace, and from a distance nothing appears except the luminous parts. Thus, it seems as though the dark parts are part of the background itself; the objects look like they have been cut out and appear to have much less relief than they should because the background is so dark.

### *A Precept. CHAPTER 343.*

**F**igures possess more grace when placed in <term>universal light</term> <note>broad, diffused light, such as an overcast sky, rather than a single direct source</note> than in specific and small lights. This is because great and powerful lights embrace the relief of bodies, and works made in such lights appear graceful from a distance. Those that are portrayed in small lights take on a great amount of shadow; works made with such shadows never appear as anything more than colored stains when viewed from afar.

### *On depicting the regions of the world. CHAPTER 344.*

**Y**ou shall be advised that in maritime places, or those near the sea, facing the southern regions, you should not depict winter in the trees or meadows as you would in northern parts or those remote from the sea—except in the case of trees that shed their leaves every year.

### *On depicting the four seasons of the year, or those phases partaking of them. CHAPTER 345.*

**I**n autumn you shall depict things according to the age of that season; that is, in the beginning, the leaves of the trees start to turn pale on the oldest branches, more or <note>Text ends mid-sentence; the author is beginning to describe the color transitions of autumn foliage.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains how light backgrounds enhance the three-dimensional "relief" of figures and warns against harsh lighting that obscures form at a distance. He also advises painters to consider geographical climate and the specific progression of seasons when painting landscapes.</summary>
<keywords>relief, background, universal light, shadow, maritime climate, autumn, landscape painting, optical clarity</keywords>

<page-num>107</page-num>
->OF LEONARDO DA VINCI.<-

...less so according to whether the plant is in a sterile or fertile place: and do not do as many, who make all sorts of trees—even if they are equally distant—with the same quality of green. Thus the color of meadows, stones, and the <term>trunks</term> <note>original: "pedali"; refers to the base or lower part of the tree trunk</note> of the aforementioned plants always varies, because nature is infinitely variable.

### *On painted wind. CHAPTER 346.*

**I**n the representation of wind, besides the bending of branches and the overturning of leaves toward the direction the wind is coming from, one must depict the clouds of fine dust mixed with the turbid air.

### *On the beginning of a rain. CHAPTER 347.*

**T**he rain falls through the air, darkening it with a glistening tint, taking the light of the sun from one side and shadow from the opposite side, as is seen to happen with mists. The earth is darkened because it is deprived of the sun’s splendor by such rain. Objects seen through the rain have blurred and indistinguishable edges, and those things that are closer to the eye will be more distinct. Things seen within the shaded rain will be clearer than those seen in the illuminated rain. This happens because objects seen in shaded rain only lose their principal lights, but those seen in the luminous rain lose both light and shadow, because their illuminated parts mix with the brightness of the lit air, and their shadowed parts are brightened by that same clarity of the aforementioned illuminated air.

### *On shadows cast by bridges onto their waters. CHAPTER 348.*

**T**he shadows of bridges will never be seen upon their waters unless the water first loses its ability to act as a mirror due to its turbidity. This is proven because clear water has a lustrous and polished surface, and it reflects the bridge at every point positioned at equal angles between the eye and the bridge; it also reflects the air under the bridge, where the shadow of said bridge ought to be. This, turbid water cannot do, because it does not reflect, but instead it receives the shadow, just as a dusty road would.

### *Precepts of painting. CHAPTER 349.*

**P**erspective is the bridle and the rudder of painting.
**T**he size of a painted figure should show at what distance it is seen.
**I**f you see a figure that is life-sized, know that it will appear to be close to the eye.

### *Precepts. CHAPTER 350.*

**T**he <term>balance</term> <note>original: "bilico"; the center of gravity or equilibrium</note> is always on the central line of the chest from the navel upward; thus, one must account for the accidental weight of a man as well as his natural weight. This is demonstrated when extending an arm: the fist placed at its extremity acts as the counterweight placed at the end of a <term>steelyard</term> <note>original: "stadera"; a portable balance scale with a sliding weight</note>. Therefore, of necessity, as much weight is thrown on the side opposite the navel as there is accidental weight in the fist, and it is necessary for the heel to rise.

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

### *On the statue.* CHAPTER 351.

**I**f you wish to make a figure of marble, first make one of clay; once it is finished and dry, place it in a box that is large enough to later receive the marble block which you intend to carve into the likeness of that clay figure. After the clay figure is placed inside this box, take small rods that fit precisely through holes in the box. Push them into each hole until each white rod touches the figure at various points. Paint the portion of the rods remaining outside the box black, and make a mark on each rod and its corresponding hole so they may be matched at your will. Then, remove the clay figure from the box and place your piece of marble in it; remove enough of the marble so that all your rods are hidden up to their marks in said holes. To better accomplish this, ensure that the entire box can be lifted upward while its bottom remains always beneath the marble; in this way, you can remove material with your <term>chisels</term> <note>original: "ferri"; literally "irons," the tools used for carving</note> with great ease.

<margin>* L</margin>
### *On making a painting with eternal varnish.* CHAPTER 352.

**P**aint your picture upon very delicate and flat paper stretched on a frame; then apply a good, thick <term>ground</term> <note>original: "imprimitura"; the preparatory layer applied to a surface before painting</note> of pitch and well-crushed brick. After this, apply a ground of <term>white lead</term> <note>original: "biacca"</note> and <term>giallolino</term> <note>original: "giallolino"; a traditional lead-tin yellow pigment common in the Renaissance</note>. Then apply your colors and varnish with clear, thick old oil, and attach it firmly to flat glass. However, it is better to make a panel of well-glazed clay, with a ground of white lead and yellow; then color and varnish it. Then attach <term>crystalline glass</term> <note>original: "vetro cristallino"; high-quality, clear glass</note> to it with very clear varnish. But first, let the colors dry well in a dark <term>stove</term> <note>original: "stufa"; likely a drying chamber or oven</note>, and then varnish it with walnut oil and amber, or walnut oil thickened in the sun.

<margin>* L</margin>
### *Method of coloring on canvas.* CHAPTER 353.

**P**lace your canvas in a frame and give it a coating of weak glue; let it dry, then draw upon it. Apply the <term>flesh tones</term> <note>original: "incarnatione"</note> with bristle brushes, and while it is still fresh, you shall create the <term>softened shadow</term> <note>original: "ombra sfumata"; referring to Leonardo’s famous "sfumato" technique of blurring edges</note> according to your manner. The flesh tones shall be composed of white lead, <term>lake</term> <note>original: "lacca"; a transparent red pigment made from organic dyes</note>, and yellow; the shadow shall be black, <term>maiolica</term> <note>original: "majorica"; likely referring to a dark earth pigment or perhaps crushed ceramic pigment</note>, and a little lake, or if you prefer, hard <term>chalk</term> <note>original: "lapis duro"</note>. Once you have softened the transitions, let it dry; then retouch it when dry with lake and gum that has sat for a long time in gum-water until liquid, which is better because it performs its function without becoming glossy.

**F**urthermore, to make the shadows darker, take the aforementioned gum-lake and ink; with this shadow, you can shade many colors because it is transparent. You can thus shade blue, lake, and various other shadows. I say this because you will shade different highlights with simple gum-lake over the lake without <term>tempera</term> <note>original: "tempera"; the binding medium for the paint</note>, or over <term>cinnabar</term> <note>original: "cinabro"; a bright red mineral pigment</note> that is tempered and dry.

### *Precept of perspective in painting.* CHAPTER 354.

**W**hen you do not perceive a variety in the clarity or darkness within the air, then the perspective of shadows should be discarded from your imitation; you must then rely only on the perspective of the diminution of bodies, the diminution of colors, and the fading of the definition of things to the eye con—

<summary>This page details Leonardo's mechanical "pointing" method for duplicating clay models in marble and provides specific technical recipes for creating durable glazes and painting on canvas. It concludes with a rule on when to prioritize linear perspective over atmospheric perspective.</summary>

<keywords>pointing method, marble sculpture, varnish, white lead, giallolino, sfumato, pigments, lake, cinnabar, aerial perspective, Renaissance painting techniques</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo's technical advice for painters, moving from the physical preparation of sculptures and canvases discussed on page 108 to the optical principles of distance, lighting, and the artistic treatment of drapery. It emphasizes how color and atmosphere, rather than just lines, define the depth of a scene.</meta>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

interposed: and this makes one and the same thing appear more remote, namely the loss of the perception of the figure of whatever object.
   The eye will never have, through <term>linear perspective</term> <note>The geometric method of creating the illusion of depth on a flat surface</note>, without its own motion, knowledge of the distance between the object and another thing, except by means of the <term>perspective of colors</term> <note>Also known as atmospheric perspective, where distance is suggested by changes in color and clarity</note>.

### *On objects.* Chapter 355.

**T**hat part of the object will be most illuminated which is nearest to the luminous source that lights it.
   The <term>likeness</term> <note>original: "similitudine"; in Renaissance optics, this refers to the visual "species" or image of an object that travels through the air to the eye</note> of things at every degree of distance loses degrees of power; that is, the more remote the thing is from the eye, the less its likeness will be able to penetrate through the air.

### *On the diminution of colors and bodies.* Chapter 356.

**L**et the diminution of the qualities of colors be observed together with the diminution of the bodies to which they are applied.

### *On the interposition of transparent bodies between the eye and the object.* Chapter 357.

**T**he greater the transparent interposition between the eye and the object, the more the color of the object changes into the color of the interposed transparent medium <note>Usually referring to the blue or hazy quality of the air over long distances</note>.
   When the object is placed between the eye and the light, along the central line that extends between the center of the light and the eye, then such an object shall be totally deprived of light.

### *On the clothes that dress figures, and their folds.* Chapter 358.

**T**he clothes that dress figures should have their folds arranged to encircle the limbs they cover, so that in the illuminated parts no folds of dark shadow are placed, and in the shaded parts no folds of too much brightness are made; and let the lines of these folds go in some part surrounding the limbs they cover, and not with lines that cut across the limbs, nor with shadows that sink deeper than the surface of the dressed body. And in effect, let the cloth be so adapted that it does not appear uninhabited—that is, that it does not seem like a bundle of cloth stripped from a man, as many are seen to do, who fall so much in love with various groupings of various folds that they fill an entire figure with them, forgetting the purpose for which such cloth is made, which is to clothe and surround the limbs with grace where they rest, and not to fill them all with winds or inflated bladders over the illuminated reliefs of the limbs. I do not deny that one should make some beautiful folds, but let them be made in parts of the figure where the limbs, between themselves and the body, gather and collect such cloth. And above all, vary the draperies in <term>narrative scenes</term> <note>original: "historie"; multi-figure compositions that tell a story</note>, as in making for some the folds with sharp, faceted breaks—and this is for thick cloths—and let some cloths have soft folds, with their turns not torn, and others twisted.

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

## On the nature of drapery folds. Chapter 359.

**M**any love the folds of the hems of cloths to have sharp, harsh, and brisk angles; others prefer almost imperceptible angles; others still prefer no angles at all, but instead certain curves in their place.

## How drapery folds should be made. Chapter 360.

**T**hat part of the folds which is found furthest from its constrained ends <note>Points where the fabric is pulled, pinned, or held</note> will return most to its original nature. Naturally, every thing desires to maintain itself in its own state of being. Cloth, because it is of equal density and thickness on both its reverse and its right side, desires to remain flat <note>original: "planitie"</note>. Therefore, when it is forced by some fold or crease to leave that flatness, it follows the nature of the force in that part of itself where it is most constrained; and you will find that the part furthest from those constraints returns most to its original nature—that is, being spread out and wide. 

For example, let **A. B. C.** be the fold of the cloth mentioned above. Let **A. B.** be the place where the cloth is folded and constrained. I proposed to you that the part of the cloth furthest from the constrained ends would return most to its original nature; therefore, **C.** being furthest away, the fold at **C.** will be wider than in any other place.

## How the folds of drapery should be made. Chapter 361.

**A** cloth should not be given a confusion of many folds; rather, create them only where they are held by the hands or arms, and let the rest fall <note>The text ends abruptly here, likely continuing the thought that the remaining fabric should hang naturally according to gravity</note>

<summary>Leonardo describes the physical "desire" of fabric to remain flat, explaining that folds are widest and most natural at their furthest point from where they are being pulled or held.</summary>
<keywords>drapery, folds, physics of fabric, painting technique, tension, Leonardo da Vinci, artistic anatomy of clothing</keywords>

...simply, and they must be drawn from nature; that is, if you wish to represent woolen cloth, use folds according to that material, and if it is silk, or fine cloth, or rustic peasant cloth <note>original: "panno da villano"</note>, diversify the folds for each one. ### On the folds of drapery in foreshortening. CHAPTER 362.

**W**here the figure is foreshortened <term>scorcia|Foreshortening: an artistic technique used to create the illusion of an object receding strongly into the distance by shortening the lines of its length</term>, show a greater number of folds than where it is not foreshortened, and let the limbs be surrounded by dense folds winding around them. Let it be that where the eye is positioned, the line **M. N.** shows the middle of each circle to be further from the eye than their ends. **N. O.** shows them as straight, because they are found directly opposite. **P. Q.** shows them in the opposite direction. <note>Leonardo is using the letters in the accompanying diagram to explain how the curvature of a fold changes depending on whether it is above, below, or at the viewer's eye level.</note>

### On the eye that sees the folds of drapery surrounding a person. CHAPTER 363.

**T**he shadows interspersed between the folds of drapery surrounding human bodies will be darker as they are more directly opposite the eye within the concavities <note>The hollow or recessed parts of the fold</note> where such shadows are generated. This is intended to be the case when the eye is situated between the shaded part and the luminous part of the aforementioned figure.

<warning>Significant water damage and staining on the left side of the page, particularly in the lower left quadrant, though it does not obscure the text.</warning>

...it is good with its outlines <term>lineamenti|Lineamenti: the defining contours or linear strokes that establish the form and movement of a figure</term> to show the action of such a figure, in a way that they do not create ambiguity or confusion regarding the true posture to whoever considers it: and that no fold with its shadow takes away any limb, that is, that the depth of the fold appears further in than the surface of the clothed limb. And if you represent figures dressed in multiple garments, let it not seem that the outermost garment encloses within itself the simple bones of such figures, but the flesh together with them, and the cloths as clothing of the flesh, with as much thickness as is required by the layering of its folds.

The folds of the cloths surrounding the limbs must decrease in their thickness toward the ends of the object they surround.

The length of the folds that are tighter to the limbs must wrinkle on that side where the limb, by its bending, decreases, and stretch on the side opposite to its bending.

### On the horizon reflected in the waves. CHAPTER 365.

**T**he horizon will be reflected according to the geometry of this <note>original: "per la sesta di questo"; likely referring to a diagram or the "sixth rule" of a specific optical section</note> on the side seen by the horizon and by the eye, as the horizon **F.** is shown seen from side **B. C.**, which side is also seen by the eye. Therefore you, painter, who have to represent the flooding of waters <term>innondationi|Moving or surging waters, rather than a static flood</term>, remember that the color of the water will not be seen by you as being any lighter or darker than the lightness or darkness of the place where you are, mixed together with the color of the other things that are behind you.

<note>Leonardo is explaining that water acts as a mirror; the "color" of a wave is actually a reflection of the environment behind the viewer and the sky above.</note>

# LEON BATTISTA
## ALBERTI
### ON PAINTING: <note>original: "DELLA PITTVRA"</note>
### ON SCULPTURE. <note>original: "DELLA STATVA"</note>

<note>Leon Battista Alberti (1404–1472) was a foundational figure of the Italian Renaissance—an architect, artist, and theorist. These two works established the intellectual framework for Western art. *On Painting* (1435) famously introduced the mathematical principles of linear perspective, while *On Sculpture* provided a system for measuring the human body with scientific accuracy.</note>

This suggests this specific edition was printed centuries after Alberti's death, during a time when his theories were being rediscovered by Enlightenment scholars.</note>

### TO MY MOST ILLUSTRIOUS AND MOST RESPECTED LORD,
# CHARLES ERRARD
## PAINTER TO THE MOST CHRISTIAN KING. <note>original: "Re' Cristianissimo," a traditional title for the Kings of France; here referring to Louis XIV.</note>

->MOST ILLUSTRIOUS LORD,<-

**D**edications are always well-bestowed and are primarily suited to those who profess the same arts and sciences that are taught in books; because if they value their own virtue and reputation, it follows that they must also prize the gift made to them. Furthermore, being of sufficient strength to resist the slanders of the envious and the quibbles of the ignorant, they preserve the dedicated works from every enchanting and poisonous tongue with the mere characters of their name. Driven by these considerations, but much more by the desire I have always had to reveal with some public act the great affection of my service toward Your Lordship, I offer you these two treatises revised by me, and almost resurrected, adding to them the life of their author **Leon Battista Alberti**, <note>A foundational Renaissance architect and polymath (1404–1472) whose theories on art and architecture shaped the era.</note> whom you deemed worthy to accompany his countryman **Leonardo da Vinci** even to the royal throne of the most virtuous **Queen of Sweden**. <note>Christina, Queen of Sweden (1626–1689), a famous patron of the arts who lived in Rome after her abdication.</note> Both writers owe much to the
