# L I F E

## OF LEON BATTISTA ALBERTI

### DESCRIBED BY RAFAELLE DU FRESNE.

*HOW ancient, noble, and powerful the Alberti family was in Florence is attested to by histories; and Scipione Ammirato <note>A celebrated 16th-century historian of Florence.</note>, who for certain reasons wished to elevate the name of the Concini family, found no more beautiful or expedient device than to place them in the company of the Alberti, assigning the same origin to both families. For our purposes, it will suffice to say that by 1304, they were already of great authority in Florence and favored the White faction <note>The "White" Guelfs (Bianchi), a political faction in medieval Florence that famously included the poet Dante Alighieri.</note>. In the year 1384, during the festivities held in Florence to celebrate the acquisition of Arezzo, the displays and pageantry of the Alberti were of such magnificence that they seemed more fitting for any great prince than for private individuals. In the records of the Republic, it is found that the Alberti held the office of <term>Gonfalonier <note>original: "gonfalonerato"; the "Gonfalonier of Justice" was the highest-ranking official in the Florentine government.</note></term> nine times, which was the supreme level of honor and power to which Florentines could aspire. But in the frequent storms and upheavals of public affairs, they did not always enjoy favorable fortune. In the year 1387, Cipriano and Benedetto degli Alberti were driven from their homeland, and then in 1411, even the children of that house were banished. But in 1428, the ban was lifted, and an order was given that everyone could come and reside freely in Florence. The aforementioned Cipriano was the father of Alberto, Lorenzo, and Giovanni. Alberto Alberti was first a canon, and then in the year 1437, Bishop of Camerino; and Pope Eugene [IV], who was welcomed by the Florentines into their city with such pageantry and demonstrations of affection, having tested the virtues of that prelate during the time he celebrated the Council there, honored him with the rank of <term>Cardinal <note>original: "cardinalato".</note></term> as a sign of gratitude toward his nation and as a reward due to his merits. Lorenzo, Alberto’s brother, left several sons: Bernardo, Carlo, and Leon Battista, whose rare qualities will provide ample material for praise in this brief discourse. With how much care and discipline these brothers were raised in their youth by their father can be read in the treatise that Leon Battista himself wrote on the advantages and disadvantages of letters <note>original: "delle commodità & incommodità delle lettere"; referring to Alberti's Latin work "De commodis litterarum atque incommodis," which reflects on the hardships and benefits of a scholarly life.</note>, where he recounts that all the hours of the day were distributed among their various studies in such a way that*

<summary>This page introduces the illustrious history of the Alberti family in Florence, detailing their political influence, periods of exile, and the rise of Leon Battista’s uncle to the cardinalate. It begins to describe the strict educational upbringing Leon Battista received from his father, Lorenzo.</summary>
<keywords>Leon Battista Alberti, Alberti family, Florence, Scipione Ammirato, Gonfalonier, Pope Eugene IV, exile, Renaissance education, biography</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Leon Battista Alberti. The previous page discussed the noble Alberti family of Florence, their political influence as Gonfaloniers, and their eventual exile and return to the city. It concluded by introducing Leon Battista and his brother Carlo as young men devoted to their studies.</meta>

## LIFE

they never remained idle. Having reached a more mature age, in addition to the study of letters, *Carlo* took up the care of domestic business, but *Leon Battista*, taking no account of anything but books, gave himself entirely to the cultivation of his intellect. He made such progress in the sciences that he surpassed all the talented men of fame living in his time.

The first proof he gave of the vivacity and sharpness of his genius was in deceiving the judgment of the learned men of his time with a scholarly and ingenious hoax—and with more success than Sigonio <note>Carlo Sigonio (c. 1524–1584), a famous humanist scholar who also attempted to pass off his own writing as an ancient work by Cicero.</note> later achieved. While studying in Bologna at the age of twenty, he secretly composed a play called *Philodoxios* under the name of "Lepidus the comic poet." He later published it as an ancient work, claiming it had recently come into his hands from an old manuscript. Indeed, Alberti imitated the ancient style of the Latin comic poets with such success that when it fell into the hands of *Aldo Manucci* <note>Also known as Aldus Manutius the Younger (1547–1597), a member of the famous Venetian printing family and a renowned scholar of Latin.</note>—who was considered by everyone to be the standard-bearer for the truest and purest Latinity—he had it printed in Lucca in the year 1588. He dedicated it to *Ascanio Persio*, another figure of profound erudition, as if it were the work of an ancient writer:

> I send to you, most learned Persio, the play *Lepidum* by Lepidus, an ancient comic poet, whoever he may be; since it came into my hands, I did not want it to perish, and I felt that consideration should be given to its antiquity. There are many things in it worthy of observation which will not displease you, a most skillful seeker of all antiquity, as they certainly pleased me, etc. <note>original: "Lepidum Lepidi , antiqui comici , quisquis ille sit , fabulam ad te mitto , eruditissime Persi , quę cum ad manus meas peruenerit , perire nolui : & antiquitatis rationem habendam esse duxi . Multa sunt in ea obseruatione digna , quæ tibi , totius vetustatis sollertissimo indagatori , non displacebunt , mihi certè cum placuerint , &c."</note>

But that Alberti composed this play in the twentieth year of his age, he himself wished to indicate in the prologue:

> I certainly do not desire, nor do I seek to be praised, because I have written this play somewhat clumsily in this twentieth year of my age. But I expect that you might be persuaded that I have not spent my years in idleness or entirely without care. <note>original: "Non quidem cupio , non peto in laudem trahi , quod hac vigesima annorum meorum ætate , hanc ineptius scripserim fabulam . Verum exspecto inde haberi apud vos hoc persuasionis , non vacuum me scilicet , non exundique incure meos obiuisse annos ."</note>

Having thus tested his strengths at that age, there was no science that Alberti did not acquire through study, letting no day pass without reading or composing something, as he himself states. He possessed a mind so versatile that he seemed equally born for every sort of discipline. It is not known whether he was a better orator or poet, whether he was a more excellent writer in Latin or the Tuscan dialect, whether he was more capable in the practical or speculative sciences, or whether he spoke with more gravity on lofty matters or with more grace and wit on ordinary and humble ones.

One reads that once *Lorenzo de' Medici*, the true Maecenas <note>A term for a generous patron of the arts, named after Gaius Maecenas, the advisor to Augustus Caesar.</note> of his century, in order to pass the great heat of summer with less discomfort, gathered a group of illustrious figures from every branch of literature in the forest of Camaldoli. Among these, *Marsilio Ficino*, *Donato Acciaioli*, *Leon Battista Alberti*, *Alamanno Rinuccini*, and *Cristoforo Landino* were the principals. Anyone can imagine what the conversation of such learned people was like. But more than any other, *Alberti* made himself admired; with lofty discourses full of sublime doctrine, he made it fully clear that within the *Aeneid*, under the shell of various and beautiful fictions, the highest secrets of philosophy were hidden, and that *Virgil* was a true and real philosopher, but dressed fantastically as a poet. Such solid reasoning made such an impression on the minds of the listeners that *Cristoforo Landino* (who on that occasion chose to be the secretary of the assembly) recorded them all in a book, and <note>This refers to Landino's famous work, the *Disputationes Camaldulenses* (Camaldolese Disputations), which features Alberti as a main speaker.</note>

<meta>catchword: ne</meta>

<summary>This page highlights Leon Battista Alberti's early intellectual brilliance, specifically his successful literary hoax of an ancient Latin play and his participation in the famous intellectual circle of Lorenzo de' Medici at Camaldoli.</summary>
<keywords>Leon Battista Alberti, Philodoxios, literary hoax, Aldo Manuzio, Lorenzo de' Medici, Camaldolese Disputations, Cristoforo Landino, Virgil's Aeneid, Renaissance Humanism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Leon Battista Alberti, transitioning from his early literary hoaxes to his reputation among humanists and his groundbreaking work in architecture.</meta>

# BY LEON BATTISTA ALBERTI.

...he then formed that work which is seen printed in the Latin language under the name of the *Camaldolese Disputations* <note>original: *Questioni Gamaldolensi*; a famous philosophical dialogue by Cristoforo Landino (1424–1498) in which Alberti is featured as a primary speaker on the active versus the contemplative life.</note>: in which toward the end Landino writes thus:

> *These are the things that I wished to remember from the many and far more excellent matters which Leon Battista Alberti discussed from memory, clearly and at length, in the gathering of such great men.* <note>original Latin: "Hæc sunt quæ de plurimis longeque eccellentioribus , quæ Leo Baptista Albertus memoriter , dilucide , ac copiose,in tantorum virorum concessu disputauit, meminisse volui."</note>

Alberti left many beautiful compositions in Latin and in Tuscan <note>The Italian vernacular of the time.</note>, of which a copious index will be seen below. Among the Latin works, the one titled *Momus* <note>original: *Momo*; a satirical work about the gods of Olympus used to critique human behavior and politics.</note> is worthy of eternal praise and can be compared with all of antiquity; for its excellence, it was printed twice in Rome in the same year, 1520. And truly in that work—with extraordinary charm and unexpected skill, joking, laughing, and mocking—he explains in four books those things which others wrote of moral philosophy in grave and severe manners. He primarily proposed to touch upon those things expected to form a perfect and excellent prince, and to recognize the character of those who surround him. Also beautiful is the small work called *Trivia*, or *Concerning the Causes Pertaining to Senators*, and that which he titled *On Law* <note>original: *De iure*.</note>, that is, *On the Administration of Justice*. I do not know for what reason Cosimo Bartoli <note>Cosimo Bartoli (1503–1572) was a diplomat and scholar who translated many of Alberti's works into Italian to make them accessible to a wider audience.</note>, who translated and printed many of Leon Battista Alberti’s pamphlets in one volume, made them the fifth and sixth books of *Momus*, or *Of the Prince*. He wrote a little book of fables, in which it is said that in the whimsy of his concepts he surpassed Aesop. He also composed a treatise on the life and habits of his dog, and another on the fly, being able with an artful manner to joke about important and grave matters, and to philosophize about low and base things. In the Italian language, he left three books on *Economy* <note>Likely referring to *Della Famiglia* (On the Family), his famous treatise on domestic life and finance.</note>, and some romantic things in prose and in verse; and he was the first (as Giorgio Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari (1511–1574) was the famous biographer of Italian Renaissance artists.</note> writes in his *Life*) who attempted to reduce vernacular verses to the measure of Latin ones, as seen in his epistle:

> *This very extreme, miserable letter I send*
> *To you who miserably despise us, etc.* <note>original: "Questa pur estrema miserabile piftola mando / A te che spregi miseramente noi, &c." This refers to Alberti's attempt to write Italian poetry using classical dactylic hexameters.</note>

But in discussing Alberti's singular genius in every kind of refined literature, and the place he holds among men of letters, I feel pulled by people of another profession—that is, by painters and architects—who claim him as their own. Showing me how much he achieved in painting and architecture, they call me back; and as if I were writing the virtues of another Alberti, they force me to make the passage from the speculative sciences to the practical and mechanical arts. And truly, so great was the capacity and vastness of our Alberti’s genius that he could not only embrace all disciplines with general knowledge but also descend into the particulars of each. Applying himself to whatever thing, he made men believe that he had never employed his most noble intellect in anything else, equaling or even surpassing those who were considered the best in that profession. In his time, the study of architecture was entirely dead, or if some knowledge of it was held, it was so corrupt and far from the refinement and nobility of the ancient Roman century that in practice it produced crude results. Leon Battista Alberti was the first who attempted to restore that art to its original purity, and by driving out the barbarism of the Gothic ages, he introduced order and proportion into it, so that he was universally called by everyone the

e <note>catchword</note>

<summary>This page catalogs Leon Battista Alberti's literary and philosophical works, highlighting his satire 'Momus' and his pioneering efforts to apply classical structures to Italian poetry. It then transitions to his monumental impact on architecture, where he is credited with reviving the pure Roman style and rejecting medieval 'Gothic' influences.</summary>

<warning>Water staining is visible on the bottom left corner of the page, though it does not obscure the text.</warning>

# LIFE

**The Florentine Vitruvius.** <note>This title compares Leon Battista Alberti to Vitruvius, the most famous architect of Greek and Roman antiquity.</note> The fame of his name induced Pope Nicholas V <note>Reigned 1447–1455; known for his grand urban planning in Rome.</note> to make use of him in the ordering of many buildings in Rome, and to trust his counsel all the more because Biondo of Forlì <note>Flavio Biondo (1392–1463), a pioneering humanist historian and geographer.</note>, a personage of high merit and a member of his household, had particularly informed him of Alberti's rare qualities.

For Sigismondo Pandolfo Malatesta, Lord of Rimini, he created the design for the Church of St. Francis <note>Now known as the *Tempio Malatestiano*, one of the most important buildings of the early Renaissance.</note>, which was begun in the year 1447 and turned out to be one of the most superb and sumptuous in Italy. It was brought to the state seen today in the year 1550 <note>The text likely contains a typographical error; the church's main construction was underway in 1450, as Alberti died in 1472.</note>. And because Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari (1511–1574), the famous biographer of Italian artists.</note>—who is often quite long-winded on matters of minor importance—has shown himself very sparse with words in his description of this temple, we shall supply what he neglected. Despite the fact that Vasari stayed in Rimini (where he painted the St. Francis seen on the high altar of said building) and could have observed every part minutely, we, to honor the memory of the architect, will write what appeared worthy of observation to us after considering it many times.

Beginning with the façade, we shall say that one sees a beautiful base made entirely of Istrian marble, which runs around the whole building and has as its crowning a beautiful ornament of foliage and Pandolfeschi arms <note>The heraldic symbols of the Malatesta family.</note>, intertwined together with charming invention. Above this rise four fluted columns of the composite order in half-relief. The three intervals are occupied by three niches, the middle one of which forms the main door, which recedes somewhat with a beautiful foliage decoration. Then follows the architrave, the frieze, and the cornice, above which, directly over the door, two pillars with a niche in the middle were intended to follow the same order; had this been completed, it would have served to provide light to the central nave and to house the statue of the Lord <note>Sigismondo Malatesta.</note>. On the outer side of the temple, with superb and noble invention, seven large arches are seen, and beneath them as many tombs, made specifically to serve as the burial places for illustrious men of Rimini.

The interior of the building yields nothing to the exterior, neither in the grandeur of the design nor in the delicacy of the ornaments, which, although they have a certain "Gothic" quality about them <note>The author acknowledges the transition from Medieval/Gothic styles to the Classical Renaissance style.</note>, are nonetheless not without praise if one considers the crudeness of that century. Marbles of various sorts, both inside and out, have been used with profusion; one reads in the life of Sigismondo that as he passed with his people near Ravenna, he took the opportunity to strip the ancient churches of San Severo and Classe <note>San Severo and Sant'Apollinare in Classe, famous Byzantine-era churches.</note>, removing the marble veneers and carrying to Rimini everything that seemed most appropriate to complete his work. For this, he was deservedly blamed by Pope Pius II and called sacrilegious. In one of the chapels (of which there are six), one sees the very beautiful and rich tombs of Sigismondo and Isotta, his wife <note>Isotta degli Atti, Sigismondo's longtime mistress and eventually his wife.</note>, and above one (as Vasari writes) is the portrait of the Lord himself, and in another part of the same work, that of Leon Battista.

In the year 1551 <note>Likely another typographical error for 1451, during the reign of Ludovico Gonzaga.</note>, Ludovico Gonzaga, Marquess of Mantua—who was devotedly attached to the Annunziata in Florence because of a vow made by his consort for a successful childbirth—had the choir, or tribune, that is currently seen in that church built according to the design of Leon Battista. It features the arms of the Gonzaga family around it. Just as it bears witness to the magnificence of that Lord, it also shows the skill of the architect, who with a capricious <note>In this context, "capricious" (capricciosa) means highly imaginative, original, or inventive.</note> and very difficult manner arranged that building in the style of a circular temple with nine chapels around it. And because there are certain things that do not appear to the eye with total...

-> BY LEON BATTISTA ALBERTI. <-

<warning>Water staining is present in the lower right quadrant of the page, though the text remains legible. The bottom edge of the paper is slightly damaged and stained.</warning>

...that grace which is required, for it appears—due to the curve of the structure—that the arches of the chapels fall backward when viewed in profile; we refer the reader to what *Vasari* writes regarding this.

The same Marquis <note>Ludovico III Gonzaga, Marquis of Mantua from 1444 to 1478.</note>, wishing to rebuild from its foundations the Church of St. Andrew in his own city—venerable for the blood of Christ which is preserved there <note>The Basilica of Sant'Andrea in Mantua houses a famous relic of the "Precious Blood," said to have been brought to the city by the Roman soldier Longinus.</note>—summoned Alberti to him in the year 1472. Having expressed his desire to illustrate Mantua with a most noble and proud temple, he had Alberti create the model for the new temple seen today. It is built entirely of terra cotta in the shape of a cross, with a single vault forming the lower part, placed over the main body of the church. It is 104 braccia long and 40 braccia wide <term>braccia: an Italian unit of measurement (literally "arms") roughly equal to 23 inches or 58 centimeters.</term>, without any iron or wooden tie-rods to support it. It is designed entirely in the composite order <term>Composite order: A sophisticated architectural style that combines the scrolls of the Ionic order with the acanthus leaves of the Corinthian order.</term>, with three large chapels on each side and an equal number of small ones. In the arms of the cross, there are two chapels for each, opposite one another. The center of the square, where the dome is to be built, is nearly forty braccia wide. Beyond the square of the dome is the oval-shaped choir, 52 braccia long and as wide as the body of the church; this, along with the aforementioned square, was completed up to the final cornices in the year of our Savior 1600, following Alberti’s ancient model. The facade is divided by three doors; the largest, in the middle, is decorated with white marble and beautifully carved foliage, while the smaller side doors are of grey marble, also finely worked. Anyone wishing to see every detail described more particularly should read Donesmondi <note>Ippolito Donesmondi, author of the *Ecclesiastical History of Mantua* (1612).</note> in the sixth book of his ecclesiastical history of Mantua, from which we have drawn what is mentioned above. Mario Equicola <note>An Italian humanist and historian (1470–1525).</note> teaches us in his Mantuan history that this same Alberti began the Church of St. Sebastian in that same city. He had as his assistant and faithful executor of his designs in Mantua one Luca Fiorentino <note>Commonly identified as the architect and sculptor Luca Fancelli (c. 1430–1494).</note>, who had previously worked for him in Florence on the construction of the choir of the Annunziata.

But if Rome, Rimini, and Mantua owe much to the industry of Leon Battista, his own homeland feels no less obliged to his virtue, for he contributed greatly to its beauty. The facade of the Church of Santa Maria Novella in Florence was arranged according to his design, artfully decorated with a charming mixture of black and white marble, corresponding to the grandeur of the building's entire body. He gave Cosimo Rucellai the design for the palace he had built on the street called *La Vigna*, and in the Church of San Pancrazio, one can see a chapel of his invention <note>The Rucellai Sepulchre, a small-scale recreation of the Holy Sepulchre of Jerusalem.</note>. He did many other things that are omitted here for the sake of brevity. He left very few works of painting. Paolo Giovio <note>A renowned 16th-century physician, historian, and biographer.</note>, who composed his eulogy and gave him a place among illustrious men of letters, praises the portrait he made of himself; at the time *Vasari* wrote, this was held in the house of Palla Rucellai, along with other paintings by the same Alberti.

It is evident, therefore, from what we have written above, that through his study of letters and his knowledge of design, Leon Battista Alberti can with excellent reason be recorded among the famous men of both professions. Indeed, to marry them even more closely together, he intended the discourses of the one to serve to illustrate the operations of the other, making those arts speak which in the past had remained almost mute, leaving their precepts written in a most beautiful style in the lan- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely intended to be "language" (linguaggio or lingua), referring to his treatises written in Latin and the Tuscan vernacular.</note>

<summary>This page details Alberti's major architectural projects in Mantua, specifically the Basilica of Sant'Andrea and San Sebastiano, and his contributions to Florentine landmarks like Santa Maria Novella and the Palazzo Rucellai. It highlights his unique ability to combine literary scholarship with practical architectural theory.</summary>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Leon Battista Alberti, transitioning from his architectural projects to a detailed account of his written treatises on the arts and his final bibliography.</meta>

<header>LIFE</header>
<warning>The bottom portion of the page exhibits water staining and discoloration, though the text remains clearly legible throughout the affected area.</warning>
<meta>catchword: Leonis</meta>

Latin language. Sculpture was the first art he undertook to treat, writing a small book in the Latin language titled *On Sculpture* <note>Original: "della statua." Though the text is Italian, it refers to his Latin work *De Statua*.</note>. He then wrote, in the same language, three books *On Painting*, which are highly praised by all experts, both for their noble and pure style and for the importance of their precepts. In the first book, the principles of the art are explained, drawn from geometry. The second contains the true rules from which the painter must never depart, concerning composition as much as drawing and coloring; these are the three things to which all considerations that can be made in painting are reduced. In the third book, he discusses the office of the painter and the goal he must set for himself in painting.

The last work of Leon Battista Alberti, and the most worthy of all, having been produced with the greatest study and diligence, is the book he wrote *On Architecture* <note>Original: "dell' architettura," referring to his Latin masterpiece *De re aedificatoria*.</note>. In this work, with exquisite order and great ease, all the secrets of that art are revealed—secrets which were previously locked away in the obscure writings of Vitruvius <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the Roman author of the only major ancient treatise on architecture to survive.</note>. It was not published until after his death by his brother Bernardo, who dedicated it to Lorenzo de' Medici <note>Known as "Lorenzo the Magnificent," the de facto ruler of the Florentine Republic and a great patron of the arts.</note>, just as the author himself had intended to do. It was translated into the Italian language and illustrated with drawings by Cosimo Bartoli, a Florentine gentleman, who presented it to Cosimo de' Medici in the year 1550. The same Bartoli also translated the books on painting and sculpture and had them printed in the year 1568 along with Alberti’s other short works. There already existed another version of the treatise on painting, made by Domenichi <note>Ludovico Domenichi (1515–1564), an Italian translator and editor.</note> and printed in the year 1447.

After having discussed the virtues of Leon Battista in a language foreign to us <note>Referring to the previous sections written in or discussing his Latin works.</note> to accompany this volume, and having admired the fruits of his most fertile intellect, nothing remains for us to say except that we desire—for the merit of such a great man, and even more for the public good and the glory of letters—that all his works be gathered together one day. To this end, we provide the list below. Alberti died in Florence, his homeland, and was buried in the church of Santa Croce.

# LIFE OF LEON BATTISTA ALBERTI.

*The Life of Saint Potitus.* <note>Alberti wrote this hagiography of the martyr Potitus in Latin (*Vita Sancti Potiti*) around 1433, likely to demonstrate his classical prose skills to the Papal Curia.</note>
*The Cipher.* <note>Original: "La Cifra." This refers to his groundbreaking treatise on cryptography, *De componendis cifris*, which invented the polyalphabetic cipher disk.</note>
*Mathematical Recreations.* <note>Original: "Le piaceuolezze Matematiche," usually known by the Latin title *Ludi rerum mathematicarum*. It explores practical geometry, mechanics, and surveying in a playful, accessible manner.</note>
*On the republic, on civil and rustic life, and on fortune.* I would believe that this treatise was written in the Tuscan tongue by **Alberti**, as we noted above.
*On the Statue.* <note>Original: "Della statua." This is the Italian translation of *De Statua*, Alberti’s foundational text on the theory of sculpture and human proportions.</note>
*On Painting.* <note>Original: "Della pittura." One of the most significant works of the Renaissance, it provided the first mathematical definition of linear perspective.</note>
*On the Fly.* <note>Original: "Della mosca." A satirical or mock-encomium written in the style of the ancient Greek author Lucian, praising the common fly.</note>
*On the Dog.* <note>Original: "Del Cane." A mock-heroic funeral oration for his beloved dog.</note>
*One Hundred Apologues.* <note>Original: "Cento Apologi." A collection of short, Aesop-like fables and moral observations written in a concise, pithy style.</note>
*Hecatomfila.* <note>Greek for "The woman of a hundred loves." An Italian dialogue regarding the art of love and female desire.</note>
*Deiphira.* <note>A dialogue focusing on how to escape the pains of unrequited or harmful love.</note>
These two last works were not translated by **Bartoli** <note>Cosimo Bartoli (1503–1572) was a Florentine scholar famous for translating Alberti’s Latin architecture and art treatises into the vernacular.</note>, but are the same as those mentioned above under the titles **On Love** and **On the Remedy for Love**, written in the Tuscan language by **Alberti**. <note>The author clarifies that while some knew these works by their Latin-style titles (*De amore* and *De remedio amoris*), they were actually original Italian compositions, not translations of Latin texts.</note>

It specifically clarifies that his famous works on love were originally written in Italian rather than being translations by Cosimo Bartoli.</summary>

# LIFE OF LEON BATTISTA ALBERTI.

<meta>This page transitions from the list of Leon Battista Alberti’s varied intellectual works to the formal dedication of the translation. The translator, Cosimo Bartoli, addresses his contemporary, the famous artist and historian Giorgio Vasari. # COSIMO BARTOLI

## TO THE VIRTUOUS GIORGIO VASARI

### MOST EXCELLENT PAINTER AND ARCHITECT.

*What could I say of you, my dear Master Giorgio, more than what your infinite and praiseworthy works already say of you, both in painting, in writing, and in architecture? These works speak so clearly of your praises without a tongue, that the tongues or pens of others must yield to your brushes, your styles, and your drawings. Most recently, that most stupendous and praiseworthy great hall of the royal palace of their Highnesses* <note>Bartoli is referring to the *Salone dei Cinquecento* in the Palazzo Vecchio, Florence, which Vasari extensively remodeled and decorated for Duke Cosimo I de' Medici.</note> *—constructed with such art and with such wonderful judgment by your most refined genius—has left all men who see it so marveled that they stand like marble statues for a long time, almost senseless, while contemplating it. Thus, I judge it much better to leave this marvel in the minds of the public, rather than seeking to increase with words that which I would certainly only diminish. But I did not take up the pen for this purpose; let men of judgment marvel at your works, let them follow you, and let your rivals imitate you without envy. May the youth run continually to learn from your drawings, from your coloring, and from your teachings. In the meantime, do not be indignant that this little work on painting by the most virtuous Leon Battista Alberti should be published—translated by me into our own language* <note>original: "in questa nostra lingua" — Bartoli translated the Latin text into the Tuscan vernacular to make it accessible to practicing artists.</note> *—under your name. I do this so that beginners in painting may take from this little work the first elements, as they say, and then learn the perfections of good painting from your own marvelous works. Love me as you are accustomed to do, and live happily.*

<summary>A formal dedication from translator Cosimo Bartoli to Giorgio Vasari, praising Vasari's work on the Palazzo Vecchio and presenting this translation of Alberti as a foundational text for young artists.</summary>

## ->LEON<-
# ->BATTISTA ALBERTI<-
## ->ON PAINTING.<-

### BOOK ONE.

<insert>S</insert>INCE I am to write about painting in these very brief commentaries, so that my speech may be clearer, I shall first take from mathematicians those things that seem to me relevant to the subject. Once these are understood, I will declare (as far as my talent serves) from those principles of nature what painting is. But in all my reasoning, I want it to be noted that I will speak of these things not as a mathematician, but as a painter. For mathematicians consider the appearance and forms of things with the intellect alone, separate from any matter. But because I want the subject to be placed before the eyes, I will use in my writing, as the saying goes, a <term>fatter Minerva</term> <note>original: "più grassa Minerva." This is a translation of the Latin idiom *crassiore Minerva*, meaning a practical, down-to-earth approach rather than an abstract or subtle one.</note>. And truly it will seem to me that I have done enough if painters, in reading this, understand in some way this truly difficult material, about which—as far as I have seen—no one has yet written. I ask, therefore, as a favor, that these writings of mine be interpreted not as by a pure mathematician, but as by a painter.

Therefore, one must first know that a <term>point</term> <note>Alberti begins with the Euclidean definitions of geometry, but adapts them for the visual art of perspective.</note> is a mark (so to speak) that cannot be divided into parts. A "mark" I call, in this place, anything that is on a <term>surface</term> in such a way that it can be perceived by the eye. For those things that are not perceived by the eye, no one can deny that they have nothing to do with the painter, since the painter strives to imitate only those things that can be seen by means of <term>light</term>.

These points, if placed continuously one after another in order, will extend into a <term>line</term>. And the line, for our purposes, will make a mark whose length can be divided into parts, but it will be so very thin that it can never be split <note>Alberti is defining a line as having length but negligible width, suitable for the "outline" of a drawing.</note>.

<summary>Alberti begins his foundational treatise on painting by establishing a practical, geometric framework, defining the basic elements of art—points, lines, and surfaces—from the perspective of a painter rather than an abstract mathematician.</summary>

<keywords>Leon Battista Alberti, On Painting, geometry, perspective, point, line, surface, light, art theory</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues Leon Battista Alberti’s introduction to the geometric foundations of painting. Having just defined a "point" as a mark on a surface, he now proceeds to define lines, surfaces, and the specific geometric shapes like circles that form the basis of a painter's perspective.</meta>

## ->ON PAINTING<-

<warning>Water staining visible in the lower left corner of the page.</warning>

Of lines, some are straight and some are curved. A **straight line** is a mark drawn straight along its length from one point to another. A **curved line** is one that is drawn not straight from one point to another, but instead forms an arc. Many lines, like threads in a cloth, if joined together, will create a <term>surface</term> <note>In Alberti's theory, the "surface" is the two-dimensional plane that a painter works upon or perceives.</note>; for a surface is that outer part of a body that is considered not in terms of its depth, but only in terms of its width and length, which are its proper qualities. Of these qualities, some are so inherent in the surface that they cannot be moved or separated from it unless the surface is completely altered. Other qualities are such that, while the face of the surface remains the same, they appear to the viewer to change, so that the surface seems altered to those looking at it. The perpetual qualities of surfaces are two: one is recognized by that outer circuit by which the surface is enclosed, which some call the **horizon**. We, if we may be permitted, will use a certain similarity to call it by the Latin term <term>Ora</term> <note>original: "Ora." Alberti uses the Latin word for "border" or "edge" to describe the outline of a shape.</note>, or if we prefer, the **outline** [**d’intorno**]. This outline will be formed by one or more lines: by one, such as a circular line; or by more, such as a curved line and a straight one, or even by several straight lines or several curved lines. A **circular line** is that which embraces and contains within itself the entire space of a circle. And a **circle** is a type of surface surrounded by a line like a crown.

In the middle of which, if a point is placed, all the rays that extend in length from this point and go straight to the crown or circumference will be equal to one another.

And this same point is called the **center** of the circle. The straight line that cuts the circumference twice and passes through the center is called the **diameter** of the circle by mathematicians.

We shall call this same line the <term>centric</term> <note>original: "centrica." Alberti often renames mathematical terms to emphasize their role in the "centric" or central point of a painter's perspective.</note>. And let us be persuaded here by what the mathematicians say: that no line which cuts the circumference—

# BOOK ONE.
<sig>a ij</sig>

[circumfe]rence cannot, within that circumference, form equal angles, except for the one that touches the center.

---

But let us return to surfaces. From what I have said above, it can be easily understood how, by changing the drawing of the outer lines—or rather the <term>outline</term> <note>original: "d’intorno." This refers to the perimeter or contour of a shape.</note> of a surface—that surface immediately loses its name and its original appearance. What was perhaps called a triangle might now be called a quadrangle, or perhaps a polygon. The outline will be considered changed whenever the lines or the angles are made not only more numerous, but more obtuse, longer, sharper, or shorter. This point reminds us to say something regarding angles. 

An angle is truly that which is formed by two lines that intersect each other at the edge of a surface. There are three kinds of angles: <term>right</term> <note>original: "a squadra," literally "at the square."</note>, <term>acute</term> <note>original: "sotto squadra," literally "under the square."</note>, and <term>obtuse</term> <note>original: "sopra squadra," literally "above the square."</note>. A **right angle** is one of those four angles made by two straight lines that intersect each other in such a way that it is equal to any of the other three remaining angles. From this, it follows that they tell us all right angles are equal to one another.

An **obtuse angle** is that which is greater than a right angle.

An **acute angle** is that which is smaller than a right angle.

Let us return again to the surface. We have described how, by means of an outline, a quality was imprinted upon the surface; it remains for us to speak of the other quality of surfaces, which is (so to speak) almost like a skin stretched over the entire face of the surface. This is divided into three types. Some are **flat and uniform**, others are **spherical and swollen**, and others are **hollowed and concave**. To these, a fourth type may be added: those surfaces composed of the aforementioned types. We shall treat these later; let us now speak of the first.

A **plane surface** is that which, when a <term>straightedge</term> <note>original: "regolo." A tool used to check the flatness of a surface.</note> is placed upon it, touches...

<summary>Alberti defines the properties of angles (right, acute, and obtuse) and begins categorizing surfaces into plane, spherical, and concave types based on their "skin" or texture.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on surface types and geometric definitions, specifically moving from the description of a plane surface into curved and compound surfaces, followed by the introduction of optics and perspective.</meta>

...equally throughout every part of it. Very similar to this will be the surface of a very pure water that is still. <note>The author is finishing a description of a plane surface.</note>

A **spherical surface** mimics the <term>outline</term> <note>original: "d'intorno." In this context, it refers to the exterior contour or perimeter of a shape.</note> of a sphere. They say a sphere is a round body, rotatable in every direction, in the middle of which is a point from which all the outermost parts of that body are equally distant.

A **concave surface** is one that has its boundary on the inner side, which is located beneath, so to speak, the "skin" of the sphere, such as the innermost surfaces inside eggshells.

But a **compound surface** is one that has one part of itself plane and the other either concave or round, such as the inner surfaces of reeds, or the outer surfaces of columns or pyramids.

Therefore, the qualities found either in the perimeter or on the faces of the surface have imposed various names upon surfaces, as was said. But there are also two qualities which change their appearance without the surface itself being altered: for if the location or the lights are changed, they appear varied to those who look at them. We shall speak of location first, and then of lights. And one must certainly first consider in what way, when the location is changed, those qualities that are in the surface appear to be changed. These matters truly pertain to the power and virtue of the eyes: for it is necessary that the outlines, either by moving further away or by changing position, appear to us either smaller, or larger, or entirely different from how they first appeared to us. Or likewise, the surfaces may appear to us either enriched or diminished in color. All these things are what we measure or reason with the <term>visual square</term> <note>original: "squadro." This refers to the geometric framework or "visual pyramid" used to calculate perspective.</note>. And we are now investigating how this square or sight is formed. We begin with the opinion of the philosophers, who say that surfaces are examined by means of certain rays—servants of the sight—which they therefore call **visual rays**, meaning that through them the <term>images</term> <note>original: "simulacri." These are the visual likenesses or species of objects that the eye perceives.</note> of things are imprinted upon the sense. For these same rays, between the eye and the viewed surface, stretched by their own...

### BOOK ONE.

...by their own nature, and through a certain marvelous subtlety of theirs, they run together most brilliantly, penetrating the air and other similar <term>rare or diaphanous</term> <note>Refers to transparent or semi-transparent materials, like air, water, or glass, that allow light to pass through.</note> bodies, having light as their guide, until they encounter some dense body that is not completely dark; in which place, striking with their tip, they immediately stop. But among the <term>ancients</term> <note>The author refers to classical Greek and Roman scholars. Perspective theory drew heavily on ancient optics (Euclid and Ptolemy).</note> there was no small dispute as to whether these rays issued from the eyes or from the surface <note>The author is referencing the "extramission" theory (light comes from the eyes) versus the "intromission" theory (light comes from objects). He chooses to bypass the physics to focus on the geometry.</note>. This dispute—which is indeed very difficult and, for our purposes, unnecessary—we shall set aside. Let us be permitted to imagine that these rays are like very thin threads tied together at one end, held very straight as if in a bundle, and that they are received within the eye where vision is formed or created. There they stay, like a trunk or stem of rays; from this place, the busy rays emerge and extend to full length like very straight <term>shoots</term> <note>original: "vermene." While literally meaning "worms" or "thin twigs," it here describes the straight, thin lines of light.</note>, rushing toward the surface that stands opposite them. But among these rays there is a certain difference that is well worth knowing, for they differ both in strength and in function. Some of them, by touching the <term>outlines</term> <note>original: "i d'intorni." The perimeter or boundary of the object being viewed.</note> of the surface, encompass the entire dimensions of that surface. Because these go flying through the air and barely touch the outermost parts of the surface, we shall call them **extreme or final rays**.

---

<insert>Take notice that this surface is shown facing forward so that the four final rays can be seen going to the points by which the surface is bounded.</insert>

Other rays—whether received from or issued by the entire face of the surface—also perform their function within that <term>pyramid</term> <note>The "visual pyramid" is the core concept of Renaissance perspective; the eye is the point, and the object is the base.</note> of which we shall speak shortly below; for they fill themselves with the same colors and lights with which that surface shines. And for this reason, we call these **middle or median rays**.

The entire picture is a single surface, but if one has to paint an <term>octagon</term> <note>An eight-sided geometric shape, used here as a model to demonstrate how sightlines define form.</note> within it, rays are shown which are called **median** <note>original: "mezzani." These are the rays that fill the space between the outer edges and the center of the "visual pyramid."</note> rays, which go from the eye to the points of the **layout** <note>original: "scompartimento." This refers to the specific arrangement or division of the shape on the flat surface.</note> of the octagon.
Among these rays, one is also found that is made in the likeness of that centric line we mentioned; it can be called the **centric ray**, or the ray of the center, because it rests on the surface in such a way that it creates equal angles around itself on every side. <note>In modern terms, this is the "principal ray" or "orthogonal," which strikes the picture plane at a perfect 90-degree angle.</note>

->[A geometric diagram demonstrating the visual pyramid with an octagon inscribed within a rectangular frame, illustrating perspective lines (rays) meeting at a single point.]<-

Thus, we have found the rays to be of three kinds: the **outer**, the **median**, and the **centric**. Let us now investigate what each of these kinds of rays contributes to **vision** <note>original: "veduta."</note>. First, we shall speak of the outer rays, then the median, and finally the centric. With the outer rays, **quantities** <note>original: "quantità." In Renaissance geometry, "quantity" refers to the measurable dimensions (length and width) of the objects being viewed.</note> are perceived; and quantity is truly that space which lies between two separate points of the outline, which passes through the su-

<summary>Alberti defines three types of visual rays—outer, median, and centric—that form the geometry of sight. He explains that the centric ray is unique because it is perpendicular to the surface, and begins to explain how outer rays allow us to perceive the dimensions (quantities) of objects.</summary>

<header>### BOOK ONE</header>

surface; this space is perceived by the eye with these outer rays, almost, so to speak, as if with **compasses** <note>original: "seste." This refers to the mathematical tool used for measuring and drawing circles, emphasizing that sight "measures" the dimensions of an object.</note>. And there are as many dimensions on a surface as there are separate points along a perimeter that face one another. For with our sight, we recognize length by means of its height or depth; width by means of right or left; thickness by means of closeness or distance; or indeed, we understand all other measures, whatever they may be, only with these outer rays. Hence it is commonly said that sight occurs by means of a **triangle** <note>Alberti uses the triangle as a simplified 2D version of the 3D visual pyramid.</note>, the base of which is the dimension seen, and the sides of which are those same rays that emerge from the points of the dimension and come to the eye. And it is a most certain thing that no dimension is seen except by means of this triangle. The sides of the visual triangle are therefore manifest. But in this same triangle there are two angles—namely, those at the two ends of the dimension. However, the third and principal angle is that which is formed within the eye, opposite the base.

---

->A B C. This can be called the **pyramid** <term>piramide: The three-dimensional cone of light rays that extend from an object to the eye, forming the geometric basis of perspective.</term>.<-

---

Nor is this the place to dispute whether this sight rests, as they say, in the junction of the **inner nerve** <term>neruo interiore: The optic nerve, which Renaissance thinkers believed was the point where visual information was processed.</term>, or whether the images are formed on the surface of the eye, as if in a **living mirror** <term>specchio animato: A poetic description of the eye's ability to reflect and capture images of the world.</term>. But one must not recount in this place all the functions of the eyes regarding sight, since it will be enough to briefly put in these commentaries those things that seem necessary to us. Since, then, the principal **visual angle** <term>angolo visivo: The angle formed at the eye by the rays coming from the extremities of an object.</term> resides in the eye, this rule has been derived: the more acute the angle is in the eye, the smaller the seen dimension will appear. From which it is clearly seen why it happens that from a long distance, the seen dimension appears to thin out, as if it comes to a point. But even though things are in this manner, it nonetheless happens with some surfaces that the closer the eye of the viewer approaches them, the smaller they appear; and the further the eye moves away from them, the larger they seem.

<summary>Alberti explains that vision operates through a "visual triangle" or "pyramid," where the object forms the base and the eye forms the apex. He establishes the fundamental rule of perspective: objects appear smaller as the visual angle in the eye becomes more acute.</summary>
<keywords>visual pyramid, visual angle, optic nerve, geometry of sight, perspective theory, Leon Battista Alberti, optics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Alberti's geometric explanation of vision. Having previously defined the "outer rays" that mark the edges of an object, he now explores how distance changes our perception and introduces the "visual pyramid," the "middle rays" which carry color, and the "centric ray."</meta>

# ON PAINTING
<page-num>8</page-num>

...that part of the surface: which is clearly seen on spherical surfaces.
Dimensions, therefore, by means of the **interval** <note>original: "interuallo." In this context, it refers to the distance or space between the eye and the object.</note>, sometimes appear larger or smaller to the viewer. Anyone who understands the reason for this will not doubt for a moment that **middle rays** <term>raggi mezzani: The light rays that fall between the outer edges of a visual pyramid, carrying the internal information of color and light.</term> sometimes become **outer rays** <term>raggi vltimi: Also called "extrinsic rays," these define the perimeter of the object being seen.</term>, and outer rays, when the interval changes, become middle rays. Therefore, one should know that when middle rays become outer rays, the dimensions will immediately appear smaller. Conversely, when the outer rays are drawn closer within the perimeter; the further they are from the perimeter, the larger that dimension will appear. Here, therefore, I am accustomed to giving a rule to my close friends: the more rays we occupy with our sight, the larger we should think the seen dimension is; and the fewer we occupy, the smaller. Finally, these outer rays, embracing every part of the entire perimeter of a surface, circle around and around, almost like a **moat** <note>original: "fossa." Alberti uses the metaphor of a ditch or moat surrounding a fortified area to describe how the rays encompass the shape.</note>, surrounding that entire surface. Hence, they say that sight is achieved by means of a **pyramid of rays**.

Now we must say what a pyramid is. The **pyramid** is a figure of a long body, from the base of which all straight lines drawn upwards end in a single point. The base of the pyramid is the surface being seen; the sides of the pyramid are those visual rays which we call the outer rays. The tip of the pyramid stays right here within the eye, where the angles of the dimension join together: and let this suffice for the outer rays, of which the pyramid is made, through which one sees for every reason that it matters greatly what and how many intervals there are between the eye and the surface.

It remains for us to treat the **middle rays**. The middle rays are that multitude of rays which, surrounded by the outer rays, are found to be inside the pyramid. And these rays do what it is said the **chameleon** does, and similar beasts terrified by fear, which are accustomed to take on the colors of the things closest to them so as not to be found by hunters. This is what the middle rays do. For from their contact with the surface until the tip of the pyramid, finding throughout this entire stretch a variety of colors and lights, they are so stained by them that at whatever point you might cut them, they would project from themselves in that same place that same light and that same color with which they have been soaked. And these middle rays, by the very nature of the fact, it has been seen first that over a long distance they fail and cause sight to be weaker; finally, the reason why this happens has been found. Since these same rays, and all other visual rays, being full and heavy with light and color, pass through the air—and the air itself being full of some thickness—it happens that because of the great part of the weight, while they flow through the air, they are pulled downward like they are tired. And therefore they say rightly that the greater the distance, the more the surface appears darker and more obscured <note>Alberti is describing an early theory of atmospheric perspective. He suggests that light "tires" or loses energy as it travels through the density of the atmosphere.</note>.

It remains for us to treat the **centric ray**. We call the **centric ray** that which alone strikes the dimension in such a way that the equal angles on both sides correspond to the angles that are beside them. And truly,

Regarding what pertains to this **centric ray** <term>raggio centrico: The central axis of the visual pyramid, which strikes the object directly and provides the sharpest focus.</term>, it is most true that of all rays, this is the strongest and most vivid. Nor can it be denied that no quantity will ever appear larger to the sight than when the centric ray is upon it. Many more things could be recounted concerning the power and function of the centric ray, but let this one thing not be left behind: this ray alone is strengthened by all the other rays that have surrounded it, as if they had formed a certain united congregation to favor it, so much so that it can rightly be called the head and the prince of rays. Let other things be left behind that would seem more appropriate for the ostentation of wit than suitable for those things we have ordained to say; many things will yet be said of rays more conveniently in their proper places.

->*A, B, C, and D are the outer rays; all the others are middle rays.*<-

<note>This caption refers to the accompanying diagram, illustrating how the "outer rays" define the boundary of a shape (like the octagon shown), while the "middle rays" fill the interior space.</note>

The middle rays of an octagon can be called an eight-faced pyramid within a four-faced pyramid.

And let it suffice in this place to have recounted these things, as far as the brevity of these commentaries allows, so that no one may doubt that the matter stands in this way—which I believe has been sufficiently shown: namely, that when the **interval** <note>original: "interuallo." This refers to the distance between the eye and the object.</note> is changed, and the position of the centric ray is changed, it immediately appears that the surface has been altered. For it will appear either smaller or larger, or changed, according to the order that the lines or angles have among themselves. Therefore, the position of the centric ray and the distance contribute greatly to the true certainty of the sight.

There is yet another certain third thing, by means of which surfaces appear different and varied to the viewer: this is the **reception of light** <term>riceuimento de’ lumi: How light falls upon a surface, creating highlights and shadows that define its three-dimensional form.</term>. For it can be seen in a **spherical surface** and in a concave one, that if there is only one light, the surface will appear somewhat dark on one side and brighter on the other. And from the same original distance, and keeping the original position of the centric ray fixed, provided that this surface...

...is subjected to a light different from the first, you will see that those parts of the surface which appeared bright under the first light will now, the light having changed, appear dark, and the dark parts will appear bright. And besides this, if there are several lights around it, different degrees of darkness and brightness will appear on such surfaces, and they will vary according to the quantity and the strength of the lights. This fact is proved by experience <note>original: "esperienzia"; in this context, Alberti refers to both "experience" and "experimentation," the foundation of his scientific approach to art.</note>.

---

But this point reminds us that some things should be said about light and colors. It is obvious that colors vary by means of light, since no color whatsoever appears to our sight in shadow the same as it appears when it is placed in the rays of light. For shadow shows the color obscured, and light makes it bright and clear. The philosophers <note>Alberti likely refers to Aristotelian optics or contemporary scholastic philosophers who debated the nature of light as a "form" that makes color visible.</note> say that nothing can be seen unless it is clothed in light and color; therefore, there is a great kinship between colors and light in creating sight. How great this relationship is can be seen from the fact that when light is absent, they <note>The sentence ends abruptly here, suggesting the thought continues on the next page regarding the disappearance of colors in the dark.</note>

<meta>This page continues Alberti's discussion from the end of the previous page, which concluded with the idea that light and color are intrinsically linked. He moves from the theoretical existence of color to how a painter practically categorizes and uses it.</meta>

II
### BOOK ONE.
b ij
<meta>image quality: high; catchword: de'</meta>

colors also, gradually becoming dark, they too disappear; and when the light or brightness returns, the colors also return together with it to our sight, through the power of the lights. Since this is the case, it will be well first to treat colors, and then we will go on investigating how said colors vary by means of light. Let us set aside that philosophical dispute through which the births and first origins of colors are investigated. For what does it matter to the painter to have known how color is generated from the mixing of the rare and the dense, or from that of the hot and the dry, or from that of the cold and the moist? <note>Alberti is dismissing the Aristotelian "Four Qualities" of matter (hot, cold, wet, dry) which natural philosophers used to explain the physical world, arguing that these scientific theories are less useful to an artist than practical observation.</note> Nor do I despise those who, philosophizing, dispute about colors in such a way that they affirm there are seven species of colors: that is, that **white** and **black** are the two extremes, between which there is one in the middle; and that between each of these two extremes and the middle one, there are two others on each side; and because one of these two approaches the extreme more than the other, they place them in a way that it seems they doubt where to put them. To the painter, it is enough to know what the colors are, and in what way they are to use them in painting. I would not wish to be rebuked by those who know more, who, while following the philosophers, say that in the nature of things there are found only two true colors, namely white and black, and that all the others are born from the mixing of these. I truly, as a painter, understand it in this way regarding colors: that through the mixing of colors, other almost infinite colors are born.

But among painters, there are four true types <term>generi: Alberti uses this to mean "primary classes" or "genera"</term> of colors, just as there are also four elements, from which many, many species are drawn. For there is that which seems like fire, so to speak, namely **red**; then that of the air, which is called **blue**; that of water is **green**; and that of the earth has the **ash-color** <note>original: "cenerognolo." In the Renaissance, earth was often associated with ochre or greyish tones rather than just brown.</note>. All other colors we see are made of mixtures, no differently than it seems to us with jasper and porphyry <note>original: "diaſpro & il porfido." These are variegated stones used here as examples of how distinct colors can blend into a composite appearance.</note>. Therefore, there are four types of colors from which, through the mixing of white and black, innumerable species are generated; for we see green leaves lose so much of their greenness little by little until they become white. We see the same thing also in the air itself, which sometimes, having taken the quality of some white vapor toward the horizon, returns gradually to its own color. Beyond this, we see the same in roses, some of which are sometimes so bright in color that they imitate **crimson** <note>original: "chermiſi"</note>, others seem the color of a young girl's cheeks, and others appear white as ivory. The color of the earth also, through the mixing of white and black, has its species. Thus, the mixing of white does not change the *types* of colors, but generates and creates their species. And the color black has the same power similarly; for through the mixing of black, many species are generated. This is quite right, because color is altered by means of shadow, where before it was clearly seen; for as shadow increases, the brightness and whiteness of the color fails, and as light increases, it becomes clearer and more candid. And therefore the painter can be sufficiently persuaded that white and black are not true colors, but the "alterers," so to speak, of

<summary>Alberti rejects purely philosophical definitions of color in favor of a practical system for painters, identifying four primary colors corresponding to the four elements: red (fire), blue (air), green (water), and ash-gray (earth).</summary>
<keywords>primary colors, four elements, red, blue, green, ash-gray, white and black as modifiers, Alberti, optics, Renaissance color theory</keywords>

# ON PAINTING

colors. Since the painter has found nothing more than white, by means of which he can express that ultimate brightness of light, nor anything with which he can represent the obscurity of shadows more than black. Add to these things that you will never find white or black anywhere that does not itself fall under some kind of color.

Let us treat now the power of lights. Lights are either from constellations—that is, from the Sun, the Moon, and the star of Venus—or from material lights and fire. And between these there is a great difference. For the lights of the heavens make shadows almost equal to the bodies; but fire makes them larger than the bodies are, and the shadow is caused by the rays of the light being intercepted. The intercepted rays are either bent elsewhere or they double back on themselves. They bend just as when the sun's rays strike the surface of the water, and then from there they rise to the ceilings; and every bending of the rays is done, as mathematicians say, with equal angles to one another <note>Alberti is describing the law of reflection: the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection.</note>. But these things belong to another part of painting. The rays that bend are soaked <note>original: "s’inzuppano". Alberti uses this vivid term to suggest that light literally "drenches" itself in the color of the surface it bounces off of.</note> in some part of that color which they find on that surface from which they are bent or reflected. And we see this happen when the faces of those who walk through meadows appear green to us. I have treated, therefore, the surfaces; I have treated the rays; I have treated how the <term>visual pyramid</term> <note>The "visual pyramid" is Alberti's geometric model for vision, where the object is the base and the eye is the point (apex).</note> is made from triangles. I have proved how greatly it matters that the interval, the positioning of the <term>centric ray</term> <note>The centric ray is the straight line of sight that hits the center of the object being viewed.</note>, and the reception of lights be determined and certain. But since with a single glance we see not just one surface alone, but multiple surfaces at once. And since we have treated, and not superficially, each surface by itself, now it remains for us to investigate in what way several surfaces joined together present themselves to the eyes. Each surface certainly enjoys being particularly filled with its own lights and its own colors, just as has been said of its own pyramid. And since bodies are covered by surfaces, all the quantities of the bodies that we see, and all the surfaces, create a single pyramid, pregnant, so to speak, with as many minor pyramids as there are surfaces that, through that view, are encompassed by the rays of said view. And things being thus, someone might perhaps say: What need does the painter have for such consideration? Or what utility will it give him in painting? This is certainly done so that he may know that he is bound to become an excellent master whenever he knows the differences of surfaces perfectly and notes their proportions; which has been known by very few. For if they are asked what it is they seek to achieve in coloring that surface, they can answer much better about anything else than know how to tell the reason for what they are striving to do. For which reason I pray that studious painters listen to me. Because learning those things that are useful was never a bad thing, from whatever master one might wish. And let them truly learn, while they circumscribe <note>To "circumscribe" here means to draw the outline or "circumference" of a shape.</note> a surface with lines and while they cover the drawn and defined places with colors, that nothing is more sought after than that on this single surface there are represented many...

<summary>Alberti explains how light reflects and carries color (like green grass reflecting onto a face) and argues that a master painter must understand the geometry of surfaces and the "visual pyramid" to truly master their craft.</summary>

Having previously established that light and color are essential to vision, Alberti now moves to define painting itself as a mathematical cross-section of the sightlines between the eye and the object.</meta>

### BOOK ONE.

...forms of surfaces: no differently than if this surface, which they cover with colors, were as if made of glass or some other similar transparent thing, such that through it the entire <term>visual pyramid</term> <note>Alberti’s term for the cone of light rays traveling from an object to the eye.</note> passed to see the real bodies, with a determined and fixed distance, and with a fixed position of the centric ray, and of the lights placed in the air at their distant locations. And that this is so, painters demonstrate when they step back from the thing they are painting to consider it from a distance; guided by nature, they go seeking in this way the tip of that very pyramid. From which they realize that from that place they consider and judge all things better.

But since this is a single surface—either of a panel or a wall—on which the painter strives to paint several and diverse surfaces and pyramids contained within a single pyramid, it will be necessary that in some part of its location, this visual pyramid be cut, so that in this place the painter, with both lines and painting, can express the outlines and colors that the cut will give him. This being the case, those who look at the painted surface see a certain cut <note>original: "taglio". In modern geometry, this is often called the "intersection" or "section" of the visual pyramid.</note> of the pyramid. Therefore, **painting will be the cut of the visual pyramid** according to a determined space or distance with its center, and with determined lights, represented with lines and colors upon a given surface.

Now, since we have said that painting is a cut of the pyramid, we must therefore go about investigating all those things by which all parts of such a cut might become very well known to you. We must, therefore, speak again of the surfaces from which it has been shown the pyramids come, which must be cut by painting. Some of these surfaces lie on the ground, such as floors and the spaces of buildings; and some others are equally distant from those spaces <note>That is, surfaces parallel to the ground.</note>. Some surfaces are upright, like walls, and other surfaces that have the same kinds of lines as walls. Those surfaces are said to be "equally distant" <note>original: "ugualmente lontane". Alberti is describing parallel planes.</note> from each other when the distance between them is equally the same everywhere. Surfaces that have the same kinds of lines are those that are touched on every side by a continuous straight line, such as the surfaces of square columns placed in a row in a loggia. 

These are the things that must be added to what was said above about surfaces. But to those things we said about the rays—the outermost ones, those inside, and the centric one—and to the things recounted above about the visual pyramid, one must add that theorem of the mathematicians which proves that if a straight line cuts the two sides of any triangle, and if this cutting line is such that it forms another triangle and is equally distant from the other line that is the base of the first triangle, then certainly that larger triangle will be proportional in its sides to this smaller one. 

This is what the mathematicians say. But we, so that our speech may be clearer to painters, will explain the matter more plainly. It is necessary for us to know what thing we wish to call *proportional* in this context. We say that proportional triangles are those whose sides and angles have the same correspondence among themselves. For if one of the sides of the triangle is longer than the base by

<summary>Alberti defines a painting as a "cut" or intersection of the visual pyramid and introduces the mathematical principle of proportional triangles to explain how three-dimensional space is scaled onto a two-dimensional surface.</summary>

...two and a half times, or another by three, all such triangles—whether they be larger or smaller than this one—provided they have the same correspondence of the sides to the base, so to speak, will be proportional to each other. Because the same relationship that a part has to its corresponding part in the larger triangle, it will also have in the smaller triangle. All triangles that are made in this way shall therefore be called proportional among us: and so that this may be understood more clearly, we will provide an analogy. A small man will be proportional to a very large one by means of the <term>cubit</term> <note>An ancient unit of measurement based on the length of the forearm, from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger.</note>, provided the same proportion of the palm and the foot is used to measure the other parts of the body. This was seen in <note>Evander, a hero of Roman mythology.</note> Evander, just as it was observed in <note>Hercules, the famous Greek hero.</note> Hercules, of whom <note>Aulus Gellius (c. 125 – 180 AD), a Roman author who wrote about Hercules' stature being determined by the size of his foot.</note> Gellius said was of a stature much larger than all other men. Nor was there any other proportion in the limbs of Hercules than there was in the body of the giant <note>Antaeus, a giant in Greek mythology defeated by Hercules.</note> Antaeus. For just as the hand corresponded in each of them in proportion to the cubit, and the cubit in proportion to the head and to the other limbs with equal measure among themselves, the same will occur in our triangles; there will be some kind of measure among the triangles, by means of which the smaller ones will correspond to the larger ones in all things, except for their size. 

And if these things are understood sufficiently, let us determine—by the judgment of mathematicians, in so far as it serves our purpose—that every section of any triangle, parallel to the base, generates and makes a triangle similar (as they say) to that larger triangle of theirs, and as we call it, proportional. And because in all those things that are proportional to one another, their parts are also corresponding, it follows that in those things where the parts are different and do not correspond, they are not proportional. The parts of the <term>visual triangle</term> <note>The 2D representation of a slice of the visual pyramid.</note> are, besides the lines, also the rays themselves; these rays, in looking at the quantities of the painting, will certainly be equal in number to the true ones in proportional quantities, and in those that are not proportional, they will not be equal. This is because one of these non-proportional quantities will occupy either more rays or fewer. You have learned, then, in what manner any smaller triangle is called proportional to the larger, and you remember that the <term>visual pyramid</term> is made of triangles. 

Therefore, let all our reasoning regarding triangles be applied to the pyramid, and let us be persuaded that none of the seen quantities of a surface, which are equally distant from the <term>cut</term> <note>original: "taglio". This refers to the picture plane—the flat surface of the painting that intersects the visual pyramid.</note>, undergo any alteration in the painting. For they are truly quantities equally distant and proportional in every equally distant cut from their corresponding parts. This being the case, it follows that no alteration of the outlines occurs in the painting, and that the quantities which fill the field or space, and by which the outlines are measured or understood, are not altered. And it is manifest that every cut of the visual pyramid that is equally distant from the seen surface is likewise proportional to that seen surface.

We have spoken of the surfaces proportional to the cut, that is, those equally distant from the painted surface. But because we will have to paint many diverse surfaces that will not be equally distant, we must...
