# BOOK ONE.

...[let us] make a more diligent investigation of these things, so that any reason behind the <term>section</term> <note>original: "taglio." This refers to the intersection of the visual pyramid, which Alberti defines as the surface of the painting.</note> may be explained. And because it would be a long, very difficult, and most obscure matter to narrate everything regarding these sections of <term>triangles</term> and the <term>pyramid</term> according to the rules of mathematicians, we shall instead proceed as painters, speaking according to our own custom. Let us recount very briefly some things about quantities that are not equally distant [from the eye]; once these are known, it will be easy to understand every consideration of surfaces that are not equally distant. Of those quantities that are not equally distant, some are lines entirely similar to <term>visual rays</term> <note>Lines imagined to travel from the eye to the object being viewed.</note>, and some are equally distant from certain <term>visual rays</term>. Those quantities entirely similar to visual rays—because they do not form a triangle and do not occupy a number of rays—do not gain any place for themselves in the <term>section</term>. But in quantities equally distant from <term>visual rays</term>, the more obtuse that larger angle at the base of the triangle is, the fewer rays that quantity will receive, and therefore it will have less space in the section. We have said that a surface is covered by quantities; and because it often happens in surfaces that there will be some quantity equally distant from the section, while other qualities of the same surface will not be equally distant, it follows that only those quantities which are equally distant on the surface suffer no alteration in the painting. But those quantities that are not equally distant will suffer more alteration the more obtuse their angle is (which is the largest in the triangle at the base). Finally, to all these things one must add that opinion of the philosophers, by which they affirm that if the heavens, the stars, the seas, the mountains, and animals themselves, and then all bodies, should become by the will of God half as small as they are now, it would happen that all these things would not appear to us to be diminished in any part from what they are now. For greatness, smallness, length, shortness, height, depth, narrowness, and breadth, darkness, brightness, and all other such things that can be found or not found in things, the philosophers called <term>accidents</term> <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "accidents" are qualities that can change without the object losing its essential nature (e.g., a man remains a man whether he is tall or short).</note>: and they are of such a sort that the entire knowledge of them is gained through <term>comparison</term>. <note>Alberti is introducing a revolutionary idea for the time: that vision is relative rather than absolute.</note> <note>Virgil (70–19 BC), the Roman poet.</note> Virgil said that <note>Aeneas, the Trojan hero.</note> Aeneas surpassed all other men by his shoulders. But if a comparison were made between him and <note>Polyphemus, the man-eating giant from the Odyssey.</note> Polyphemus, he would seem a Pygmy to us. They say that <note>Euryalus, a character in Virgil's Aeneid known for his beauty.</note> Euryalus was most beautiful, who, if compared to <note>Ganymede, a Trojan prince so beautiful he was abducted by Zeus/Jove to be the cupbearer of the gods.</note> Ganymede snatched by Jove, would appear ugly. In Spain, some girls are held to be fair-skinned, who in Germany would be held to be olive-toned and dark. Ivory and silver are white in color, and nonetheless, if one makes a comparison with swans or with white linen cloths, they will appear somewhat more pale. For this reason, surfaces in a painting appear most beautiful and most radiant to us when one sees in them that same proportion of white to black that exists in the objects themselves from lights to shadows. So, all these things are learned by making a comparison of them. Since in comparing things there is a certain power by which one knows what is more, or less, or equal. For this reason, we call "large" that thing which is greater than a smaller one, "largest" that which is greater than the large, "luminous" that which is brighter than the dark, lu-

<page-num>16</page-num>
->DELLA PITTURA<-
<meta>Catchword: Qui. Significant water damage/staining on the bottom left corner extending into the left margin.</meta>

...most luminous is that which is brighter than the luminous. And truly, the comparison of things is made with those things that are already most manifest to us. But since man is the most known of all things to man, perhaps Protagoras <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher famous for the dictum "Man is the measure of all things."</note> said that man was the model and the measure of all things; and he intended by this that the qualities of all things could be both well known and compared with the qualities of man. These things teach us this: that we understand that whatever sort of bodies we depict in painting, they will appear large or small to us according to the measure of the men who are painted there. And I believe I see that this power of comparison was understood most excellently, more than by any other of the ancients, by Timanthes <note>A celebrated Greek painter of the 4th century BCE.</note>. This painter, depicting on a small panel a sleeping Cyclops, painted Satyrs near him who were embracing the sleeper's thumb, so that by means of the measure of the Satyrs, the one who slept appeared infinitely larger.

We have until now said nearly all those things that pertain to the power of sight and to knowing the **section** <note>original: "taglio." In Alberti's geometry, the "section" is the flat surface of the painting that intersects the "pyramid" of light rays traveling from the object to the eye.</note>; but because it serves our purpose to know not only what it is, and of what things the section consists, but also how it is made, it remains for us to speak of this section and with what art it is expressed in painting. Of this, therefore (leaving other things aside), I will recount what I do while I am painting. The first thing, when painting a surface, I draw a rectangle of right angles as large as I please, which serves me as an **open window** through which the story <note>original: "historia." In the Renaissance, this referred to the narrative or subject matter of a painting, often historical, mythological, or biblical.</note> is to be seen; and there I determine the sizes of the men I wish to put in the painting, and I divide the height of this man into three parts, which are proportional to me with that measure which the common people call the <term>braccio</term> <note>original: "braccio." A traditional Italian unit of measurement (literally "an arm"), approximately 23 inches or 58 centimeters in Florence.</note>. For it is three *braccia* long, as is clearly seen from the proportion of the members of a man, because such is the common length for the most part of the human body. With this measure, then, I divide the bottom line that lies at the base of the drawn rectangle, and I see how many of these parts fit into it; and this same base line of the rectangle is proportional to me to the nearest transverse quantity equally distant in the space [of the floor]. After this, I place a single point where the sight should run within the rectangle, which point occupies that place where the <term>centric ray</term> <note>The visual ray that travels in a straight line from the center of the eye to the object, perpendicular to the face.</note> must arrive, and therefore I call it the <term>point of the center</term> <note>Now commonly known as the vanishing point.</note>. This point shall be placed appropriately, no higher from the base line than the height of the man who is to be painted there; for in this way, both those who look at it and the things painted appear to be on the same level plane. Having placed the point of the center, I draw straight lines from this point to each of the divisions of the base line: which lines truly show me the manner in which, having to proceed toward the infinite and final distance, the transverse quantities are narrowed to my sight and view.

<summary>Alberti explains the relativity of scale using the example of a sleeping Cyclops and then provides his famous practical "recipe" for linear perspective, introducing the painting as an "open window."</summary>

There are some who would draw a line within the quadrangle <term>quadrangle: a four-sided shape, here representing the frame of the painting</term> equally distant from the line already divided, and would divide the space between these two lines into three parts. Then, by this rule, they would draw another line equally distant from this second line, in such a way that the space between the first divided line and this second parallel line (or line equally distant) exceeds by one part of itself that space which is between the second and third lines; and then they would add the other lines, so that the space that followed next between the lines would always be half as much again <note>This describes a mathematical progression where each receding tile or space is reduced by a set ratio, such as 3:2, to simulate depth.</note>, to speak like the mathematicians. This is how those people would proceed, who, even though they claim to follow an excellent way of painting, I nevertheless think err significantly. Because, having placed the first parallel line at random in relation to the primary one, even if the other parallels are placed with rule and order, they still have nothing by which they might have a certain and determined location for the tip of the pyramid <note>The "tip of the pyramid" refers to the observer's eye or the viewpoint from which the scene is constructed.</note> to be able to see the object properly, from which great errors easily occur in the painting. Add to this that their rule would be very false if the centric point <term>centric point: what we now call the vanishing point, where all parallel lines seem to meet</term> were placed either higher or lower than the height of the painted man; for all those who are skilled will say that none of the painted things can conform to the real ones if they are not placed with a specific, regulated distance from the eye, for otherwise they cannot be seen or discerned properly. Concerning which thing...

He argues that without a determined 'centric point' and a fixed distance for the observer, the painted scene will appear distorted and unrealistic.</summary>

<warning>Water staining visible on the bottom left margin.</warning>

We shall explain the reasoning if we ever write of these demonstrations of painting; when these were first produced by us, our friends looked upon them with wonder and called them the "miracles of painting." For all these things I have said belong primarily to that part of the work. Let us return, then, to our purpose. Since these things are so, I have therefore found this excellent method. In all other matters, I follow the same line, the centric point <term>centric point: the vanishing point where all parallel lines meeting the eye converge</term>, the division of the base line, and the drawing of lines from the point to each division of the base line. But in the transverse quantities <note>depth measurements moving back into the scene</note>, I maintain this order: I have a small space in which I draw a straight line, and I divide this into the same number of parts as the base line of the quadrangle. Then I place a point high above this line, exactly as high as the centric point is above the base line of the quadrangle, and I draw lines from this point to each division of that line. Then I determine how much distance I want between the eye of the observer and the painting, and there I establish the location of the "cut" <note>the vertical plane of the painting</note> with a perpendicular plumb line; I then mark the intersections of all the lines it finds. A plumb line is one that, falling upon another straight line, creates right angles on every side.

->Centric point at three cubits.<-

---

->Base line of nine cubits.<-

*A. viewpoint three cubits high; B. C. D. E. F. G. H. I. K. parallel lines.*

This plumb line will therefore give me, through its intersections, all the boundaries of the distances that must exist between the transverse parallel lines of the floor <term>floor: the "pauimento," or tiled ground plane of the perspective</term>. In this way, I will have drawn all the parallels on the floor. A proof of how accurately they are drawn will be if a single continued straight line serves as a diagonal <note>original: "diametro"</note> for the joined quadrangles in the painted floor. Among mathematicians, the diagonal of a quadrangle is that straight line which starts from one of the angles...

### BOOK ONE.

...goes to the other point opposite it, which divides the quadrangle <term>quadrangle: a four-sided figure, here referring to the frame of the painting or the floor grid</term> into two parts, in such a way that it makes two triangles from said quadrangle. Having then diligently finished these things, I draw again from above another transverse line <note>a horizontal line</note>, equally distant from the others below, which intersects the two upright sides of the large quadrangle and passes through the central point. And this line serves me as a limit and boundary, beyond which no quantity exceeds the height of the eye of the viewer. And because it passes through the central point, let it be called the **centric line** <note>today known as the horizon line</note>. From this it follows that those men who are painted between the two further parallel lines will be much smaller than those between the front parallel lines. It is not because they are truly smaller than the others; but because they are further away, they *appear* smaller, a thing which nature clearly demonstrates to us is true. For we see in churches that the heads of men walking appear to stay at almost the same equal height, but the feet of those who are quite far away seem to us to correspond to the knees of those who are in front of us.

This entire rule of dividing the pavement <note>the "floor" or ground plane of the painting</note> pertains principally to that part of painting which we shall call, in its proper place, **composition** <term>composition: the art of arranging the various elements—figures, buildings, and objects—into a unified narrative scene</term>. And it is such a rule that I fear, because it is a new thing and because of the brevity of these my commentaries, it may be little understood by the reader; for, as we easily recognize through ancient works, it was not known by our ancestors because it was obscure and difficult. Indeed, among the works of the ancients, you will have to labor greatly to find any **history** <term>history: or "istoria," the depiction of a significant narrative or scene, considered the highest form of art</term> that is well composed, well painted, well formed, or well sculpted. For this reason, I have said these things with brevity, and, as I think, not yet with obscurity. But I know how they are and what they are, and that I cannot gain any praise for eloquence through them; those who do not understand them at first sight will have to labor with great difficulty to ever comprehend them. These things are very easy and very beautiful to subtle minds inclined toward painting, in whatever way they are told. But to coarse men, who are little suited or inclined by nature to these most noble arts, even if one spoke of them most eloquently, they would be little pleasing; and perhaps these very things recited by us very briefly without any eloquence will be read not without annoyance.

But I would wish to be pardoned if, while I have principally wanted to be understood, I have attended to making my writing clear rather than composed or ornate; and those things that follow will bring, as I hope, less boredom to those who read. We have therefore treated the triangles, the pyramid, the "cut" <note>the intersection of the visual pyramid that forms the painting's surface</note>, and those things that seemed necessary to say. Regarding these things, I was nonetheless accustomed to discuss them with my friends much more at length with a certain rule of geometry, and to show them the reasons why it happened thus. This I have thought to leave aside for brevity in these my commentaries, because in this place I have recounted only the first principles of painting; and I have wanted to call them the "first principles" because they are the first foundations of the art for painters who do not know them. But they are such that those who understand them well will know...

<page-num>20</page-num>
<header>ON PAINTING</header>
<warning>Visible water staining on the left margin and bottom left corner.</warning>

...will be of no small use to them, as much regarding their intellect as in understanding the definition of painting, and also regarding those things which we have yet to say. And let no one doubt that a man will never become a good painter if he does not excellently understand what he seeks to do in his painting. For one draws the bow in vain if one has not first designated the place where the arrow is to be aimed. I would certainly wish us to be persuaded that only he is destined to become an excellent painter who has learned to perfectly place all the **outlines** <term>outlines: Alberti uses "d'intorni," referring to the "circumscription" or the boundary lines of a shape</term> and all the qualities of the surfaces. On the contrary, I affirm that he will never succeed as a good painter who does not know exactly and most diligently the things we have discussed. Therefore, all that has been said regarding surfaces and the **cut** <term>cut: the "intercision" or the flat plane of the painting that intersects the visual pyramid</term> was necessary. It remains now to instruct the painter in the method he must hold to imitate with his hand those things which he has first imagined in his mind.

# LEON BATTISTA ALBERTI
## ON PAINTING.
### BOOK TWO.

**B**UT because this study of learning might perhaps seem too fatiguing to the young, it seems right to me to show in this place how painting is not unworthy of our every effort and all our diligence. For painting possesses a certain divine power, such that it not only does what they say friendship does—representing to us those who are far away—but it also places before our eyes those who have been dead for many, many years. This occurs with the greatest wonder for the painter and delight for the viewer. Plutarch <note>original: "Plutarco"; a Greek historian and biographer (c. 46–119 AD)</note> relates that Cassander, one of Alexander’s captains, began to tremble with his whole body upon seeing the effigy of the deceased Alexander <note>original: "Alessandro Magno"; Alexander the Great</note>, recognizing in it that royal majesty. They also say that Agesilaus the Lacedaemonian <note>Agesilaus II, King of Sparta (r. 399–360 BC)</note>, knowing himself to be very ugly, did not want his effigy to be seen by descendants, and therefore he never wished to be painted or sculpted by anyone. Thus, the faces of the dead live a long life, in a certain sense, through painting. 

And that painting has expressed for us the gods who are revered by the nations must be considered a very great gift granted to mortals; for painting has served piety most greatly, through which we are principally joined to the gods and our minds are kept in a state of complete religious devotion. They say that Phidias <note>original: "Fidia"; the most famous Greek sculptor of the Classical period</note> made a Jupiter in Elis <note>The Statue of Zeus at Olympia, one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World</note>, the beauty of which added much to the religious devotion already conceived. But as to how much painting serves the most honorable pleasures of the mind, and how much ornament it brings to things, one can see from many sources and especially from this: that you will find almost no object, however precious, which does not become much more dear and much more prized through the accompaniment of painting.

<summary>Alberti concludes his technical rules for outlines and surfaces before beginning Book Two, where he argues for the "divine power" of painting to preserve human memory and inspire religious devotion.</summary>
<keywords>Leon Battista Alberti, outlines, surfaces, Book Two, divinity of painting, portraiture, Plutarch, Alexander the Great, Phidias, piety, aesthetics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Book Two, which moves from the technical geometry of Book One into a defense of the nobility and history of painting. Alberti is establishing painting as the most divine and foundational of all arts.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>BOOK SECOND.</header>

Ivory, gems, and other such precious things become more valuable through the hand of the painter. Even gold itself, when adorned by painting, is esteemed much more than gold alone. Indeed, even lead—the lowliest of all metals—if Phidias or Praxiteles <note>Phidias and Praxiteles were the most famous sculptors of ancient Greece, known for achieving ideal beauty.</note> had fashioned a statue of it with their own hands, it would perhaps be held in higher regard than an equal amount of raw and unworked silver. The painter Zeuxis began to give away his works because, as he said, they could not be paid for at any price; for he judged that no price could be found that could satisfy one who, in painting or sculpting living beings, was like another god among mortals. Painting therefore possesses these praises: that those who are masters of it not only marvel at their own works, but realize they are most similar to the gods. What shall I say? Is painting not either the mistress of all the arts, or at least their principal ornament?

For the architect, if I am not mistaken, has taken from the painter alone the moldings, the capitals, the bases, the columns, the cornices, and all the other such praised elements of buildings. Indeed, the painter, by means of his rules and art, has taught and provided the method to stone-cutters, to sculptors, and to all the workshops of smiths, of carpenters, and of all those who work in manual construction; so that, in the end, no art will be found, however humble, that does not have regard for painting. Thus I will dare to say that all the ornament in things is drawn from painting.

But painting was honored by the ancients primarily with this honor: that while almost all other artisans were called **smiths** <term>smiths: "Fabri" in the original Latin, a term that denoted craftsmen or manual workers, often implying a lower social status than artists</term> among the Latins, the painter alone was not numbered among the smiths. This being so, I am accustomed to saying among my friends that the inventor of painting was—according to the opinion of the poets—that Narcissus who was turned into a flower. For since painting is the flower of all the arts, the whole fable of Narcissus seems perfectly suited to the subject. For what else is painting but to embrace and capture with art that surface of the fountain? <note>Alberti compares the plane of a painting to the surface of the water where Narcissus saw his reflection; painting captures a fleeting image and makes it permanent.</note>

Quintilian thought that the ancient painters were accustomed to drawing shadows according to how the sun cast them, and that the art then grew from hand to hand with further additions. There are some who relate that a certain Philocles the Egyptian and a Cleanthes (I know not which one) were the first inventors of this art. The Egyptians affirm that painting had been in use among them six thousand years before it was transported to Greece, and our writers say that it came from Greece to Italy after the victories of Marcellus in Sicily. <note>Marcus Claudius Marcellus captured Syracuse in 212 BCE, bringing Greek art back to Rome as spoils of war.</note> But it does not matter much to know the first painters or the inventors of painting, since we do not wish to recount the history of painting like Pliny, but to treat newly of the art. Of this art, up to this age, there is no memory left to us (that I have seen) by the ancient writers—even though they say that Euphranor of the Isthmus wrote something about measures and colors, and that Antigonus and Zenocrate wrote some things about painting, and that Apelles also compiled some things about painting and sent them to Perseus. Diogenes Laertius relates that the philosopher Demetrius also wrote some commentaries on painting. Beyond this, I also estimate that since our people...
